Anisotropic local law for non-separable sample covariance matrices
Zhou Fan ∗, Renyuan Ma ∗, Elliot Paquette †, Zhichao Wang ‡

Abstract
. We establish local laws for sample covariance matrices K = N − 1 ​ ∑ i = 1 N 𝐠 i ​ 𝐠 i ∗ K=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*} where the random vectors 𝐠 1 , … , 𝐠 N ∈ ℝ n \mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} are independent with common covariance Σ \Sigma . Previous work has largely focused on the separable model 𝐠 = Σ 1 / 2 ​ 𝐰 \mathbf{g}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{w} with 𝐰 \mathbf{w} having independent entries, but this structure is rarely present in statistical applications involving dependent or nonlinearly transformed data. Under a concentration assumption for quadratic forms 𝐠 ∗ ​ A ​ 𝐠 \mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g} , we prove an optimal averaged local law showing that the Stieltjes transform of K K converges to its deterministic limit uniformly down to the optimal scale η ≥ N − 1 + ε \eta\geq N^{-1+\varepsilon} . Under an additional structural assumption on the cumulant tensors of 𝐠 \mathbf{g} — which interpolates between the highly structured case of independent entries and generic dependence — we establish the full anisotropic local law, providing entrywise control of the resolvent ( K − z ​ I ) − 1 (K-zI)^{-1} in arbitrary directions. We discuss several classes of non-separable examples satisfying our assumptions, including conditionally mean-zero distributions, the random features model 𝐠 = σ ⁡ ( X ​ 𝐰 ) \mathbf{g}=\sigma(X\mathbf{w}) arising in machine learning, and Gaussian measures with nonlinear tilting. The proofs introduce a tensor network framework for analyzing fluctuation averaging in the presence of higher-order cumulant structure.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Sample covariance matrices and the deformed Marchenko-Pastur law

Random matrices arise naturally across mathematics, statistics, and physics. In statistics, Wishart
[
89
]
introduced random covariance matrices in 1928 for multivariate analysis, while in physics, Wigner
[
88
]
employed random matrices in 1955 to model energy levels in heavy nuclei.

A fundamental random matrix in statistics involves the observation of
N
independent random vectors
\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
equal in distribution to a vector
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with mean zero and covariance matrix
\Sigma
. Understanding the spectral properties of the associated sample covariance matrix

K=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}=\frac{1}{N}GG^{*},\qquad\text{where }G=[\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{N}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times N},

is central to covariance estimation, principal component analysis, and numerous other statistical procedures
[
14
]
. The associated
Gram matrix

\widetilde{K}=\frac{1}{N}G^{*}G\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}

is central to kernel methods and has the same nonzero eigenvalues as
K
. Many problems in optimization theory involving random data, as well as related questions in machine learning, can also be reduced to understanding the singular value and singular vector structure of the data matrix
G

[
21
,
20
,
62
,
66
]
. Beyond classical statistics, the spectral theory of such matrices has found applications in wireless communications, where the channel capacity of MIMO systems is determined by the log-determinant
\frac{1}{n}\log\det(I+\text{SNR}\cdot K)

[
81
]
, as well as in kernel methods
[
34
]
, random features regression
[
70
]
, and the analysis of neural networks
[
68
,
53
]
; see
[
29
]
for a comprehensive introduction to random matrix theory in machine learning.

In the high-dimensional regime where the dimension
n
and sample size
N
grow proportionally,
n/N\to\gamma\in(0,\infty)
, the classical theory initiated by Marchenko and Pastur
[
61
]
describes the limiting spectral distribution of
K
. When
\Sigma=I_{n}
and
\mathbf{g}
has i.i.d. entries with mean zero and variance one, the empirical spectral distribution of
K
converges weakly to the
Marchenko-Pastur distribution
with density

\rho_{\mathrm{MP}}(x)=(1-1/\gamma)_{+}\delta_{0}(x)+\frac{1}{2\pi\gamma x}\sqrt{(x-\lambda_{-})(\lambda_{+}-x)}\,\mathbf{1}_{[\lambda_{-},\lambda_{+}]}(x),

where
\lambda_{\pm}=(1\pm\sqrt{\gamma})^{2}
are the spectral edges.

For general covariance
\Sigma
, a deterministic approximation for the spectral distribution is the
deformed Marchenko-Pastur law

\mu_{0}
, characterized through its Stieltjes transform
m_{0}(z)=\int(x-z)^{-1}\,d\mu_{0}(x)
which satisfies the self-consistent equation

(1.1)

m_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sigma_{\alpha}(1-\gamma-\gamma zm_{0}(z))-z},

where
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}
are the eigenvalues of
\Sigma
. This law can also be understood as the multiplicative free convolution of the standard Marchenko-Pastur law with the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\Sigma

[
83
]
.

A sufficient condition for the convergence of the empirical spectral distribution of
K
to the deformed Marchenko-Pastur law was established by Bai and Zhou
[
16
]
: the weak convergence in probability of quadratic forms

(1.2)

\frac{1}{n}(\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}-\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma A)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0,

for all matrices
A
with bounded operator norm. This condition is anticipated by the classical literature, as it is essentially all that is needed for the arguments of
[
76
]
; see also
[
91
]
for a necessary and sufficient formulation in the isotropic case.
This condition is also readily verified in simple cases, most notably in the
separable
(or
linear
) model where
\mathbf{g}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{w}
for a vector
\mathbf{w}
with i.i.d. standard entries, the setting of the foundational work of Silverstein and Bai
[
74
]
.

1.2.
Local laws: from global to optimal scale

While the convergence of the empirical spectral distribution provides a global description of the spectrum, many applications in random matrix theory — including universality of local eigenvalue statistics, eigenvalue rigidity, and eigenvector delocalization — require understanding the spectrum at
local
scales, down to the scale of individual eigenvalue spacings.

An
averaged local law
asserts that the Stieltjes transform
m(z)=n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}(K-zI)^{-1}
remains close to its deterministic approximation
m_{0}(z)
uniformly over spectral parameters
z=E+i\eta
with
\eta
as small as
N^{-1+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
. Specifically, in the setting where
\mathbf{g}
has identity covariance and satisfies concentration of quadratic forms, the celebrated work of Pillai and Yin
[
69
]
established

(1.3)

|m(z)-m_{0}(z)|\prec(N\eta)^{-1}

uniformly over
z
in suitable regular spectral domains extending down to the optimal scale
\eta\geq N^{-1+\varepsilon}
. (The
\prec
notation means that the inequality holds except on an event of probability at most a power of
N
and up to errors that grow slower than any power of
N
; see Section
3
for details.)

For many applications, including eigenvector delocalization, the distribution of eigenvectors, and the analysis of finite-rank deformations, the averaged local law is insufficient. This leads to the
isotropic local law

[
25
]
and more generally the
anisotropic local law
. For the
separable model

\mathbf{g}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{w}
, Knowles and Yin
[
54
]
, building on
[
25
]
in the case
\Sigma=I_{n}
, established that

(1.4)

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R(z)\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{*}\Pi(z)\mathbf{v}|\prec\Psi(z),\qquad\text{for all unit vectors }\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}

where
\Pi(z)=(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)\Sigma)^{-1}
,
\Psi(z)=\sqrt{\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)/(N\eta)}+(N\eta)^{-1}
is the optimal error parameter, and
\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of the limiting spectral distribution of the Gram matrix
\widetilde{K}=N^{-1}G^{*}G
.

The anisotropic local law has profound implications: it yields eigenvector delocalization in any fixed basis (not just the standard basis) and eigenvalue rigidity at the optimal scale. Combined with a comparison argument, it leads to Tracy-Widom fluctuations for edge eigenvalues and bulk universality of local eigenvalue statistics
[
56
,
57
]
. These results have made the anisotropic local law a cornerstone of modern random matrix theory.

1.3.
Beyond the separable model: the main question

The separable model
\mathbf{g}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{w}
with independent entries in
\mathbf{w}
is restrictive in practice. Many statistical and machine learning applications naturally involve random vectors with more complex dependence structures. This brings us to the central question of the present work:

Do optimal local laws hold for distributions of
\mathbf{g}
beyond the separable setting?

There are many possible answers to this question, depending on the class of distributions under consideration. One important class is the
random features model

(1.5)

\mathbf{g}=\sigma(X\mathbf{w}),

where
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is a deterministic feature matrix,
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a random vector with independent entries, and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a (possibly entrywise) nonlinear activation function. This model arises naturally in the study of random features regression, which is a statistical approximation of kernel methods
[
51
,
58
,
62
,
49
]
and linear approximants for neural networks
[
68
,
45
,
23
]
. We discuss applications and related work on random features models further in Section
1.5
and further details in Section
2.5
.

The weak convergence condition (
1.2
) is not sufficient for the local law to hold at optimal scales, as a quantitative strengthening in terms of the rate of concentration in (
1.2
)
can be seen to be necessary for an averaged local law to hold (see Section
2.5
). A first contribution of our work is to show that such a strengthening is also sufficient for the averaged local law to hold. In brief, we work in a standard high-dimensional regime where
n/N
remains bounded and
\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C
, and we assume the strong concentration

\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}-\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma A\prec\|A\|_{F}

for all deterministic matrices
A
(see Assumptions
1
and
2
in Section
2.1
for the precise formulation). This concentration is known to hold in the separable case under moment bounds on
\mathbf{w}
, and also for vectors satisfying a log-Sobolev inequality
[
5
]
or log-concave distributions
[
19
]
. We show that the averaged local law holds under this assumption alone (Theorem
2.5
).

To establish the full anisotropic local law, which provides control of the resolvent in arbitrary directions, our analyses require additional structure on the higher-order cumulants of
\mathbf{g}
. We introduce Assumption
3
in Section
2.1
, a condition on the cumulant tensors of
\mathbf{g}
that interpolates between the highly structured case of independent entries (where cumulant tensors are diagonal) and generic tensors (where no such structure exists). This assumption is significantly more general than requiring a separable model, yet remains verifiable for important examples including the random features model (
1.5
).

1.4.
Main contributions

The main contributions of this paper are:

(i)

Averaged local law under concentration of quadratic forms
(Theorem
2.5
): Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, we establish the optimal averaged local law (
1.3
) together with an entrywise local law for the Gram matrix resolvent
\widetilde{R}(z)
. This extends the results of
[
69
]
to the case of non-identity covariance.

(ii)

Anisotropic local law under cumulant structure
(Theorem
2.8
): Under the additional Assumption
3
on cumulant tensors, we establish the optimal anisotropic local law (
1.4
) for the linearized resolvent (
2.15
). This
extends results of
[
54
]
beyond the separable model, providing the first anisotropic local law down to optimal spectral scales
\eta\sim N^{-1+\varepsilon}
for a general class of non-separable sample covariance matrices.

(iii)

Verification for non-separable examples
(Section
2.5
): We verify Assumptions
2
and
3
for several classes of non-separable distributions, including conditionally mean-zero distributions (Proposition
2.13
), the random features model
\mathbf{g}=\sigma(X\mathbf{w})
(Proposition
2.17
), and Gaussian measures with random features tilting (Proposition
2.18
).

(iv)

Tensor network methodology
: Our proofs employ a novel tensor network framework for analyzing fluctuation averaging in the presence of higher-order cumulant structure. This framework, which systematically reduces complex moment expansions to graph-theoretic bounds, may be of independent interest for other problems in random matrix theory. We give a brief introduction in Section
2.7
.

As corollaries, we obtain eigenvalue rigidity at the optimal scale and delocalization of eigenvectors of both
K
and
\widetilde{K}
in any fixed basis (see Section
2.4
for more details).

1.5.
Related work

Random features and Gaussian equivalence.

The random features model (
1.5
) was introduced by Rahimi and Recht
[
70
]
as a computationally efficient approximation to kernel methods, and is equivalent to a two-layer neural network with frozen first-layer weights and trainable readout. A remarkable phenomenon, observed empirically and established rigorously in several settings
[
49
,
62
,
64
,
47
,
46
,
51
]
, is that the random features model is asymptotically equivalent to a surrogate linear Gaussian model: in the proportional limit, the training and generalization errors of the nonlinear model match those of a Gaussian equivalent. The anisotropic local law established in this paper (Theorem
2.8
) can be viewed as a
strong form of Gaussian equivalence
at the level of the resolvent: it asserts that
(K-zI)^{-1}
is well-approximated at optimal scales by a deterministic matrix
\Pi(z)
depending only on the first two moments of
\mathbf{g}
. This extends naturally to spectral filters
\varphi(K)
for functions
\varphi
smooth on any scale larger than the mean eigenvalue spacing. The model also extends to deep architectures with frozen intermediate layers
[
73
]
; our cumulant assumption is designed to accommodate such compositional structure, though verifying it for deep models with many layers remains an open problem.

Covariance estimation and concentration.

The estimation of covariance matrices in high dimensions has a vast literature; see
[
14
,
82
]
for comprehensive treatments. A central question is: what conditions on the distribution of
\mathbf{g}
ensure that the sample covariance
K=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}
concentrates around
\Sigma
in operator norm?

For log-concave distributions, optimal
\|K-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O(\sqrt{n/N})
rates were established by Adamczak, Litvak, Pajor, and Tomczak-Jaegermann
[
4
]
. The work of Srivastava and Vershynin
[
77
]
showed that if all
k
-dimensional marginals of the whitened vector
\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathbf{g}
have
(2+\varepsilon)
-moment tails decaying like
t^{-(1+\eta)}
for
t>Ck
, then
N=O(n)
samples suffice for operator norm concentration. This was refined by Tikhomirov
[
80
]
, who obtained the optimal Bai-Yin rate under
L_{p}
-
L_{2}
norm equivalence for
p>4
, which gives moment growth estimates of
linear forms
, i.e. uniform control of moments of
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{g}\rangle
in place of our
quadratic forms

\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}
.
Most recently, Abdalla and Zhivotovskiy
[
1
]
proved a non-asymptotic, dimension-free Bai-Yin theorem: if
\|\mathbf{g}\|_{2}\leq N^{1/2+\varepsilon}
and one-dimensional marginals satisfy
L_{p}
-
L_{2}
norm equivalence for some
p>4
, then the sample covariance achieves the optimal rate depending on the effective rank rather than the ambient dimension.

Hence, linear form estimates tend to be sufficient for the
covariance estimation problem
. Importantly, boundedness of linear forms does not imply concentration of quadratic forms, even in the weak sense of (
1.2
). A simple counterexample is the
mixture model
: let
\mathbf{g}=\xi\cdot\mathbf{z}
where
\mathbf{z}\sim N(0,I_{n})
is Gaussian and
\xi
is an independent bounded scalar variable with unit second moment. Then
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}=0
and
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}\mathbf{g}^{*}=I_{n}
, and linear forms
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{g}\rangle
are sub-Gaussian. However, the variance of
\xi^{2}
may induce fluctuations in the quadratic form
\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}=\xi^{2}\mathbf{z}^{*}A\mathbf{z}
that are large enough to cause the standard Marchenko-Pastur law to
not
be a deterministic equivalent for the spectral distribution (see Example
2.16
for further discussion).

Local laws for Wigner matrices and generalizations.

The development of local laws began with Wigner matrices (symmetric matrices with i.i.d. entries), where the semicircle law holds globally. Pioneering work of Erdős, Schlein, and Yau
[
39
,
40
]
established local semicircle laws down to scales
\eta\sim N^{-1+\varepsilon}
. This was subsequently extended to generalized Wigner matrices and models of mild sparsity
[
41
,
36
,
37
,
35
]
. A powerful generalization to less homogeneous matrices is the
matrix Dyson equation
(MDE) formalism developed by Ajanki, Erdős, and Krüger
[
7
,
8
]
. For a random matrix
H
with mean
A=\mathbb{E}H
and covariance operator
\mathcal{S}[R]=\mathbb{E}(H-A)R(H-A)
, the resolvent
G=(H-zI)^{-1}
is approximated by a deterministic matrix
M
satisfying

I+(zI-A+\mathcal{S}[M])M=0.

This framework accommodates matrices with correlated entries (provided correlations decay sufficiently fast), non-identical variances, and non-zero means.

The characteristic flow method.

A complementary approach to local laws proceeds dynamically: one interpolates the given matrix toward a Gaussian ensemble by running a Dyson Brownian motion, and studies how the resolvent evolves along this flow. The key observation, going back to Pastur
[
67
]
, is that the Stieltjes transform of the empirical spectral measure satisfies a transport equation whose characteristics are curves in the upper half-plane contracting away from the spectral support as time increases; this allows one to propagate estimates that hold at larger scales to the optimal shorter scales
\eta\sim N^{-1+\varepsilon}
. This method was pioneered for local laws by
[
52
,
6
]
in the context of
\beta
-ensembles.
[
27
,
24
]
used related stochastic advection equations to study extreme gap statistics and eigenvector statistics respectively. The method has been also extended beyond the self-adjoint setting:
[
26
]
employed it in the context of non-Hermitian dynamics, and
[
28
]
introduced the
zig-zag method
, which applies the characteristic flow iteratively to prove multi-resolvent local laws by alternating short flow steps with Greens function comparison estimates; this method has also been applied to generalized Wigner models
[
38
]
, which are closely related to the linearized resolvents of sample covariance matrices. Although the present paper does not use the characteristic flow method, identifying the correct assumptions on non-separable random vectors that would allow one to run this dynamical argument remains interesting for future work, and we expect it would be similar in spirit to what we need here. See
[
84
]
for a short and elegant dynamical proof of a local semicircle law via the characteristic flow method.

Local laws for sample covariance and Gram matrices.

For sample covariance matrices with identity covariance, the averaged local law program was initiated by Pillai and Yin
[
69
]
(see also
[
18
]
). The work of Knowles and Yin
[
54
]
established the anisotropic local law for separable covariance matrices (c.f. Proposition
2.12
) via a strategy closely related to the method here, though they use a sophisticated interpolation argument to show the full anisotropic local law. Alt, Erdős, and Krüger extended these results to general Gram matrices and correlated sample covariance models using the Dyson equation approach
[
10
,
11
,
9
]
. An important statistical application of these local laws is to edge universality: Lee and Schnelli
[
57
]
used local laws as input to establish Tracy-Widom fluctuations for the largest eigenvalue of sample covariance matrices with general population covariance, via a Green function comparison argument. This was extended to Tracy-Widom fluctuations at each regular edge of the spectrum by Fan and Johnstone
[
43
]
.

Polynomial scaling regimes and spike eigenvalues.

The proportional regime
n\asymp N
considered in this paper is not the only scaling of interest. In the
polynomial regime

N\asymp d^{\ell}
for integer
\ell\geq 1
, limiting spectral distributions of random inner-product kernel matrices have been analyzed by
[
59
,
90
]
; universality for general distributions was established in
[
33
,
65
]
. Polynomial scalings of random features regression have also been studied
[
50
,
30
]
, revealing subtleties such as the failure of Gaussian equivalence in the quadratic scaling regime
[
87
]
.
Beyond the bulk spectrum, “spike” eigenvalues separated from the bulk are often the primary features of interest. The celebrated Baik-Ben Arous-Péché (BBP) phase transition
[
17
]
describes when spike eigenvalues emerge from the bulk in spiked covariance models. For the separable model
\mathbf{g}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{x}
, these phenomena are well-understood
[
15
,
22
]
. The work of Wang, Wu, and Fan
[
86
]
extends this to nonlinear models, characterizing how spiked structure in input data propagates through neural network layers and establishing a Gaussian equivalence for spike eigenvalue behavior for non-separable sample covariance matrix.

1.6.
Notation

We use
C,c
for positive constants that may change from line to line but depend only on fixed model parameters. The notation
a\asymp b
means
c\leq a/b\leq C
for constants
c,C>0
. We write
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{F}
for the operator (spectral) norm and Frobenius norm of matrices, and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
for the Euclidean norm of vectors. For a general tensor
T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}
, we write
\|T\|_{\infty}
for the entrywise
l_{\infty}
(maximum) norm, and
\|T\|_{F}
for its Frobenius tensor norm, i.e.
\|\cdot\|_{2}
-norm of its vectorization. We write
X\prec Y
to denote stochastic domination: for any
\varepsilon,D>0
, there exists
C\equiv C(\varepsilon,D)>0
such that
\mathbb{P}[|X|>N^{\varepsilon}Y]\leq CN^{-D}
for all
N\geq 1
. Properties of this notation are further reviewed in Section
3
.

2.
Main results

2.1.
Assumptions

We work in the following standard high-dimensional setting.

Assumption 1

(Basic assumptions)
.

There exist constants
C,c>0
such that
c\leq n/N\leq C
,

\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C
, and
\Sigma
is positive semidefinite with at most

(1-c)n
of its eigenvalues belonging to
[0,c]
.

The central assumption we make on quadratic forms is the following.

Assumption 2

(Concentration of quadratic forms)
.

\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{N}
are independent random vectors satisfying
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}_{i}=0
,

\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}=\Sigma
, and

\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{g}_{i}\|_{2}^{k}\leq n^{C_{k}}\text{ for each }k\geq 1\text{ and constants }C_{k}>0.

Furthermore, for any
\varepsilon,D>0
, there exists a constant
C\equiv C(\varepsilon,D)>0

such that for each
i=1,\ldots,N
and any
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,

\mathbb{P}[|\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}A\mathbf{g}_{i}-\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma A|\geq n^{\varepsilon}\|A\|_{F}]\leq Cn^{-D}.

Assumption
2
can equivalently be formulated in terms of
L^{p}
bounds on the re-centered quadratic form
\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}A\mathbf{g}_{i}-\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma A
, by Markov’s inequality. It is known to hold in the separable case
\mathbf{g}_{i}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{w}_{i}
under moment bounds on the entries of
\mathbf{w}_{i}
, and also for vectors satisfying a convex concentration inequality with at most logarithmic dependence on the dimension
[
5
]
, including isotropic log-concave random vectors
[
19
]
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, we will
establish an optimal local law for the Stieltjes transforms of
K
and

\widetilde{K}
and an entrywise local law for the resolvent of

\widetilde{K}
(c.f. Theorem
2.5
)
over spectral domains of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
satisfying suitable regularity conditions.

We will then establish an optimal anisotropic local law for the
resolvents of
K
and
\widetilde{K}
(c.f. Theorem

2.8
) under an additional assumption for the higher-order
cumulant tensors of
\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{N}
. To formulate this assumption, we
introduce the following definition.

Definition 2.1

(
\mathcal{U}
-norm)
.

For any subset
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
containing all standard basis vectors

\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}
, and for any
k\geq 1
, we define a norm on tensors

T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}
by

\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k}\in\mathcal{U}}|\langle\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{x}_{k},T\rangle|.

Note that for
\mathcal{U}
containing the standard basis vectors
\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}

and having polynomial cardinality
|\mathcal{U}|\leq n^{C}
, the norm
\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}
may
be understood as a certain strengthening of the entrywise
\ell_{\infty}
norm. Our additional assumption for the cumulant tensors of
\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{N}
is
the following.

Assumption 3
.

Let
\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}_{i})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}
denote the
k
-th order cumulant
tensor of
\mathbf{g}_{i}
, whose entries are given by

\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}_{i}),\mathbf{e}_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\alpha_{k}}\rangle=\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{e}_{\alpha_{1}}^{*}\mathbf{g}_{i},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{\alpha_{k}}^{*}\mathbf{g}_{i})

where
\kappa_{k}(\cdot)
on the right side is the
k^{\text{th}}
mixed cumulant
of entries of
\mathbf{g}_{i}
.

Then for each
k\geq 3
, there exists a constant
C_{k}>0
and a subset of
deterministic vectors
\mathcal{U}_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying

\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{k},\quad|\mathcal{U}_{k}|\leq n^{C_{k}},\quad\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq C_{k}\;\text{ for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{U}_{k},

such that the following holds: For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant

C\equiv C(\varepsilon,k)>0
such that for each
i=1,\ldots,N

and any
m\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
,

\mathbf{s}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{s}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
,

(2.1)

|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}_{i}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|\leq Cn^{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n})^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}_{k}}\prod_{t=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{t}\|_{2}.

Remark 2.2
.

To provide some intuition for Assumption
3
, we note that a simple cumulant growth condition that would imply the needed concentration of quadratic forms in Assumption
2
is

(2.2)

|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),S\otimes T\rangle|\prec\|S\|_{F}\|T\|_{F}

for all fixed
k\geq 3
,
m\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
,
S\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes m}
,
and
T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
.
(That (
2.2
)
implies Assumption
2
follows from the high moment estimate of Lemma

5.7
and Markov’s inequality.)
Specializing this growth condition
(
2.2
) to
S=\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}
and applying
\|T\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{n}^{k-m}\|T\|_{\infty}
would give

(2.3)

|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|\prec(\sqrt{n})^{k-m}\|T\|_{\infty}\prod_{t=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{t}\|_{2},

which is a version of (
2.1
) with a weaker bound
\sqrt{n}^{k-m}
in place of
\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}
. Although the condition (
2.1
) is not directly comparable to
(
2.2
),
one may interpret (
2.1
) as a certain strengthening of (
2.2
) by an extra factor of
n^{-1/2}
when restricted to rank-1 tensors
S
and measured in a
\ell_{\infty}
-type norm
\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}_{k}}
rather than
\|T\|_{F}
.

One may also check that for a “generic” tensor
\kappa_{k}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}
satisfying (
2.2
), Assumption
3
with the stronger bound of
\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}
cannot hold. Indeed, given any set
\mathcal{U}_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying the conditions of Assumption
3
, there exists a unit vector
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying

\sup_{\mathbf{u}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}|\langle\kappa_{k},\mathbf{s}\otimes\mathbf{u}_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{u}_{k}\rangle|\prec n^{-1/2}\sup_{\mathbf{u}:\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}=1}\sup_{\mathbf{u}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}|\langle\kappa_{k},\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{u}_{k}\rangle|\prec n^{-1/2}\|\kappa_{k}\|_{\text{inj}}

where
\|\kappa_{k}\|_{\text{inj}}
is the injective norm, and also

\|\mathbf{s}\cdot\kappa_{k}\|_{F}^{2}\asymp n^{-1}\|\kappa_{k}\|_{F}^{2}

where
\mathbf{s}\cdot\kappa_{k}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-1}
is the contraction of
\mathbf{s}
with
\kappa_{k}
in the first coordinate.
(It is easily checked that both conditions hold with high probability over a uniform random choice of
\mathbf{s}
on the unit sphere.)
Then for
m=1
and
T=\mathbf{s}\cdot\kappa_{k}
, we have

|\langle\kappa_{k},\mathbf{s}\otimes T\rangle|=\|\mathbf{s}\cdot\kappa_{k}\|_{F}^{2}\asymp n^{-1}\|\kappa_{k}\|_{F}^{2},\qquad\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}_{k}}\prec n^{-1/2}\|\kappa_{k}\|_{\text{inj}}.

If
\kappa_{k}
is generic and satisfies (
2.2
) — e.g.,
\kappa_{k}=n^{-(k-1)/2}Z_{k}
where
Z_{k}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}
is a symmetric Gaussian tensor with i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries up to symmetry — then we would expect

\|\kappa_{k}\|_{F}^{2}\asymp n^{-(k-1)}\|Z_{k}\|_{F}^{2}\asymp n
and
\|\kappa_{k}\|_{\text{inj}}\asymp n^{-(k-1)/2}\|Z_{k}\|_{\text{inj}}\asymp n^{-k/2+1}
(with high probability over
Z_{k}
). Thus (
2.1
) can only hold with the weaker factor
\sqrt{n}^{k-1}=\sqrt{n}^{k-m}
instead of

\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}
, so Assumption
3
requires a certain non-genericity of each cumulant tensor
\kappa_{k}
.

In the simplest setting of a separable model where
\mathbf{g}_{i}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{w}_{i}
and
\mathbf{w}_{i}
has independent
entries with bounded moments, it is easily checked (c.f. Proposition
2.12
) that Assumption
3
holds in a stronger form with
\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}
in (
2.1
) replaced by
\sqrt{n}^{\mathbf{1}\{m=1\}}
.
Importantly, Assumptions
2
and
3
are significantly more general than requiring this separable form, and we discuss several illustrative examples in Section

2.5
.
We show also in Section

2.5
that neither Assumption
2
or

3
strictly implies the other: for a Gaussian mixture model with random variance, there is a regime where Assumption
3
holds while Assumption
2
fails; conversely, for the spherical
4
-spin model at very high temperature (e.g.
\beta=n^{-1/6}
), Assumption
2
holds while Assumption
3
fails, precisely through the large gap between injective and Frobenius tensor norms discussed above.

2.2.
Deformed Marchenko-Pastur law and regular spectral domains

Under general conditions encompassing those of Assumptions
1
and

2
, the empirical eigenvalue distributions of

K,\widetilde{K}
are well-approximated in large dimensions by
deterministic laws
\mu_{0},\tilde{\mu}_{0}

[
61
,
76
,
74
]
. We
review here relevant background regarding
\mu_{0},\tilde{\mu}_{0}
and their
regularity properties.

Denote the ordered eigenvalues of
\Sigma
by

\sigma_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\sigma_{n}\geq 0.

For each
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, there exists a unique solution
\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)\in\mathbb{C}^{+}

[
61
]
to the Marchenko-Pastur equation

(2.4)

z=-\frac{1}{\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{\alpha}}{1+\sigma_{\alpha}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)}.

Define also
m_{0}(z)\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
by

(2.5)

\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)=\gamma m_{0}(z)+(1-\gamma)(-1/z),\qquad\gamma=n/N.

Then
\mu_{0}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{0}
are the (unique) probability distributions
whose Stieltjes transforms are given by
m_{0},\widetilde{m}_{0}:\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}
,

(2.6)

m_{0}(z)=\int\frac{1}{x-z}\,d\mu_{0}(x),\qquad\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)=\int\frac{1}{x-z}\,d\widetilde{\mu}_{0}(x).

This coincides with (
1.1
), and moreover
\mu_{0}
is the deformed Marchenko-Pastur law.
Note that the relation (
2.5
) implies that

\widetilde{\mu}_{0}=\gamma\mu_{0}+(1-\gamma)\delta_{0}
(denoting a mixture of

\mu_{0}
and an atom at 0) if
\gamma\leq 1
, or

\mu_{0}=(1/\gamma)\widetilde{\mu}_{0}+(1-1/\gamma)\delta_{0}
if
\gamma\geq 1
. In
particular,

\operatorname{supp}(\mu_{0})\cap(0,\infty)=\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})\cap(0,\infty).

It is shown in
[
75
]
that
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}

admits a continuous density at each point
x>0
, given by

\rho_{0}(x)=\lim_{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}\rightarrow x}\frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z).

Furthermore,
\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})\cap(0,\infty)
is a finite union of
intervals characterized in the following way: Let
\bar{n}=\operatorname{rank}(\Sigma)
and
consider the meromorphic function
with poles
P=\{0,{-}\sigma_{1}^{-1},\ldots,-\sigma_{\bar{n}}^{-1}\}
,

(2.7)

z_{0}(m)={-}\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{\alpha}}{1+\sigma_{\alpha}m},\qquad m\in\mathbb{C}\setminus P,

which locally inverts
\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)
defined by (
2.4
).
Then under Assumption
1
(c.f.
[
54
, Lemmas 2.4–2.6]
),

z_{0}
has an even number
2p
of critical points counting multiplicity on the
extended real line
\bar{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
,
with exactly one critical point
m_{1}\in(-\sigma_{1}^{-1},0)
, either 0 or 2 critical points in each interval

(-\sigma_{k+1}^{-1},-\sigma_{k}^{-1})
where
\sigma_{k}\neq\sigma_{k+1}
,
and exactly one critical point
m_{2p}\in(-\infty,-\sigma_{\bar{n}}^{-1})\cup(0,\infty]
which is
m_{2p}=\infty
if
\bar{n}=N
. Ordering these critical points as

0>m_{1}>m_{2}\geq m_{3}>m_{4}\geq m_{5}>\ldots>m_{2p-2}\geq m_{2p-1}>-\sigma_{\bar{n}}^{-1}

and
m_{2p}\in(-\infty,-\sigma_{\bar{n}}^{-1})\cup(0,\infty]
, we have

(2.8)

C>x_{1}>x_{2}\geq x_{3}>x_{4}\geq x_{5}>\ldots>x_{2p-2}\geq x_{2p-1}>x_{2p}\geq 0,\quad\text{ where }x_{j}=z_{0}(m_{j}),

for a constant
C>0
and

\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})\cap(0,\infty)=\Big(\bigcup_{k=1}^{p}[x_{2k},x_{2k-1}]\Big)\cap(0,\infty)\subseteq[0,C].

If
m_{j}
is a critical point of multiplicity 1, then
x_{j}
is an edge of

\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
(i.e. a boundary point of
\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})
),
which is a right edge for
j
odd and a left edge for
j
even. If

m_{2k}=m_{2k+1}
is a critical point of multiplicity 2, then
x_{2k}=x_{2k+1}

is a cusp of
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
(i.e. interior point
x\in\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})
where the density
\rho_{0}(x)=0
). We refer to
[x_{2k},x_{2k-1}]
for

k=1,\ldots,p
as the bulk components of
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
.

Corresponding to these bulk components and edges/cusps, and denoting
z=E+i\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
where
E=\operatorname{Re}z
and
\eta=\operatorname{Im}z
,
we define for
\delta>0
and
\tau\in(0,1)
the domains

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{k}^{b}(\delta,\tau)

\displaystyle=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:E\in[x_{2k}+\delta,x_{2k-1}-\delta],\,N^{-1+\tau}\leq\eta\leq 1\}

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{j}^{e}(\delta,\tau)

\displaystyle=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:E\in[x_{j}-\delta,x_{j}+\delta],\,N^{-1+\tau}\leq\eta\leq 1\}

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{o}(\delta,\tau)

\displaystyle=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:\operatorname{dist}(z,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0}))\geq\delta,\,|z|\geq\delta,\,|E|\leq\delta^{-1},\,N^{-1+\tau}\leq\eta\leq 1\}.

Here,
\operatorname{dist}(z,U)=\inf_{x\in U}|x-z|
.
Our local law results will hold over those domains which satisfy appropriate
regularity properties as discussed in
[
54
]
, and which we
summarize in the following definition.

Definition 2.3

(Regular spectral domain)
.

We call

\mathbf{D}\subset\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:\eta\in(0,1]\}

a
spectral domain
if
z=E+i\eta\in\mathbf{D}
implies that
z^{\prime}=E+i\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{D}
for each
\eta^{\prime}\in[\eta,1]
.
Corresponding to
z\in\mathbf{D}
, denote

\kappa=\min_{j=1}^{2p}|x_{j}-E|
, and define

L(z)=\{z\}\cup\{w\in\mathbf{D}:\operatorname{Re}{w}=E,\,\operatorname{Im}{w}\in[\eta,1]\cap\{N^{-5},2N^{-5},3N^{-5},\ldots,1\}\}.

Then the domain
\mathbf{D}
is
regular
if there exist constants
C,c>0
such
that the following hold:

(a)

(Basic bounds for
\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)
) For all
z\in\mathbf{D}
,

(2.10)

\begin{gathered}c\leq|z|\leq C,\qquad c\leq|\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)|\leq C,\qquad\min_{\alpha\in[n]}|1+\sigma_{\alpha}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)|\geq c,\\
cg(z)\leq\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)\leq Cg(z)\quad\text{ where }\quad g(z)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}&\text{if }E\in\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})\\
\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}&\text{if }E\notin\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})\end{cases}\end{gathered}

(b)

(Stability of the Marchenko-Pastur equation)
Let
u:\mathbb{C}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{+}
be the Stieltjes transform of any probability measure, and let
\Delta:\mathbf{D}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
be any deterministic function satisfying:

•

(Boundedness)
N^{-2}\leq\Delta(z)\leq(\log N)^{-1}

for all
z\in\mathbf{D}
,

•

(Lipschitz continuity)
|\Delta(z)-\Delta(w)|\leq N^{2}|z-w|

for all
z,w\in\mathbf{D}
, and

•

(Monotonicity) For each fixed
E
, the function

\eta\mapsto\Delta(E+i\eta)
is non-increasing over
\eta\in[N^{-1+\tau},1]
.

For each
z\in\mathbf{D}
, if
|z_{0}(u(w))-w|\leq\Delta(w)
for every
w\in L(z)
, then

(2.11)

|u(z)-\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)|\leq\frac{C\Delta(z)}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+\sqrt{\Delta(z)}}.

The following result was shown in
[
54
]
for the regularity of the
preceding domains
\mathbf{D}_{k}^{b}(\delta,\tau)
,

\mathbf{D}_{j}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
, and
\mathbf{D}^{o}(\delta,\tau)
.

Lemma 2.4

(
[
54
]
)
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds. Fix any constant
\tau\in(0,1)
.

(a)

(Regular bulk component) Suppose, for a bulk component

[x_{2k},x_{2k-1}]
, there exist constants
\delta,c_{0}>0
such that
\rho_{0}(x)>c_{0}

for all
x\in[x_{2k}+\delta,x_{2k-1}-\delta]
. Then
\mathbf{D}_{k}^{b}(\delta,\tau)
is
regular.

(b)

(Regular edge) Suppose, for an edge
x_{j}=z_{0}(m_{j})
, there exists a
constant
\delta>0
such that

x_{j}>\delta,\qquad|x_{j}-x_{k}|>\delta\text{ for each }k\neq j,\qquad\min_{\alpha}|m_{j}+\sigma_{\alpha}^{-1}|>\delta.

Then there exists a constant
\delta^{\prime}>0
such that

\mathbf{D}_{j}^{e}(\delta^{\prime},\tau)
is regular.

(c)

(Outside the spectrum) Fix any constant
\delta>0
.
Then
\mathbf{D}^{o}(\delta,\tau)
is regular.

(For each statement, Definition
2.3
holds with constants
C,c>0

depending on
\tau,\delta,\delta^{\prime},c_{0}
.)

Proof.

Parts (a), (b), and regularity of
\mathbf{D}^{o}(\delta,\tau)\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:\operatorname{dist}(E,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})\cup\{0\})\geq\delta/2\}
in (c) follow from
[
54
, Lemmas A.4, A.5, A.6,
A.8]
. The remaining points
z\in\mathbf{D}^{o}(\delta,\tau)
with
\operatorname{dist}(E,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})\cup\{0\})<\delta/2
must satisfy
\eta\geq\delta/2
: For these points, (
2.10
) follows from the bounds
\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}\geq c\eta
and
|\widetilde{m}_{0}|\leq\eta^{-1}
implied by (
2.6
), and (
2.11
) follows directly from the condition
|z_{0}(u(z))-z|\leq\Delta(z)
, uniqueness of the solution in
\mathbb{C}^{+}
to (
2.4
) which implies

u(z)=\widetilde{m}_{0}(z+\Delta)
for some
|\Delta|\leq\Delta(z)\leq(\log N)^{-1}
, and the Lipschitz continuity
|\widetilde{m}_{0}^{\prime}(z)|\leq\eta^{-2}
.
∎

We remark that the regularity conditions of Definition
2.3
are satisfied in a wide range of examples; see
[
54
, Section 3]
for detailed verifications under various conditions on the population covariance
\Sigma
.

2.3.
Local law for the Stieltjes transform

This and the following section state the main results of our paper. Proofs of Theorems
2.5
,
2.8
,
2.10
and their corollaries will be given in Section
4
.

For spectral parameters
z=E+i\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, define the resolvents and Stieltjes
transforms of
K
and
\widetilde{K}
by

R(z)=(K-zI_{n})^{-1},\quad m(z)=n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}R(z),

\widetilde{R}(z)=(\widetilde{K}-zI_{N})^{-1},\quad\widetilde{m}(z)=N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\widetilde{R}(z).

We will denote the entries of these resolvents by

R_{\alpha\beta}(z)=\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}^{*}R(z)\mathbf{e}_{\beta}
and

\widetilde{R}_{ij}(z)=\mathbf{e}_{i}^{*}\widetilde{R}(z)\mathbf{e}_{j}
.
For
z=E+i\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, define also the error control parameter

\Psi(z)=\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{Im}{\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)}}{N\eta}}+\frac{1}{N\eta}

where we recall the bounds for
\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)

from (
2.10
).

The following theorem establishes a local law for the Stieltjes transforms

m(z)
and
\widetilde{m}(z)
, together with an entrywise local law for the
resolvent
\widetilde{R}(z)
of the Gram matrix
\widetilde{K}
,
over a regular spectral domain.

Theorem 2.5
.

(Averaged and entrywise local laws)
Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold,
and
\mathbf{D}
is a regular spectral domain. Then for any
\varepsilon,D>0
, there
exists a constant
C\equiv C(\varepsilon,D)>0
such that
with probability at least
1-CN^{-D}
, for all
z=E+i\eta\in\mathbf{D}
we have

(2.12)

|m(z)-m_{0}(z)|\leq N^{\varepsilon}\frac{\Psi(z)^{2}}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+\Psi(z)},\qquad|\widetilde{m}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)|\leq N^{\varepsilon}\frac{\Psi(z)^{2}}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+\Psi(z)},

(2.13)

\max_{1\leq i,j\leq N}|\widetilde{R}_{ij}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)\mathbf{1}\{i=j\}|\leq N^{\varepsilon}\Psi(z).

Remark 2.6
.

The bound (
2.10
) implies
\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)\leq C\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}
in all cases, so the right side of
(
2.12
) may be further bounded as

\frac{\Psi(z)^{2}}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+\Psi(z)}\leq\frac{2C\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}(N\eta)^{-1}+2(N\eta)^{-2}}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+(N\eta)^{-1}}\leq\frac{2(C+1)}{N\eta}.

The result stated in (
2.12
) is equivalent to a
bound of
N^{\varepsilon}(N\eta)^{-1}
for
E\in\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{\mu}_{0})
inside the
spectral support, where
\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)\asymp\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}
. The stronger form of (
2.12
)
for
E\notin\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{\mu}_{0})
has served useful to show optimal concentration of the eigenvalues of
\widetilde{K}
around

\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{\mu}_{0})
(see e.g.
[
69
, Eq. (8.8)]
).

We state here a few known implications
of Theorem
2.5
on eigenvalue rigidity and eigenvector delocalization
[
40
,
42
]
. Let

\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{\min(n,N)}

be the leading eigenvalues of
\widetilde{K}
(or equivalently of
K
).
For any
i\in\{1,\ldots,\min(n,N)\}
, define the classical eigenvalue location

\theta(i)>0
through

N\int_{\theta(i)}^{\infty}\rho_{0}(x)\;dx=i-\frac{1}{2}

where we recall the density
\rho_{0}(x)
of
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
on
(0,\infty)
.
Corresponding to each edge/cusp
x_{j}
defined in (
2.8
), denote

(2.14)

N_{j}=N\int_{x_{j}}^{\infty}\rho_{0}(x)\;dx.

(It is shown in
[
54
, Lemma A.1]
that
N_{j}
is always an integer.)

Corollary 2.7
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold.
Then for any
\delta,\varepsilon,D>0
, there exist constants
\tau\equiv\tau(\varepsilon)\in(0,1)
and
C\equiv C(\delta,\varepsilon,D)>0
such that
with probability at least
1-CN^{-D}
, the following hold:

(a)

(Spectral support)
K
has no eigenvalues outside

\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\operatorname{dist}(x,\operatorname{supp}(\mu_{0}))\leq\delta\}
, and
\widetilde{K}
has no eigenvalues outside

\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\operatorname{dist}(x,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0}))\leq\delta\}
.

(b)

(
N^{-2/3}
-concentration at regular edges)
If
x_{2k-1}
is any right edge for which

\mathbf{D}_{2k-1}^{e}(\delta,1/3)
is regular, then
K
and
\widetilde{K}
have no
eigenvalues in
[x_{2k-1}+N^{-2/3+\varepsilon},x_{2k-1}+\delta]
.

The analogous statement holds for a left edge
x_{2k}

and
[x_{2k}-\delta,x_{2k}-N^{-2/3+\varepsilon}]
.

(c)

(Rigidity of eigenvalues)
If
x_{2k-1}
is any right edge for which

\mathbf{D}_{2k-1}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
is regular, then

|\lambda_{i}-\theta(i)|\leq(i-N_{2k-1})^{-1/3}N^{-2/3+\varepsilon}\text{ for all $i$ such that $\theta(i)\in[x_{2k-1}-\delta,x_{2k-1}]$.}

If
x_{2k}
is any left edge for which
\mathbf{D}_{2k}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
is regular,
then

|\lambda_{i}-\theta(i)|\leq(N_{2k}-i)^{-1/3}N^{-2/3+\varepsilon}\text{ for all $i$ such that $\theta(i)\in[x_{2k},x_{2k}+\delta]$}.

If
[x_{2k},x_{2k-1}]
is any bulk component for which

\mathbf{D}_{k}^{b}(\delta,\tau)
is regular, and at least one edge domain

\mathbf{D}_{2k}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
or
\mathbf{D}_{2k-1}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
is also regular, then

|\lambda_{i}-\theta(i)|\leq N^{-1+\varepsilon}\text{ for all $i$ such that $\theta(i)\in[x_{2k}+\delta,x_{2k-1}-\delta]$.}

(d)

(
l_{\infty}
-delocalization of eigenvectors of
\widetilde{K}
) If
x_{j}
is any edge for which
\mathbf{D}_{j}^{e}(\delta,\varepsilon)
is regular, then for each eigenvalue

\lambda\in[x_{j}-\delta,x_{j}+\delta]
of
\widetilde{K}
and each associated eigenvector
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}
,

\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N}}.

If
[x_{2k},x_{2k-1}]
is any bulk component for which
\mathbf{D}_{k}^{b}(\delta,\tau)
is regular, then the same holds for every eigenvector of
\widetilde{K}
corresponding to an eigenvalue
\lambda\in[x_{2k}+\delta,x_{2k-1}-\delta]
.

2.4.
Anisotropic local law for the linearized resolvent

For all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, define the linearized resolvent

(2.15)

\Pi(z)=\begin{bmatrix}-zI_{n}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}G\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}G^{*}&-I_{N}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}^{(n+N)\times(n+N)}.

Note that by Schur’s complement, we have

\Pi(z)=\begin{bmatrix}R(z)&*\\
*&z\widetilde{R}(z)\end{bmatrix}

where the two diagonal blocks contain the resolvents of
K
and
\widetilde{K}
.
Under the additional condition of Assumption
3
,
the following theorem establishes an optimal anisotropic
local law for
\Pi(z)
.

Theorem 2.8

(Anisotropic local law)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
, and

3
hold, and
\mathbf{D}
is a regular spectral domain.
Then for any
\varepsilon,D>0
, there exists a constant
C\equiv C(\varepsilon,D)>0
such
that the following holds:

Fix any deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1},\mathbf{u}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Then with probability at least
1-CN^{-D}
, for all
z=E+i\eta\in\mathbf{D}
we have

\left|\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{*}&\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}\end{bmatrix}\left(\Pi(z)-\begin{bmatrix}(-zI_{n}-z\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)\Sigma)^{-1}&0\\
0&z\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)I_{N}\end{bmatrix}\right)\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{u}_{2}\\
\mathbf{v}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right|\leq N^{\varepsilon}\Psi(z).

This implies the delocalization of eigenvectors of both
K
and
\widetilde{K}

in any fixed basis, extending the entrywise delocalization for
\widetilde{K}
in
Corollary
2.7
(d).

Corollary 2.9

(Delocalization of eigenvectors of
K
and
\widetilde{K}
)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
, and

3
hold. Fix any deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Then for any
\delta,\varepsilon,D>0
, there exists a constant
C\equiv C(\delta,\varepsilon,D)>0
such that with probability at least
1-CN^{-D}
, the following
holds:

If
x_{j}
is any edge of
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
for which
\mathbf{D}_{j}^{e}(\delta,\varepsilon)
is regular, then for each eigenvalue

\lambda\in[x_{j}-\delta,x_{j}+\delta]
and associated eigenvector
\mathbf{x}
or
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}
of
K
or
\widetilde{K}
,

|\mathbf{u}^{*}\mathbf{x}|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N}},\qquad|\mathbf{v}^{*}\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N}}.

If
[x_{2k},x_{2k-1}]
is any bulk component for which
\mathbf{D}_{k}^{b}(\delta,\tau)
is regular, then the same holds for every eigenvector
\mathbf{x}
or
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}
of
K
or
\widetilde{K}
corresponding to an eigenvalue
\lambda\in[x_{2k}+\delta,x_{2k-1}-\delta]
.

Although the primary focus of our work is on Theorems
2.5

and
2.8
for local spectral parameters
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, let
us clarify that in the case of
z=E+i\eta
where
E
has
constant separation from the spectral support,
our arguments also establish the analogue of Theorem
2.8

under Assumptions
1
and
2
alone. We
summarize this result here.

Theorem 2.10

(Outside the spectrum)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold.
Fix any constant
\delta>0
and consider

\bar{\mathbf{D}}^{o}(\delta)=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{dist}(z,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0}))\geq\delta,\,|z|\geq\delta,\,|E|\leq\delta^{-1},\,\eta\in[-1,1]\}.

Then for any
\varepsilon,D>0
, there exists a constant

C\equiv C(\delta,\varepsilon,D)>0
such that the following hold:

With probability
1-CN^{-D}
, for all
z\in\bar{\mathbf{D}}^{o}(\delta)
we have

|m(z)-m_{0}(z)|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{N},\qquad|\widetilde{m}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{N}.

Furthermore, fix any deterministic unit vectors

\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1},\mathbf{u}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
With probability
1-CN^{-D}
, for all
z\in\bar{\mathbf{D}}^{o}(\delta)
we have

\left|\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{*}&\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}\end{bmatrix}\left(\Pi(z)-\begin{bmatrix}(-zI_{n}-z\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)\Sigma)^{-1}&0\\
0&z\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)I_{N}\end{bmatrix}\right)\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{u}_{2}\\
\mathbf{v}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N}}.

This approximation for
R(z)
(the upper-left block of
\Pi(z)
) was
established previously in
[
86
]
, which used this result to
give a characterization of the outlier eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the
sample covariance matrix
K
under spiked models for
\Sigma
.

Remark 2.11
.

If
n<N
and
\operatorname{dist}(0,\operatorname{supp}(\mu_{0}))\geq 2\delta
for some constant

\delta>0
, then Corollary
2.7
(a) implies that with high probability,
m(z)
,
m_{0}(z)
,
R(z)
, and
(-zI_{n}-z\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)\Sigma)^{-1}
are all analytic in a neighborhood of 0. Then the bounds
|m-m_{0}|\leq N^{\varepsilon}/N
and

|\mathbf{u}_{1}^{*}(R-(-zI_{n}-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1})\mathbf{u}_{2}|\leq N^{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{N}

in Theorem
2.10
extend to
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|\leq\delta\}
by the maximum modulus principle.
Similarly, the bounds
|\widetilde{m}-\widetilde{m}_{0}|\leq N^{\varepsilon}/N

and
|\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}(\widetilde{R}-\widetilde{m}_{0}I_{N})\mathbf{v}_{2}|\leq N^{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{N}
extend to
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|\leq\delta\}
when

n>N
and
\operatorname{dist}(0,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0}))\geq 2\delta
.

2.5.
Examples

In this section, assuming
\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{N}
are equal in law to
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we provide concrete examples of distributions for
\mathbf{g}
that satisfy Assumptions
2
and
3
. All proofs of results in this section are deferred to Section
5
.

2.5.1.
Separable distributions

In the separable setting of
[
54
]
where
\mathbf{g}=X\mathbf{w}
for a vector

\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
having independent entries, the cumulant tensor
\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g})

is a contraction of a diagonal tensor
\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}

with
X
along each coordinate axis. It is easily checked that in this case,
Assumption
3
holds in a stronger form as guaranteed in the
following proposition.

Proposition 2.12
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds, where
\Sigma=XX^{*}
for a matrix
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
cn\leq d\leq Cn
for constants
C,c>0
.
Suppose
\mathbf{g}=X\mathbf{w}
where
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}[\alpha])_{\alpha=1}^{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
has independent entries satisfying

\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w}[\alpha]=0
,
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w}[\alpha]^{2}=1
, and
\mathbb{E}|\mathbf{w}[\alpha]|^{k}<C_{k}
for each
k\geq 3
and a constant
C_{k}>0
.

Then
\mathbf{g}
satisfies the conditions of Assumptions
2
and

3
. Each set
\mathcal{U}_{k}

of Assumption
3
may be taken as

\mathcal{U}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{U}=\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\}\cup\{X\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,X\mathbf{e}_{d}\},

and the bound (
2.1
) holds in the stronger form
(for any
k\geq 3
,
1\leq m\leq k-1
, and a constant
C\equiv C(k)>0
)

(2.16)

|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|\leq C\sqrt{n}^{\mathbf{1}\{m=1\}}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\prod_{t=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{t}\|_{2}.

2.5.2.
Conditionally mean-zero distributions

We next consider a more general class of distributions
\mathbf{g}=X\mathbf{w}
where the entries of
\mathbf{w}
need not be independent, but satisfy a conditional mean-zero assumption that
\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{i}]=0
whenever one index among
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in[d]
is distinct from the rest. We check that in such settings, Assumption
3
for
\mathbf{g}
is implied by Assumption
2
for
\mathbf{w}
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds, where
\Sigma=XX^{*}
for a matrix

X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
cn\leq d\leq Cn
for constants
C,c>0
.
Suppose
\mathbf{g}=X\mathbf{w}
where Assumption

2
holds for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{*}=I
, and
\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{i}]=0

for any indices
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in[d]
such that
\alpha_{1}\notin\{\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\}
.

Then
\mathbf{g}
satisfies
Assumptions
2
and
3
, where each set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
may again be taken as

\mathcal{U}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{U}=\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\}\cup\{X\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,X\mathbf{e}_{d}\}.

Example 2.14
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{w}
is a uniformly random vector on the sphere
\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}^{2}=d\}
. It is clear that all conditions of Proposition
2.13
hold for
\mathbf{w}
.

Example 2.15
.

Suppose
\mathbf{w}
is an isotropic “unconditionally” log-concave random vector, i.e. the log-density
\log p(\mathbf{w})\in[-\infty,\infty)
is a concave function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{*}=I
,
and
\mathbf{w}
is equal in law to
\mathbf{w}\odot\mathbf{s}

(the entrywise product) for any sign vector
\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}
. The random Gram matrix

\widetilde{K}=\frac{1}{N}G^{*}G,\qquad G=[\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{N}]

where
\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{N}
are i.i.d. copies of
\mathbf{w}
was studied recently in

[
19
]
, who built upon an entrywise local law for
\widetilde{K}
in
[
69
]
to directly show Tracy-Widom fluctuations of its leading eigenvalue.

For such distributions, Assumption
2
for
\mathbf{w}
follows from a log-concave isoperimetric inequality, as was shown in
[
19
, Lemma 3.3]
. Then it is readily checked that all conditions of Proposition
2.13
hold for
\mathbf{w}
, so our results imply that an isotropic local law holds for
G=[\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{N}]
, and also that an anisotropic local law holds for

G=[\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{N}]
where
\mathbf{g}_{i}=X\mathbf{w}_{i}
.

Example 2.16
.

Suppose
\mathbf{w}
follows a mixture model where there exists a random variable
\lambda
such that
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}[\alpha])_{\alpha=1}^{d}
has i.i.d. entries conditional on
\lambda
, and for some constants
C_{k}>0
,

(2.17)

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}[\alpha]\mid\lambda]=0,\;\;\mathbb{E}[|\mathbf{w}[\alpha]|^{k}\mid\lambda]\leq C_{k}\quad\text{ for all }k\geq 2\text{ and }\lambda\in\Lambda,\\
\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}[\alpha]^{2}\mid\lambda]]=1.\end{gathered}

Thus
\mathbf{w}
has a mean-zero, isotropic, and exchangeable law, and the conditional law
(\mathbf{w}\mid\lambda)
satisfies Assumption
2
uniformly over
\lambda\in\Lambda
. If, in addition,

(2.18)

|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}[\alpha]^{2}\mid\lambda]-1|\prec 1/\sqrt{n},

then all conditions of Proposition
2.13
hold for
\mathbf{w}
. (See Section
5
for a verification of this and the remaining claims in this example.)

In general, the condition (
2.18
) is required for the local law estimates of Theorems
2.5
and
2.8
to hold for such a model: Consider for example

(2.19)

\mathbb{P}[\lambda=1]=\mathbb{P}[\lambda=-1]=1/2,\quad(\mathbf{w}\mid\lambda)\sim N(0,(1+c_{n}\lambda)I)\text{ for some }c_{n}>0.

Then

|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}[\alpha]^{2}\mid\lambda]-1|=c_{n}
, so (
2.18
) requires
c_{n}\prec n^{-1/2}
. Note that if
c_{n}\equiv c
is a fixed constant, then even the convergence in probability of (
1.2
) fails; in this setting, the standard Marchenko-Pastur law
\mu_{0}
(corresponding to
\Sigma=I
) is not the correct deterministic equivalent for the spectral distribution of
K=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{w}_{i}\mathbf{w}_{i}^{*}
, and one must instead view
K
as a Gram matrix and take the “companion” law of the deformed Marchenko-Pastur law that results from conditioning on the corresponding latent variables
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}
:

K=N^{-1}\widetilde{W}^{*}\operatorname{diag}(1+c\lambda_{1},...,1+c\lambda_{N})\widetilde{W},\quad\widetilde{W}_{ij}\mathop{\sim}^{\text{i.i.d.}}N(0,1).

If
n^{-1/2}\ll c_{n}\ll 1
, then
\mu_{0}
provides a global approximation for the spectral distribution of
K
, but Assumption
2
does not hold, and an averaged local law also does not hold in the quantitative form of Theorem
2.5
.

For this model (
2.19
), one may also directly check that Assumption
3
holds under the weaker condition
c_{n}\prec n^{-1/4}
. Thus for
n^{-1/2}\ll c_{n}\ll n^{-1/4}
, Assumption
3
holds while Assumption
2
does not,
illustrating that Assumption
3
is not strictly stronger than Assumption
2
.

2.5.3.
Random features models

Consider the model
\mathbf{g}=\sigma(X\mathbf{w})
where
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

has independent entries and
\sigma(\cdot)
is an entrywise nonlinear map.
This model has been of considerable interest in the statistical learning
literature on random features regression models

[
58
,
49
,
62
]
and linear
approximants for neural networks
[
68
,
45
,
23
]
. The following proposition verifies
that Assumptions
2
and
3
both hold
in a linear scaling regime
n\asymp d
under suitable conditions for

\sigma(\cdot)
. We note that we allow the activation function to be different on each entry.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be a deterministic matrix,
where
\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C
and
d\leq Cn
for a constant
C>0
.
Let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a random vector with independent entries, and let

\mathbf{g}=\sigma(X\mathbf{w})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

where
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is given by
\sigma(\mathbf{x})=(\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{x}[i]))_{i=1}^{n}

for scalar functions
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
applied entrywise.
Suppose
\mathbb{E}\sigma(X\mathbf{w})=0
and
\mathbb{E}\sigma(X\mathbf{w})\sigma(X\mathbf{w})^{*}=\Sigma
, where

\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfies Assumption
1
.

(a)

Suppose the entries of
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}[\alpha])_{\alpha=1}^{d}

satisfy
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w}[\alpha]=0
,

\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w}[\alpha]^{2}=1
, and
\mathbb{E}|\mathbf{w}[\alpha]|^{k}\leq C_{k}
for each
k\geq 3
and a
constant
C_{k}>0
. If, for some constants
C,D>0
, each function

\sigma_{i}(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{D}a_{il}x^{l}

is a polynomial with degree at most
D
and coefficients satisfying

\max_{i=1}^{n}\max_{l=0}^{D}|a_{il}|<C
, then
\mathbf{g}
satisfies
Assumptions
2
and
3
.

(b)

Suppose that
\mathbf{w}\sim N(0,I_{d})
is a standard Gaussian vector.
If, for some constants
C>0
and
\beta>1/2
, each function
\sigma_{i}
admits a
(absolutely convergent) series representation

\sigma_{i}(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}a_{il}x^{l}

where
\max_{i=1}^{n}|a_{il}|<C(l!)^{-\beta}
, then
\mathbf{g}
also
satisfies Assumptions
2
and
3
.

Condition (b) is equivalent to the condition that
\sigma_{i}(x)
extends to an
entire function on
\mathbb{C}
satisfying the growth
|\sigma_{i}(z)|\leq C\exp(c|z|^{1/\beta})
for some constants
C,c>0
and
\beta>1/2
.
In particular, for
\beta=1
, this is the class of entire functions of
exponential type (i.e. having Fourier transform with compact support

[
72
, Theorem 19.3]
),
which has also been considered recently in a slightly different context of random inner-product kernel
matrices
[
55
,
59
]
.

2.5.4.
Random features tilt

A related class of examples arises when the random features structure appears not in the random vector itself but in its density. Consider a random vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with density proportional to
\exp(-U(\mathbf{w}))
, where

(2.20)

U(\mathbf{w})=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{w})

for deterministic directions
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, smooth nonlinearities
\sigma_{i}
, and a coupling constant
\lambda
. The following proposition establishes that for
|\lambda|
sufficiently small,
d
at most polynomial in
n
, and under a boundedness condition for the derivatives of
\sigma_{i}(\cdot)
, the centered vector
\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w}
satisfies both Assumptions
2
and
3
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
be a deterministic matrix with rows
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}

such that
\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C
and
d\leq n^{C}
for a constant
C>0
. Suppose

\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a random vector having density proportional to
\exp(-U(\mathbf{w}))
,
where
U(\mathbf{w})
is given by (
2.20
), and
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are smooth functions
satisfying

\sup_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|(\sigma_{i}^{(k)}(\mathbf{a}[i]))_{i=1}^{d}\|_{2}\leq C^{k}\text{ for all }k\geq 1

(where
\sigma_{i}^{(k)}
is the
k^{\text{th}}
-order derivative). Let

\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w},\qquad\Sigma=\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}\mathbf{g}^{*}.

Then there exists a constant
\lambda_{*}>0
such that whenever

|\lambda|<\lambda_{*}
, Assumptions

1
,
2
, and
3
hold for

\Sigma
and
\mathbf{g}
. Each set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
of Assumption
3
may be
taken as

\mathcal{U}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{U}=\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\}\cup\{\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}\},

and the bound (
2.1
) holds in the stronger form
(for any
k\geq 3
,
1\leq m\leq k-1
, and a constant
C\equiv C(k)>0
)

\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle\leq C\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\right)\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}.

This model is of particular interest when
d
is bounded and
\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}=1
: the higher-order cumulant tensors of
\mathbf{g}
then contain spikes along the directions
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
.
Moreover this higher cumulant structure persists even in the
whitened vector
\tilde{\mathbf{g}}=\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathbf{g}
which has identity covariance and satisfies both Assumption
2
and
3
. Our local law implies that the resolvent of the sample covariance of i.i.d. copies of
\tilde{\mathbf{g}}
is well-approximated by the Marchenko-Pastur deterministic equivalent, showing that even refined local statistics of the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the sample covariance matrix alone cannot recover the directions
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
, in the regime of sample size
N
proportional to the dimension
n
. This style of “spiked cumulant” model and the associated hidden-direction recovery task is closely related to sample-complexity and computational lower-bound results for non-Gaussian component analysis and higher-order correlation models
[
85
,
79
,
31
,
32
,
78
]
.

2.6.
Negative examples

For illustration, we discuss also some examples which do not satisfy the hypotheses of Assumptions
2
and
3
, to illustrate the necessity of the assumptions and/or limitations of our current techniques.

2.6.1.
Chaos vectors

Let
M,d
be integers with
1\leq M\leq d
.
For a vector
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}[1],\ldots,\mathbf{x}[d])\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, of i.i.d. standard normal random variables, define a vector

\mathbf{g}[i,j]=\mathbf{x}[i]\mathbf{x}[j]\quad\text{for }1\leq i<j\leq\min\{i+M,d\}.

(The case of
M=d
corresponds to all pairwise products of entries of
X
.)
We set
n
to be the size of the indexing set
\{(i,j):1\leq i<j\leq\min(i+M,d)\}
, which is up to constants
n\asymp Md
. This random vector provides an example which satisfies the weak concentration of quadratic forms (
1.2
) but does not have the rate in Assumption
2
; moreover, it is easily verified not to satisfy the averaged local law with the optimal rate.

More precisely, for any test matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, it is readily checked that

\mathbb{E}|\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}|^{2}\asymp M\|A\|_{F}^{2},

and hence from hypercontractivity of the Gaussian measure, for any
k\geq 2
,

\mathbb{E}|\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}|^{k}\leq C_{k}(\mathbb{E}|\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}^{*}A\mathbf{g}|^{2})^{k/2}\lesssim M^{k/2}\|A\|_{F}^{k}.

This suffices for the Bai-Zhou theorem
[
16
]
for any
M\leq d
, i.e. the Marchenko-Pastur law holds at the global spectral scale for the sample covariance matrix
K
defined by
N\asymp n
samples. However, it is also easily checked that

\operatorname{Var}(\operatorname{Tr}K)\asymp Mn/N\asymp M.

By a simple fourth moment estimate for
\operatorname{Tr}K-\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}K
, we can argue with probability bounded below independently of
n
that

|\operatorname{Tr}K-\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}K|\gtrsim\sqrt{M}.

Hence when
M=d^{\alpha}
for any
\alpha\in(0,1]
, this contradicts the eigenvalue rigidity that would follow from an optimal local law. Thus the rate of concentration assumed in Assumption
2
is needed to ensure that an averaged local law holds at the optimal scale.

2.6.2.
Spin glass models

As an illustration of the role of Assumption
3
, consider a spherical spin glass model. Let
J\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes p}
be a symmetric random tensor with i.i.d. standard Gaussian entries (up to symmetry), scaled so that
\|J\|_{F}\asymp n^{p/2}
, and let
\mathbf{g}
be drawn from the Gibbs measure

d\mu(\mathbf{g})\propto\exp\bigl(\tfrac{\beta}{n^{(p-1)/2}}\langle J,\mathbf{g}^{\otimes p}\rangle\bigr)

on the sphere
\{\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|\mathbf{g}\|_{2}^{2}=n\}
, where
\beta>0
is the inverse temperature or coupling constant. For
\beta<\beta_{0}
some absolute constant, the measure
\mu
is easily checked to satisfy a log-Sobolev inequality with a constant independent of
n
, which implies concentration of quadratic forms; hence Assumption
2
holds and the averaged local law of Theorem
2.5
applies to the sample covariance matrix of i.i.d. draws from
\mu
.

However, for the spherical
4
-spin model, it can be quantified rigorously (c.f.
[
44
]
) that

\|\kappa_{4}\|_{\text{inj}}\leq C\Big(\beta^{3}+\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{1}{n}\Big),\qquad\|\kappa_{4}\|_{F}\geq c\beta\sqrt{n}.

At
\beta=n^{-1/6}
, this gives
\|\kappa_{4}\|_{\text{inj}}/\|\kappa_{4}\|_{F}^{2}\leq Cn^{-7/6}
, exhibiting a small ratio of injective norm to Frobenius norm.
Then by the reasoning discussed in Remark
2.2
,
Assumption
3
does not hold, so our current results do not imply an optimal anisotropic local law in the form of Theorem
2.8
for the sample covariance matrix of i.i.d. draws from such a model.
In this case of “disordered” cumulant tensors, we note that cancellations of fluctuations in the cumulant tensors themselves must be considered to obtain optimal estimates of various cumulant contractions, and this is not captured by the proof method we employ based on Assumption
3
.

2.7.
Proof ideas

We conclude this section by explaining briefly the main novelties of the
proofs. For any subset
S\subset[N]
, let
R^{(S)}=(N^{-1}\sum_{i\notin S}\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}-zI)^{-1}
denote the
resolvent of the sample covariance matrix leaving out the samples in
S
. Then
a classical argument of
[
76
]
, applying the definition of

z_{0}(\widetilde{m})
and the Sherman-Morrison identity

R=R^{(i)}-N^{-1}R^{(i)}\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}/(1+N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}\mathbf{g}_{i})
, shows the
equality

z_{0}(\widetilde{m})-z={-}\frac{1}{\widetilde{m}}\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}-\Sigma)R^{(i)}(I+\widetilde{m}\Sigma)^{-1}}{1+N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}\mathbf{g}_{i}}.

We introduce a control parameter
\Phi=\Phi(z)\in[N^{-1/2},1]
depending on
z
and
N
, whose explicit form is not important here (c.f. Lemma
3.4
).
Assuming the two a priori estimates

(2.21)

N^{-1}\|R^{(S)}\|_{F}\prec\Phi\text{ over all }S\subset[N]\text{ of bounded cardinality},\quad|\widetilde{m}-\widetilde{m}_{0}|\ll 1,

it is readily checked from
Assumption
2
and regularity of the spectral domain that

\left|\frac{N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}-\Sigma)R^{(i)}(I+\widetilde{m}\Sigma)^{-1}}{1+N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}\mathbf{g}_{i}}\right|\prec\Phi,

and hence also
|z_{0}(\widetilde{m})-z|\prec\Phi
. The main technical step to
establish an optimal averaged local law is to improve this estimate to

(2.22)

|z_{0}(\widetilde{m})-z|\prec\Phi^{2}.

The averaged local law then follows by applying the stability condition of (
2.11
) in conjunction with
a stochastic continuity argument as developed and used in
e.g.
[
39
,
36
,
35
,
69
,
54
]
.

To illustrate the fluctuation averaging mechanism that leads to
(
2.22
), and also to explain our main novelties for extending this
to an anisotropic local law, consider the slightly simpler quantity

\displaystyle\Delta(A)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}-\Sigma)R^{(i)}A

for any deterministic matrix
A\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
with
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
.
The bound (
2.22
) is analogous to the simpler bound
|\Delta(A)|\prec N\Phi^{2}
(ignoring the factors in the denominator of
z_{0}(\tilde{m})-z
, which do not significantly alter the proof strategy).
Applying the Sherman-Morrison identity
to resolve the dependence of
R^{(i)}
on
\mathbf{g}_{j}
and vice versa,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\Delta(A)|^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big[N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}-\Sigma)R^{(i)}A\cdot\overline{N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{g}_{j}\mathbf{g}_{j}^{*}-\Sigma)R^{(j)}A}\Big]

\displaystyle=O_{\prec}(N\Phi^{2})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Bigg(\underbrace{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}-\Sigma)\frac{R^{(ij)}A}{\sqrt{N}}}_{:=\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{(ij)}}-\underbrace{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}-\Sigma)\frac{R^{(ij)}}{N}\mathbf{g}_{j}\mathbf{g}_{j}^{*}\frac{R^{(ij)}A}{\sqrt{N}}}_{:=Z_{ijj}^{(ij)}}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{1}{1+N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{j}^{*}R^{(ij)}\mathbf{g}_{j}}}_{:=\mathcal{Q}_{j}^{(ij)}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\times\overline{\Bigg(\underbrace{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{g}_{j}\mathbf{g}_{j}^{*}-\Sigma)\frac{R^{(ij)}A}{\sqrt{N}}}_{:=\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{(ij)}}-\underbrace{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{g}_{j}\mathbf{g}_{j}^{*}-\Sigma)\frac{R^{(ij)}}{N}\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}\frac{R^{(ij)}A}{\sqrt{N}}}_{:=\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{1}{1+N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(ij)}\mathbf{g}_{i}}}_{:=\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{(ij)}}\Bigg)}\Bigg]

Observe that
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{(ij)}\overline{\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{(ij)}}]=0
,

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Y}_{i}^{(ij)}\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{(ij)}}]=0
, and

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}\mathcal{Q}_{j}^{(ij)}\overline{\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{(ij)}}]=0
.
Assumption
2
and the a priori estimates
(
2.21
) imply that
|\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}|,|\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}|\prec\sqrt{N}\Phi^{2}
and
|\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{(ij)}|,|\mathcal{Q}_{j}^{(ij)}|\prec 1
. Hence

\mathbb{E}|\Delta(A)|^{2}\prec N^{2}\Phi^{4}
, and extending this to a high
moment estimate shows
|\Delta(A)|\prec N\Phi^{2}
as desired. This fluctuation averaging idea was used in
[
86
]
in the same context of a
non-separable sample covariance matrix model as our current work, to analyze outlier eigenvalues/eigenvectors in the setting of a spiked covariance
\Sigma
.

Showing an optimal anisotropic local law for the resolvent
R(z)
will instead
require a stronger estimate that is analogous to a bound of the form

|\Delta(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{*})|\prec\Phi
for
a rank-one matrix input
A=\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{*}
, assuming the a priori conditions
in place of (
2.21
),

(2.23)

N^{-1/2}\|R^{(S)}\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\prec\Phi\text{ over all deterministic unit vectors }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\quad|\widetilde{m}-\widetilde{m}_{0}|\ll 1.

Applying the same expansion for
\mathbb{E}|\Delta(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{*})|^{2}
, we may readily check as
above that

(2.24)

\mathbb{E}|\Delta(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{*})|^{2}=O_{\prec}(\Phi^{2})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}\mathcal{Q}_{j}^{(ij)}\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij}\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{(ij)}}]

where now
|\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}|,|\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}|\prec\Phi^{2}

and
|\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{(ij)}|,|\mathcal{Q}_{j}^{(ij)}|\prec 1
under
(
2.23
). However, the primary challenge is that this only
gives a bound of
\mathbb{E}|\Delta(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{*})|^{2}\prec N\Phi^{4}
, leading to

(2.25)

|\Delta(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{*})|\prec\sqrt{N}\Phi^{2},

which does not imply the desired bound

|\Delta(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{*})|\prec\Phi
when
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
is a local spectral parameter.
Overcoming this challenge was a central technical contribution of

[
54
]
,
who proposed instead a method of bootstrapping on the spectral scale in
multiplicative increments of
N^{-\delta}
in the imaginary part, and
using the additional a priori estimate

(2.26)

|\mathbf{x}^{*}R^{(S)}\mathbf{y}|\prec N^{2\delta}\text{ over all deterministic unit
vectors }\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}

obtained from the preceding bootstrap iteration to establish the bound

(2.27)

|\mathbf{x}^{*}R^{(S)}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{y}|\prec N^{C\delta}\Phi.

This then implies also
|\mathbf{x}^{*}R^{(S)}\mathbf{y}|\prec 1
, which allows one to
continue the bootstrap. The argument in
[
54
]
for showing
(
2.27
) from (
2.26
) was specific to a separable
model
\mathbf{g}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{w}
where
\mathbf{w}
has independent entries, and relied
on a technically intricate interpolation between
\mathbf{w}
and a Gaussian vector

\mathbf{z}\sim N(0,I)
.

In this work, we employ the same bootstrapping structure of

[
54
]
, but
pursue a more general proof by directly establishing the desired fluctuation averaging bound

(2.28)

|\Delta(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{*})|\prec N^{2\delta}\Phi

under the a priori assumptions
(
2.23
) and (
2.26
), without
interpolation. We note that the above estimates

|\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}|,|\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}|\prec\Phi^{2}

and
|\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{(ij)}|,|\mathcal{Q}_{j}^{(ij)}|\prec 1
leading to (
2.25
)
are sharp, and that this improved bound (
2.28
) will instead arise from verifying that under the cumulant condition of
Assumption
3
, the expectation of

\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}\mathcal{Q}_{j}^{(ij)}\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{(ij)}}

is much smaller than its typical size.
We will show this through a power series expansion of

\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{(ij)}
together with a moment-cumulant expansion of

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}\mathcal{Q}_{j}^{(ij)}\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{(ij)}}
.

To illustrate this calculation, consider the simplest term

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}}
.
Recalling the forms of
\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}
with

A=\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{*}
, we observe that
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{Z}_{ijj}^{(ij)}\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{jii}^{(ij)}}

depends on the 4th-order moment tensors
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{\otimes 4}
and

\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}_{j}^{\otimes 4}
, and one term in the resulting moment-cumulant expansion takes the form

\displaystyle\text{val}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{4},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{4}=1}^{n}\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{g}_{i})[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{4}]\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{g}_{j})[\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{4}]

(2.29)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\times\overline{\mathbf{u}}[\alpha_{1}]\frac{\overline{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}}{\sqrt{N}}[\alpha_{2}]\mathbf{u}[\beta_{1}]\frac{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{N}}[\beta_{2}]\frac{R^{(ij)}}{N}[\alpha_{3},\beta_{3}]\frac{\overline{R^{(ij)}}}{N}[\alpha_{4},\beta_{4}].

We observe that
val
takes the form
\text{val}=\langle\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{g}_{i}),\,\overline{\mathbf{u}}\otimes\frac{\overline{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}}{\sqrt{N}}\otimes T_{i}\rangle
, where
T_{i}\in(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes 2}
is the order-2 tensor encoding the remaining contraction involving
\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{g}_{j})
.
Applying Assumption
3
with
k=4
and
m=2
gives

(2.30)

|\text{val}|\prec(\sqrt{N})^{4-2-1}\,\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\left\|\frac{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{N}}\right\|_{2}\,\|T_{i}\|_{\mathcal{U}_{4}}.

To bound the
\mathcal{U}_{4}
-norm, we expand
\|T_{i}\|_{\mathcal{U}_{4}}=\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{U}_{4}}|\langle\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y},T_{i}\rangle|
.
For each fixed
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{U}_{4}
,

\langle\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y},T_{i}\rangle=\Big\langle\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{g}_{j}),\,\mathbf{u}\otimes\tfrac{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{N}}\otimes\underbrace{\tfrac{R^{(ij)*}\mathbf{x}}{N}\otimes\tfrac{\overline{R^{(ij)}}^{*}\mathbf{y}}{N}}_{=:\,T_{j}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}\Big\rangle.

A second application of Assumption
3
with
k=4
and
m=2
then gives

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y},T_{i}\rangle|

\displaystyle\prec(\sqrt{N})^{4-2-1}\,\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\left\|\frac{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{N}}\right\|_{2}\,\|T_{j}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\|_{\mathcal{U}_{4}}

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{N})^{4-2-1}\,\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\left\|\frac{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{N}}\right\|_{2}\left(\sup_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{U}_{4}}\left|\frac{\mathbf{v}^{*}R^{(ij)}\mathbf{w}}{N}\right|\right)^{2}.

Since
|\mathcal{U}_{4}|\leq n^{C_{4}}
has polynomial cardinality, combining with (
2.30
) and applying the a priori conditions (
2.23
)
and (
2.26
) shows that
|\text{val}|=O_{\prec}(N^{-1+4\delta}\Phi^{2})
.

For general terms in the high moment expansion, to manage the algebraic complexity, we will introduce a tensor network formalism and systematically map the moment expansions to a
family of tensor networks. Note that the example above has the following diagrammatic representation:

\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{g}_{i})
\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{g}_{j})
\frac{R^{(ij)}}{N}
\frac{\overline{R^{(ij)}}}{N}
\overline{\mathbf{u}}
\frac{\overline{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}}{\sqrt{N}}
\mathbf{u}
\frac{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{N}}
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{3}
\beta_{3}
\alpha_{4}
\beta_{4}
\beta_{1}
\beta_{2}
Applying
Assumption
3
on
\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{g}_{i})
\kappa_{4}(\mathbf{g}_{j})
\frac{R^{(ij)}}{N}
\frac{\overline{R^{(ij)}}}{N}
\mathbf{x}
\mathbf{y}
\mathbf{u}
\frac{R^{(ij)}\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{N}}
\alpha_{3}
\alpha_{4}
\beta_{3}
\beta_{4}
\beta_{1}
\beta_{2}

As illustrated in this example, we employ a recursive “peeling” strategy: we successively
remove high-degree vertices (representing higher-order cumulants) from the graph,
using Assumption
3
to control the cost of each removal,
until the network is reduced to simple chains and cycles that can be bounded
directly (see Lemmas
3.20
–
3.24
).

Finally, we emphasize the dual role of our tensor network framework. Beyond driving the proofs of our main results, it provides the essential machinery for verifying Assumption
3
in complex nonlinear settings. While verification is relatively straightforward for linear or sign-invariant models (Propositions
2.12
and
2.13
), the random features model (Proposition
2.17
) presents significant combinatorial challenges. In Section
5.3
, we address these by coupling a monomial expansion of
\mathbf{g}=\sigma(X\mathbf{w})
with a tensor network representation of the cumulant form
\langle\kappa(\mathbf{g}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle
. This allows us to systematically bound the resulting diagrams and rigorously establish the required cumulant decay.

3.
Fluctuation averaging lemmas

The primary technical input for our main results consists of a sequence of
fluctuation averaging lemmas
that resolve the weak dependence of the
resolvent
R(z)
on each individual sample
\mathbf{g}_{j}
for
j\neq i
.
