m_{1},\ldots,m_{2p}
and
x_{1}>x_{2}\geq x_{3}>\ldots>x_{2p}\geq 0

be as defined in (
2.8
). Note that if
a>x_{1}
(
a
is above the largest edge), then
N(a)=0
,
and (
4.6
) together with

\|\widetilde{K}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq N^{-1}\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}
and the high-probability
bound
\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C\sqrt{N}

[
82
, Corollary 5.35]

shows
\widehat{N}(a)=N(a)=0
with
probability
1-C(\delta,D)N^{-D}
.

If
a\in(x_{2k+1},x_{2k})
for some
k\in\{1,\ldots,p-1\}
(
a
is
between two separated bulk components), then
[
54
, Lemma A.1]
shows that

N(a)=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\sigma_{\alpha}\neq 0,\,{-}\sigma_{\alpha}^{-1}\in(m_{2k},0)\}
, i.e.
N(a)
counts the number of non-zero poles of
z_{0}(m)
to
the right of
m_{2k}
with multiplicity. Let
\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
where

\Sigma_{1}=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{N(a)})
contains the eigenvalues satisfying
{-}\sigma_{\alpha}^{-1}\in(m_{2k},0)
, and

\Sigma_{2}=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_{N(a)+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})
contains the rest.
Define, for
t\in[0,1]
,

\Sigma(t)=\operatorname{diag}\big((1-t)\Sigma_{1}+t\sigma_{1}I,(1-t)\Sigma_{2}\big)=:\operatorname{diag}\big(\sigma_{1}(t),\ldots,\sigma_{n}(t)\big),

which interpolates between
\Sigma(0)=\Sigma
and

\Sigma(1)=\operatorname{diag}(\underbrace{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{1}}_{N(a)},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{N-N(a)})
, and let

\widetilde{K}(t)=N^{-1}X^{*}\Sigma(t)X\text{ with eigenvalues }\lambda_{1}(t),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(t).

Observe that

\frac{d}{d\sigma_{\alpha}}z_{0}^{\prime}(m)={-}\frac{1}{N}\frac{d}{d\sigma_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{(\sigma_{\alpha}^{-1}+m)^{2}}

which is positive when
{-}\sigma_{\alpha}^{-1}>m
and negative when

{-}\sigma_{\alpha}^{-1}<m
. Then, writing
z_{0}(m;t)
and
z_{0}^{\prime}(m;t)
for

z_{0}(m)
and
z_{0}^{\prime}(m)
defined by
\Sigma(t)
,
we see that
z_{0}^{\prime}(m;t)
is increasing in
t
for each

m\in(m_{2k+1},m_{2k})
. This implies that there remain two distinct
critical points of
z_{0}(m;t)
between the poles
-\sigma_{N(a)}(t)^{-1}

and
-\sigma_{N(a)+1}(t)^{-1}
for all
t\in(0,1)
. Writing

m_{2k+1}(t)<m_{2k}(t)
for these two critical points, we have

\frac{d}{dt}\Big[z_{0}(m_{2k}(t);t)-z_{0}(m_{2k+1}(t);t)\Big]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{1}{(1+\sigma_{\alpha}(t)m_{2k}(t))^{2}}-\frac{1}{(1+\sigma_{\alpha}(t)m_{2k+1}(t))^{2}}\bigg)\frac{d}{dt}\sigma_{\alpha}(t).

Since

\frac{1}{(1+\sigma_{\alpha}(t)m_{2k}(t))^{2}}-\frac{1}{(1+\sigma_{\alpha}(t)m_{2k+1}(t))^{2}}>0

and
\frac{d}{dt}\sigma_{\alpha}(t)>0
for
\alpha=1,\ldots,N(a)
, and similarly
both are negative for
\alpha=N(a)+1\ldots,n
, we have

\frac{d}{dt}[z_{0}(m_{2k}(t);t)-z_{0}(m_{2k+1}(t);t)]>0
. Then

(4.7)

z_{0}(m_{2k}(t);t)-z_{0}(m_{2k+1}(t);t)>z_{0}(m_{2k}(0);0)-z_{0}(m_{2k+1}(0);0)>2\delta

for all
t\in(0,1)
, by the starting assumption that

(a-\delta,a+\delta)\cap\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0})=\emptyset
.
Note that
\Sigma(t)
satisfies Assumption
1
for all
t\in[0,1-c]
and any constant
c>0
. Then (
4.6
) holds
for each matrix
\widetilde{K}(t)
and
t\in[0,1-c]
. Choosing
c\equiv c(\delta)>0
sufficiently small, the operator norm bound
\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C\sqrt{N}

and a standard covering
argument show that with probability
1-CN^{-D}
,
(
4.6
) holds simultaneously for all
\{\widetilde{K}(t):t\in[0,1]\}
. On this event, (
4.6
) and
(
4.7
) imply

(4.8)

\widehat{N}(a)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}\Big\{\lambda_{i}(0)\geq z_{0}(m_{2k}(0);0)-\delta\Big\}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}\Big\{\lambda_{i}(1)\geq z_{0}(m_{2k}(1);1)-\delta\Big\}

where
m_{2k}(1)=\lim_{t\to 1}m_{2k}(t)
.
At
t=1
, denoting
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{N(a)\times N}
as the
first
N(a)
rows of
X
, we have

\widetilde{K}(1)=\sigma_{1}\cdot N^{-1}Y^{*}Y
,
and
z_{0}(m_{2k}(1);1)
is the lower edge of the
single bulk component of the standard Marchenko-Pastur law, given by

z_{0}(m_{2k}(1);1)=\sigma_{1}(1-\sqrt{N(a)/N})^{2}
. Then
(
4.7
) implies that
N(a)\leq(1-c(\delta))N
for some constant
c(\delta)>0
, and by concentration of the
smallest singular value of
Y

[
82
, Corollary 5.35]
, the right side of
(
4.8
) is exactly equal to
N(a)
with probability

1-CN^{-D}
.

Finally, if
a\in(0,x_{2p})
(
a
is below the smallest left edge), then
[
54
, Lemma A.1]
shows
N(a)=\min(N,\bar{n})
where we denote
\bar{n}=\operatorname{rank}(\Sigma)
. Write
\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\Sigma_{1},0)
where
\Sigma_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{\bar{n}\times\bar{n}}
, and consider the interpolation

\Sigma(t)=\operatorname{diag}\big((1-t)\Sigma_{1}+t\sigma_{1}I,0\big).

For the (unique) critical point

m_{2p}(t)\in(-\infty,-\sigma_{\bar{n}}(t)^{-1})\cup(0,\infty]
, the above argument establishes
\frac{d}{dt}z_{0}(m_{2p}(t);t)>0
and hence

(4.9)

z_{0}(m_{2p}(t);t)>z_{0}(m_{2k}(0);0)>\delta.

Hence, on the event where (
4.6
) holds for all
\{\widetilde{K}(t):t\in[0,1]\}
, we have that (
4.8
) holds with
k=p
. Here
\widetilde{K}(1)=\sigma_{1}\cdot N^{-1}Y^{*}Y
where
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{\bar{n}\times N}
contains the first
\bar{n}
rows of
X
,
z_{0}(m_{2p}(1);1)=\sigma_{1}(1-\sqrt{\bar{n}/N})^{2}
, and (
4.9
) implies that
\bar{n}\notin(1\pm c(\delta))N
for some
c(\delta)>0
. Then again, the right side of (
4.8
) is equal to
N(a)=\min(N,\bar{n})
with probability
1-CN^{-D}
. This shows
\widehat{N}(a)=N(a)
in all cases.

To extend this to the general non-Gaussian setting, let

\widetilde{K}=N^{-1}G^{*}G
where
G
satisfies Assumptions
1
and

2
, let
\widetilde{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times N}
have columns

\{\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
that are i.i.d.
N(0,\Sigma)
and independent
from
G
, and consider the interpolation for
t\in\{0,1/N,2/N,\ldots,1\}

G(t)=\sqrt{t}G+\sqrt{1-t}\,\widetilde{G},\qquad\widetilde{K}(t)=N^{-1}G(t)^{*}G(t).

Then
G(t)
satisfies
Assumption
2
uniformly over all such
t
, so
(
4.6
) holds with probability

1-CN^{-D}
for each such matrix
\widetilde{K}(t)
. It is easy to check that
for a constant
C>0
and each
l=1,\ldots,N
,

\|\widetilde{K}(l/N)-\widetilde{K}((l-1)/N)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq CN^{-1/2}\max_{0\leq s\leq N}\|\widetilde{K}(s/N)\|_{\mathrm{op}},

and we have
\|\widetilde{K}(s/N)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq N^{\varepsilon}
for any

\varepsilon>0
and each
s=0,1,\ldots,N
with probability
1-C(\varepsilon,D)N^{-D}
by
Lemma
3.8
(b). Then on the intersection of these events,
(
4.5
) for the Gaussian matrix

\widetilde{K}(1)
and (
4.6
) for each matrix

\widetilde{K}(l/n)
implies (
4.5
) for the original matrix

\widetilde{K}=\widetilde{K}(0)
, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
2.7
.

For (a), by (
4.6
)
and the implication of Lemma
4.9

that
\widehat{N}(a)=N(a)=0
for
a>\max(x\in\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0}))+\delta
,
we know that with probability
1-C(\delta,D)N^{-D}
,

K
and
\widetilde{K}
have no eigenvalues outside
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\operatorname{dist}(x,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\cup\{0\}))\leq\delta\}
. If there is a
constant
\delta>0
for which
\operatorname{dist}(0,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0}))>\delta
, then
applying Lemma
4.9
with
a=\delta/2
shows

\widehat{N}(a)=N(a)=N
, and thus
\widetilde{K}
also has no eigenvalues in

[0,\delta/2]
. Similarly, if
\operatorname{dist}(0,\operatorname{supp}(\mu_{0}))>\delta
, then

N\geq n
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}=(n/N)\mu_{0}+(1-n/N)\delta_{0}
, so
applying Lemma
4.9
with
a=\delta/2
shows

\widehat{N}(a)=N(a)=n
. Then
K
has full rank with no eigenvalues
in
[0,\delta/2]
. This shows all statements of (a).

For (b), the averaged local law (
2.12
) applied for

z=E+i\eta\in\mathbf{D}_{j}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
with
\kappa=E-x_{2k-1}\geq N^{-2/3+\varepsilon}

and
\eta=N^{-1/2-\varepsilon/4}\kappa^{1/4}\geq N^{-2/3}
implies

|\widetilde{m}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)|\ll(N\eta)^{-1}
(see
e.g.
[
69
, Proof of (3.4), Step 1]
). Then again

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}\{\lambda_{i}\in[E-\eta,E+\eta]\leq 2N\eta\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}(z)\ll 1
, so
\widetilde{K}
has no eigenvalue in
[E-\eta,E+\eta]
.
Applying this over a lattice of such values
z\in\mathbf{D}_{j}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
shows
(b).

For (c), if
\mathbf{D}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:E\in[E_{1},E_{2}],\,\eta\in[N^{-1+\tau},1]\}

is a regular domain, then applying (
2.12
) for
sufficiently small
\tau\equiv\tau(\varepsilon)\in(0,1)
,
the argument of
[
69
, Proof of (3.4), Step 2]
implies
that for any
a,b\in[E_{1},E_{2}]
,

|(\widehat{N}(b)-\widehat{N}(a))-(N(b)-N(a))|\leq C(\log N)N^{\varepsilon}.

Thus, together with (
4.5
), this shows that if an edge
x_{j}

is such that
\mathbf{D}_{j}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
is regular, then with probability

1-CN^{-D}
for some
C\equiv C(\delta,\varepsilon,D)>0
,

|\widehat{N}(a)-N(a)|\leq C(\log N)N^{\varepsilon}

for each
a\in[x_{j}-\delta,x_{j}+\delta]
. Furthermore if
\mathbf{D}_{k}^{b}(\delta,\tau)

is regular and either
\mathbf{D}_{2k}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
or

\mathbf{D}_{2k-1}^{e}(\delta,\tau)
is regular, then this holds also
for each
a\in[x_{2k}+\delta,x_{2k-1}-\delta]
. Together with the square-root
decay of
\rho_{0}(x)
at regular edges, this implies part (c)
(see e.g.
[
69
, Proof of (3.6)]
).

For (d), writing the spectral decomposition
\widetilde{K}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i}\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{*}
,
for any
j\in[N]
, we have

\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{R}_{jj}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i}[j]|^{2}\eta/((\lambda_{i}-E)^{2}+\eta^{2})
. If
\mathbf{D}_{j}^{e}(\delta,\varepsilon)
is regular, then for each eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}\in[x_{j}-\delta,x_{j}+\delta]
, applying (
2.13
) at
z=\lambda_{i}+iN^{-1+\varepsilon}
shows

|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i}[j]|^{2}\leq N^{-1+\varepsilon}\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{R}_{jj}\prec N^{-1+\varepsilon}
. The same argument holds for a regular bulk domain
\mathbf{D}_{k}^{b}(\delta,\varepsilon)
, showing part (d).
∎

4.3.
Proof of anisotropic law (Theorems
2.8
)

We now prove Theorem
2.8

on anisotropic approximations for the linearized
resolvent
\Pi(z)
. Note that for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
,

\Pi(z)=\begin{bmatrix}-zI_{n}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}G\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}G^{*}&-I_{N}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}R(z)&\Pi_{o}(z)^{*}\\
\Pi_{o}(z)&z\widetilde{R}(z)\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{(n+N)\times(n+N)}.

Indexing
[N]
by Roman indices
i,j,\ldots
and
[n]
by Greek indices

\alpha,\beta,\ldots
, the
following resolvent identities hold by Schur complement:

(4.10)

(\Pi(z))_{ij}=z\widetilde{R}_{ij}(z),\quad(\Pi(z))_{\alpha\beta}=R_{\alpha\beta}(z),\quad(\Pi(z))_{i\alpha}=(\Pi_{o}(z))_{i\alpha}=z\widetilde{R}_{ii}(z)\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}(z)\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}.

We will first show an anisotropic local law for the upper-left block

R(z)
, and then use this to show the result for

z\widetilde{R}(z)
and
\Pi_{o}(z)
.

Fix two constants
C_{0}>0
and
\delta\in(0,\tau/2C_{0})
.
Following the same argument as in
[
54
]
,
we will bootstrap on the spectral scale in multiplicative increments of

N^{-\delta}
: For any
\eta\geq N^{-1+\tau}
, define

L\equiv L(\eta)=\max\{l\in\mathbb{N}:\eta N^{\delta(l-1)}<1\},\qquad\eta_{l}=\eta N^{\delta l}\text{ for }l=0,\dots,L-1,\qquad\eta_{L}=1.

Note that
L\leq\delta^{-1}+1
. The following lemma, applying the
fluctuation averaging result of Lemma

3.6
(a),
is the main input for the bootstrap argument.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
,
and
3
hold, and
\mathbf{D}
is a regular spectral domain.
Suppose there exists a deterministic function
\Phi:\mathbf{D}\to[N^{-1/2},N^{-\tau/2}]
and a constant
\delta>0

such that uniformly over all deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\frac{\|R\mathbf{x}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{N}}\prec\Phi,\qquad|\mathbf{x}^{*}R\mathbf{y}|\prec N^{2\delta}.

Then, uniformly over all deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and

z\in\mathbf{D}
,

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\prec\max(N^{2\delta}\Phi,\Psi)

where
\Psi
is the bound in Theorem
2.5
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-z-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|

\displaystyle\leq|\underbrace{\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-z-z\widetilde{m}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}}_{I}|+|\underbrace{\mathbf{u}^{*}(-z-z\widetilde{m}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-z-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}}_{II}|

For
II
, by the identity
A^{-1}-B^{-1}=A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}

\displaystyle|II|

\displaystyle=|z||\widetilde{m}-\widetilde{m}_{0}||\mathbf{u}^{*}(-z-z\widetilde{m}\Sigma)^{-1}\Sigma(-z-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|

\displaystyle\prec\Psi\|(-z-z\widetilde{m}\Sigma)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|(-z-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec\Psi,

where we applied Lemma
4.2
and Theorem
2.5
. For
I
, by Lemma

4.3
and Lemma
4.4
,
there exists a constant
l\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-z-z\widetilde{m}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|

\displaystyle\prec\max_{0\leq k\leq l}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\mathbf{u}^{*}\Sigma^{k}(I+\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-k-1}(\mathbf{g}_{i}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}-\Sigma)R^{(i)}\mathbf{u}}{1+N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}\mathbf{g}_{i}}\right|+\max(\Phi^{2},\Psi),

where we have used the given condition and Lemma
4.2

to bound the remainder in Lemma
4.4
as

N^{-1/2}\|R^{(i)}\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\prec N^{-1/2}\|R\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\prec\Phi

and similarly
N^{-1/2}\|\mathbf{u}^{*}\Sigma^{k+1}(I+\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-k-1}R^{(i)}\|_{2}\prec\Phi
.

Note that
\mathbf{u}^{*}\Sigma^{k}(I+\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-k-1}

is a deterministic vector bounded in
\ell_{2}
-norm, and the conditions of Lemma
3.6
are satisfied by the given assumptions
N^{-1/2}\|R\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\prec\Phi
,
|\mathbf{x}^{*}R\mathbf{y}|\prec N^{2\delta}
, and the conditions
\Gamma^{(S)}\prec\Psi
and
N^{-1}\|R^{(S)}\|_{1}\prec 1
in light of Lemma
4.2
and Theorem
2.5
. Thus
the first term is
O_{\prec}(N^{2\delta}\Phi)
by the
polarization identity and fluctuation averaging result of
Lemma
3.6
(a), concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, uniformly over all
z=E+i\eta\in\mathbf{D}
and all deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

|\mathbf{x}^{*}R(z)\mathbf{y}|\prec N^{2\delta}\sum_{l=1}^{L(\eta)}[\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{x}^{*}R(E+i\eta_{l})\mathbf{x}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{y}^{*}R(E+i\eta_{l})\mathbf{y}].

Proof.

Writing the spectral decomposition

K=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\lambda_{\alpha}\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}^{*}
,

|\mathbf{x}^{*}R(z)\mathbf{y}|\leq\sum_{\alpha}\frac{|\mathbf{x}^{*}\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}||\mathbf{y}^{*}\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}|}{|\lambda_{\alpha}-z|}\leq\sum_{\alpha}\frac{|\mathbf{x}^{*}\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}|^{2}}{|\lambda_{\alpha}-z|}+\sum_{\alpha}\frac{|\mathbf{y}^{*}\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}|^{2}}{|\lambda_{\alpha}-z|}.

Define
\eta_{-1}=0
,
\eta_{L+1}=\infty
, and the index sets

U_{l}=\{\alpha:\eta_{l-1}\leq|\lambda_{\alpha}-E|<\eta_{l}\}
for

l=0,\ldots,L+1
. Then the same argument as leading to (
4.1
),
using
\eta_{l}/\eta_{l-1}\leq N^{\delta}
in place of
\eta_{l}/\eta_{l-1}\leq 2
,
shows

\sum_{\alpha}\frac{|\mathbf{x}^{*}\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}|^{2}}{|\lambda_{\alpha}-z|}=\sum_{l=0}^{L+1}\sum_{\alpha\in U_{l}}\frac{|\mathbf{x}^{*}\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}|^{2}}{|\lambda_{\alpha}-z|}\prec N^{2\delta}\sum_{l=1}^{L(\eta)}\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{x}^{*}R(E+i\eta_{l})\mathbf{x},

and the analogous bound holds for
\mathbf{y}
.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
, and

3
hold, and
\mathbf{D}
is a regular spectral domain.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define the domain

\mathbf{D}_{k}=\{z\in\mathbf{D}:\operatorname{Im}z\in[N^{-k\delta},1]\}
.
Let
\Psi
be the bound in Theorem
2.5
.
Then the following holds:

(a)

Uniformly over all
z\in\mathbf{D}_{0}
and all deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\prec N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi,

(b)

For all
0\leq k\leq\delta^{-1}
, if uniformly over all
z\in\mathbf{D}_{k}
and all deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\prec N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi,

then uniformly over all
z\in\mathbf{D}_{k}
and all deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}\prec\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}+N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi.

(c)

For all
0\leq k\leq\delta^{-1}
, if uniformly over all
z\in\mathbf{D}_{k}
and all deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}\prec\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}+N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi,

then uniformly over all
z\in\mathbf{D}_{k+1}
and all deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\prec N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi.

Proof.

For (a), note that for
z\in\mathbf{D}_{0}
, we have
\eta=1
, so
\|R\mathbf{x}\|_{2}/\sqrt{N}\leq\|R\|_{\mathrm{op}}/\sqrt{N}\leq N^{-1/2}
and

|\mathbf{x}^{*}R\mathbf{y}|\leq\|R\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
for any unit vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. By Lemma
4.10
,

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\prec\frac{N^{2\delta}}{\sqrt{N}}=N^{2\delta-1/2}\leq N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi,

where the last bound holds for sufficiently large
C_{0}>0
and any
\delta\in(0,\tau/2C_{0})
. This proves (a).

For (b), note that uniformly over
z\in\mathbf{D}_{k}
, (a) implies

\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}\prec\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}+N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi.

Denoting
\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}
as the eigenvectors of
\Sigma
with eigenvalues
\sigma_{\alpha}
,

\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{\eta(1+\operatorname{Re}\widetilde{m}_{0})+E\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}}{|z|^{2}|1+\sigma_{\alpha}\widetilde{m}_{0}|^{2}}|\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}^{*}\mathbf{u}|^{2}\leq C\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0},

since, over a regular domain
\mathbf{D}
, we have
\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}\gtrsim\eta
,

|z|\asymp 1
,
|\operatorname{Re}\widetilde{m}_{0}|\leq|\widetilde{m}_{0}|\lesssim 1
, and

\min_{\alpha}|1+\sigma_{\alpha}\widetilde{m}_{0}|\gtrsim 1
. This proves (b).

For (c), let
z=E+i\eta\in\mathbf{D}_{k+1}
, so
\eta\geq N^{-(k+1)\delta}
.
By construction,
E+i\eta_{l}\in\mathbf{D}_{k}
for
l=1,\dots,L(\eta)
, since

\eta_{l}=\eta N^{\delta l}\geq N^{-(k+1)\delta}N^{\delta}=N^{-k\delta}
.
The assumption implies uniformly over all such
z=E+i\eta\in\mathbf{D}_{k+1}
and
unit vectors
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{x}^{*}R(E+i\eta_{l})\mathbf{x}\prec\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(E+i\eta_{l})+N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi(E+i\eta_{l})\prec 1,

since
\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(E+i\eta_{l})\leq C
,
\Psi(E+i\eta_{l})\leq CN^{-\tau/2}
for any
\eta_{l}\geq N^{-1+\tau}
, and
\delta\in(0,\tau/2C_{0})
.
Then by Lemma
4.11
, uniformly over
unit vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
|\mathbf{x}^{*}R(z)\mathbf{y}|\prec N^{2\delta}
.
Since the function
\eta\mapsto\eta\operatorname{Im}s(z)
is nondecreasing and
\eta\mapsto\operatorname{Im}s(z)/\eta
is nonincreasing for any Stieltjes transform
s(z)
,
letting
z_{1}=E+iN^{\delta}\eta\in\mathbf{D}_{m}
, we have

\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{u}^{*}R(z)\mathbf{u}\leq\frac{N^{\delta}\eta}{\eta}\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{u}^{*}R(z_{1})\mathbf{u}\prec N^{\delta}[\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z_{1})+N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi(z_{1})]\leq N^{2\delta}[\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)+N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi(z)].

Thus,

\frac{\|R\mathbf{u}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{N}}=\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}}{N\eta}}\prec N^{\delta}\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}+N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi}{N\eta}}\leq N^{\delta}(1+N^{C_{0}\delta/2})\Psi,

the last inequality using
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
and
\sqrt{\operatorname{Im}\tilde{m}_{0}(z)/N\eta}\leq\Psi(z)
.
Noting that
N^{\delta}(1+N^{C_{0}\delta/2})\leq 2N^{(C_{0}/2+1)\delta}

and setting
\Phi=N^{(C_{0}/2+1)\delta}\Psi
, Lemma

4.10
then shows that

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\prec N^{2\delta}\Phi=N^{(C_{0}/2+3)\delta}\Psi\leq N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi,

the last inequality holding for
C_{0}>6
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

Lemma
4.12
implies, uniformly over
z\in\mathbf{D}
and all deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\prec N^{C_{0}\delta}\Psi.

Since the constant
\delta\in(0,\tau/2C_{0})
here is arbitrarily small, this implies

(4.11)

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\prec\Psi.

Taking
\delta>0
arbitrarily small within the arguments
of Lemma
4.12
shows also

\frac{\|R\mathbf{x}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{N}}\prec\Psi,\qquad|\mathbf{x}^{*}R\mathbf{y}|\prec 1

uniformly over unit vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
z\in\mathbf{D}
.
Then by the resolvent identity (
3.2
),
uniformly over unit vectors
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{*}\widetilde{R}\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{m}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\mathbf{v}(i)|^{2}(\widetilde{R}_{ii}-\widetilde{m}_{0})+\sum_{i\neq j}\bar{\mathbf{v}}(i)\mathbf{v}(j)\widetilde{R}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{m}_{0}+O_{\prec}(\Psi)+z\sum_{i\neq j}\bar{\mathbf{v}}(i)\mathbf{v}(j)\widetilde{R}_{ii}\widetilde{R}_{jj}^{(i)}N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(ij)}\mathbf{g}_{j}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{m}_{0}+O_{\prec}(\Psi)+z^{-1}\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{\bar{\mathbf{v}}(i)\mathbf{v}(j)N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(ij)}\mathbf{g}_{j}}{(1+N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}\mathbf{g}_{i})(1+N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{j}^{*}R^{(ij)}\mathbf{g}_{j})}.

Then by the fluctuation averaging result of
Lemma
3.6
(b) applied with
\delta=0
and
\Phi\asymp\Psi
,

(4.12)

|\mathbf{v}^{*}\widetilde{R}\mathbf{v}-\widetilde{m}_{0}|\prec\Psi.

Similarly, by the identity (
4.10
),

\mathbf{v}^{*}\Pi_{o}\mathbf{u}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{\mathbf{v}}(i)N^{-1/2}z\widetilde{R}_{ii}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}\mathbf{u}=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\bar{\mathbf{v}}(i)N^{-1/2}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}\mathbf{u}}{1+N^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{i}^{*}R^{(i)}\mathbf{g}_{i}},

so by the fluctuation averaging result of
Lemma
3.6
(c) applied with
\delta=0
and
\Phi\asymp\Psi
,

(4.13)

|\mathbf{v}^{*}\Pi_{o}\mathbf{u}|\prec\Psi.

The theorem follows from (
4.11
), (
4.12
),
(
4.13
) and the polarization identity.
∎

Proof of Corollary
2.9
.

Writing

K=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\lambda_{\alpha}\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}^{*}
and
\widetilde{K}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i}\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{*}
, for any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
we have

\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}\rangle^{2}\eta/((\lambda_{\alpha}-E)^{2}+\eta^{2})
and

\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{v}^{*}\widetilde{R}\mathbf{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\mathbf{v},\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i}\rangle^{2}\eta/((\lambda_{i}-E)^{2}+\eta^{2})
. Thus the result follows from Theorem
2.8
by the same argument as Corollary
2.7
(d).
∎

4.4.
Proof outside the spectrum (Theorem
2.10
)

Proof of Theorem
2.10
.

Fixing
\eta\in(0,1)
and
\delta>0
,
we first show the result over the (regular) spectral domain

\mathbf{D}^{o}\equiv\mathbf{D}^{o}(\delta,\eta)
defined in (
2.9
).

(
4.6
) shows that

K
has no eigenvalues in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|x|\leq 2\delta^{-1},\,\operatorname{dist}(x,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\cup\{0\}))\geq\delta/2)\}
, with high probability. In light of Lemma
4.2
, Theorem
2.5
, and the bound
\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{m}_{0}\lesssim\eta
for
z\in\mathbf{D}^{o}
, we have
|\widetilde{m}^{(S)}-\widetilde{m}|\prec\Psi_{\Theta}^{2}\prec N^{-1}+(N\eta)^{-2}
. Then the argument for (
4.6
) may be applied equally with
\widetilde{m}^{(S)}
in place of
\widetilde{m}
, to show
that for all
S\subset[N]
with
|S|\leq L
a fixed constant, also
K^{(S)}
has no eigenvalues in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|x|\leq 2\delta^{-1},\,\operatorname{dist}(x,\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\cup\{0\}))\geq\delta/2)\}
with high probability. Thus

\|R^{(S)}(z)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec 1
uniformly over
z\in\mathbf{D}^{o}
and such subsets
S\subset[N]
.

This implies
N^{-1}\|R^{(S)}(z)\|_{F}\leq N^{-1/2}\|R^{(S)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec N^{-1/2}
, so Lemmas
4.2
,

4.4
, and
4.5
all
hold with
N^{-1/2}
in place of
\Psi_{\Theta}
.
Applying Lemma
3.4
with
\Phi=N^{-1/2}
shows,
in place of (
4.4
), that
|z_{0}(\widetilde{m})-z|\prec N^{-1}
.
Then regularity of
\mathbf{D}^{o}
and the observation
\kappa\asymp 1
for

z\in\mathbf{D}^{o}
implies that

|\widetilde{m}-\widetilde{m}_{0}|\prec\frac{N^{-1}}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+N^{-1/2}}\prec N^{-1},

and hence also
|m-m_{0}|\prec N^{-1}
.

We have also uniformly over unit vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

N^{-1/2}\|R^{(S)}\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq N^{-1/2}\|R^{(S)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec N^{-1/2}

and
|\mathbf{x}^{*}R^{(S)}\mathbf{y}|\leq\|R^{(S)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec 1
. Then applying Lemma

3.5
with
\Phi=N^{-1/2}
in place of
Lemma
3.6
in the arguments of
Lemma
4.10
,
(
4.12
), and (
4.13
) shows

|\mathbf{u}^{*}R\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{*}(-zI-z\widetilde{m}_{0}\Sigma)^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\prec N^{-1/2},\quad|\mathbf{v}^{*}\widetilde{R}\mathbf{v}-\widetilde{m}_{0}|\prec N^{-1/2},\quad|\mathbf{v}^{*}\Pi_{o}\mathbf{u}|\prec N^{-1/2}.

By the polarization identity,
this implies all statements of the theorem for
z\in\mathbf{D}^{o}
.

To extend these estimates to
\bar{\mathbf{D}}^{o}(\delta)
, first note that by the conjugate symmetry
\overline{\widetilde{m}(\bar{z})}=\widetilde{m}(z)
and
\overline{\Pi(\bar{z})}=\Pi(z)
it suffices to extend to the domain
\bar{\mathbf{D}}^{o}(\delta)\cap\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}
. Next, observe that
(
4.6
) implies also, on an event of high
probability,
R(z)
and
\widetilde{R}(z)
are
2/\delta
-Lipschitz in the
operator norm over
\bar{\mathbf{D}}^{o}(\delta)
. Applying this Lipschitz continuity and
the results for
\mathbf{D}^{o}(\delta,\eta)
shows that all statements of
Theorem
2.10
hold over
\bar{\mathbf{D}}^{o}(\delta)\cap\overline{\mathbb{C}^{+}}
with an additional additive error of

(2/\delta)N^{-1+\tau}
. Since
\tau>0
is arbitrary, this implies the theorem.
∎

5.
Analysis of examples

5.1.
Separable distributions

Proof of Proposition
2.12
.

Suppose first that
d=n
,
X=I
, and
\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{w}
. Then Assumption

2
holds by a standard high moment estimate, see e.g.
[
25
, Lemma 3.1]
.
For Assumption
3
, set
\mathcal{U}=\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\}
, so

\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}=\|T\|_{\infty}
.
Since
\mathbf{w}
has independent entries,
\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}

is diagonal. Then for any
1\leq m\leq k-1
,
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and

T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
,

|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|\leq\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha])||T[\alpha,\ldots,\alpha]|\prod_{t=1}^{m}|\mathbf{s}_{t}[\alpha]|\leq C\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\prod_{t=1}^{m}|\mathbf{s}_{t}[\alpha]|,

where the last inequality holds for some
C\equiv C(k)>0
by the moment bounds
for
\mathbf{w}[\alpha]
. We may bound
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}|\mathbf{s}_{1}[\alpha]|\leq\sqrt{n}\|\mathbf{s}_{1}\|_{2}
for
m=1
, and

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\prod_{t=1}^{m}|\mathbf{s}_{t}[\alpha]|\leq\prod_{t=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{t}\|_{2}

for
m\geq 2
, showing Assumption
3
in the form of
(
2.16
), which is stronger since
k\geq 3
and
m\leq k-1
.

For general
d\asymp n
and
X\neq I
,
since
\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{1/2}\leq C
for
a constant
C>0
, it is clear that Assumption
2
holds
for
\mathbf{g}
since it holds for
\mathbf{w}
. For any

1\leq m\leq k-1
,
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and

T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
, letting

\mathbf{s}_{t}^{\prime}=X^{*}\mathbf{s}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
T^{\prime}=(X^{*})^{\otimes(k-m)}\cdot T\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes k-m}
(denoting the multiplication of
T
along each axis
by
X^{*}
), it follows from multilinearity of the cumulants that

\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}_{i}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle=\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{i}),\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime}\rangle.

Setting
\mathcal{U}=\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n},X\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,X\mathbf{e}_{d}\}
, we have

\|\mathbf{s}_{t}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathbf{s}_{t}\|_{2}
and
\|T^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}
,
so (
2.16
) follows from the above result for
X=I
.
∎

5.2.
Conditionally mean-zero distributions

We show Proposition
2.13
.

Lemma 5.1
.

In the setting of Proposition
2.13
, for any fixed
k\geq 1
, uniformly over
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in[d]
, we have

(5.1)

|\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])|\prec 1

and

(5.2)

\sum_{\beta=1}^{d}\kappa_{k+2}(\mathbf{w}[\beta],\mathbf{w}[\beta],\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])^{2}\prec 1.

Proof.

Applying Assumption
2
for
\mathbf{w}

with
A=\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}^{*}
shows
|\mathbf{w}[\alpha]|\prec 1
. Together with the
moment bound for
\mathbf{w}
in Assumption
2
, this implies
for any fixed integer
k\geq 1
that
\mathbb{E}|\mathbf{w}[\alpha]|^{k}\prec 1
.
The first claim
|\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])|\prec 1
then
follows from the moment-cumulant relations.

For the second claim, let
S=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\}
.
Let
P_{k+2}
denote the set of all partitions of
[k+2]
. For any

\beta\notin S
, denoting
\alpha_{k+1}=\alpha_{k+2}=\beta
, the
moment-cumulant relations give

\kappa_{k+2}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}],\mathbf{w}[\beta],\mathbf{w}[\beta])=\sum_{\pi\in P_{k+2}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{j\in B}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{j}]\Big]

Observe that if any block
B\in\pi
contains exactly one of the two indices

\{k+1,k+2\}
, then
\mathbb{E}\prod_{j\in B}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{j}]=0
by the given condition for
\mathbf{w}
. Thus, denoting by
\dot{P}_{k+2}
those
partitions which put
k+1,k+2
in the same block, we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{k+2}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}],\mathbf{w}[\beta],\mathbf{w}[\beta])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\dot{P}_{k+2}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{j\in B}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{j}]\Big]

\displaystyle=\kappa_{k+1}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}],\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{k+1}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}],\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2}-1).

Now let
P_{k+1}
be all partitions of
[k+1]
, and for all
\pi\in P_{k+1}
,
let
B^{*}\in\pi
denote the block containing the last element
k+1
. Then
we may further expand this as

\displaystyle\kappa_{k+1}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}],\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in P_{k+1}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\left(\prod_{B\in\pi\setminus B^{*}}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in B}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{j}]\right]\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\prod_{j\in B^{*}\setminus\{k+1\}}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{j}]\right)(\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2}-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\underbrace{\left(\sum_{\pi\in P_{k+1}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\left(\prod_{B\in\pi\setminus B^{*}}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in B}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{j}]\right]\right)\prod_{j\in B^{*}\setminus\{k+1\}}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{j}]\right)}_{:=u}(\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2}-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[u(\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2}-1)],

where
u\in\mathbb{R}
is a scalar random variable in the probability space of
\mathbf{w}
,
depending on
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}
, and satisfying
|u|\prec 1

uniformly over
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in[d]
.

Then, applying also the first claim (
5.1
) for

\beta\in S
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta=1}^{d}\kappa_{k+2}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}],\mathbf{w}[\beta],\mathbf{w}[\beta])^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\notin S}\kappa_{k+1}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}],\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2}-1)^{2}+O_{\prec}(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta=1}^{d}\Big(\mathbb{E}[u(\mathbf{w}(\beta)^{2}-1)]\Big)^{2}+O_{\prec}(1).

The proof is concluded by the observation

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta=1}^{d}\Big(\mathbb{E}[u(\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2}-1)]\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\bigg(\sum_{\beta=1}^{d}\mathbf{v}[\beta]\mathbb{E}[u(\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2}-1)]\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\mathbb{E}u^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sum_{\beta=1}^{d}\mathbf{v}[\beta](\mathbf{w}[\beta]^{2}-1)\bigg|^{2}\prec 1,

where the last inequality uses
|u|\prec 1
and Assumption

2
for
\mathbf{w}
with
A=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{v})
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.13
.

Suppose first that
d=n
,
X=I
, and
\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{w}
. Let

\mathcal{U}=\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{d}\}
.
Fix any
k\geq 3
,
m\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
,
unit vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
. Then

\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}=\|T\|_{\infty}
, and

\displaystyle|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}=1}^{n}|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])|\prod_{l=1}^{m}|\mathbf{v}_{l}[\alpha_{l}]|.

For any index tuple
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\in[n]^{k}
, write

\pi(\alpha)
for the partition of
[k]
such that
i,j
belong to the same
block of
\pi(\alpha)
if and only if
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
.
If any block of
\pi(\alpha)
has cardinality 1, then

|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])|=0
by
the given condition for
\mathbf{w}
.
Thus, letting
P_{k}^{e}
be the set of partitions of
[k]

where each block has cardinality at least 2,

|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|\leq\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}^{e}}\sum_{\alpha\in[n]^{k}:\pi(\alpha)=\pi}|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])|\prod_{l=1}^{m}|\mathbf{v}_{l}(\alpha_{l})|.

Now consider any
\pi\in P_{k}^{e}
. Note that

(5.3)

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}1=n,\quad\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}|\mathbf{v}_{l}[\alpha]|\leq\sqrt{n}\|\mathbf{v}_{l}\|_{2},\quad\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}|\mathbf{v}_{l_{1}}[\alpha]\ldots\mathbf{v}_{l_{p}}[\alpha]|\leq\|\mathbf{v}_{l_{1}}\|_{2}\ldots\|\mathbf{v}_{l_{p}}\|_{2}\text{ for }p\geq 2.

Let
K_{0}(\pi)
and
K_{1}(\pi)
be the numbers of blocks
B\in\pi
for which 0 and 1, respectively, of the first
m
indices

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}
belong to
B
. Then, applying
(
5.1
) and (
5.3
), we get

(5.4)

|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{v}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|\prec\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{v}_{l}\|_{2}\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}^{e}}\sqrt{n}^{2K_{0}(\pi)+K_{1}(\pi)}.

Note that if 0 (or 1) such indices
belong to
B
, then
B
has at least 2 (resp. 1) indices from

m+1,\ldots,k
, so
2K_{0}+K_{1}\leq k-m
. This establishes a weaker
version of (
2.1
), with exponent
k-m
in place of

k-m-1
.

To improve this exponent to
k-m-1
, consider any

\pi\in P_{k}^{e}
such that
2K_{0}(\pi)+K_{1}(\pi)=k-m
.
Each block of
\pi
counted by

K_{0}
must have exactly 2 indices in
m+1,\ldots,k
, and each block of
\pi

counted by
K_{1}
must have exactly 1 index in
m+1,\ldots,k
. If
K_{0}\geq 1
,
let
B^{*}\in\pi
be a block counted by
K_{0}
, and suppose for
notational convenience that
B^{*}=\{k-1,k\}
. Denote

\alpha_{[k-2]}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-2})
. Then we may apply
(
5.2
) to bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in[n]^{k}:\pi(\alpha)=\pi}|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])|\prod_{l=1}^{m}|\mathbf{v}_{l}[\alpha_{l}]|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha_{[k-2]}:\pi(\alpha_{[k-2]})=\pi\setminus B^{*}}\prod_{l=1}^{m}|\mathbf{v}_{l}[\alpha_{l}]|\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}|\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k-2}],\mathbf{w}[\beta],\mathbf{w}[\beta])|

\displaystyle\prec\sqrt{n}\sum_{\alpha_{[k-2]}:\pi(\alpha_{[k-2]})=\pi\setminus B^{*}}\prod_{l=1}^{m}|\mathbf{v}_{l}[\alpha_{l}]|\leq\sqrt{n}\cdot n^{K_{0}-1}\cdot n^{K_{1}/2}=\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}.

If
K_{0}=0
and
K_{1}=k-m
,
let
B^{*}\in\pi
be a block counted by
K_{1}
, and suppose for
notational convenience that
B^{*}=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{k}\}
. Denote in this case

\alpha_{[k-2]}=(\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})
. Then we may apply
(
5.2
) to bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in[n]^{k}:\pi(\alpha)=\pi}|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])|\prod_{l=1}^{m}|\mathbf{v}_{l}[\alpha_{l}]|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha_{[k-2]}:\pi(\alpha_{[k-2]})=\pi\setminus B^{*}}\prod_{l=2}^{m}|\mathbf{v}_{l}[\alpha_{l}]|\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}|\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\beta],\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{2}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k-1}],\mathbf{w}[\beta])||\mathbf{v}_{1}[\beta]|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha_{[k-2]}:\pi(\alpha_{[k-2]})=\pi\setminus B^{*}}\prod_{l=2}^{m}|\mathbf{v}_{l}[\alpha_{l}]|\left(\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\beta],\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{2}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k-1}],\mathbf{w}[\beta])^{2}\right)^{1/2}\|\mathbf{v}_{1}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\prec\sum_{\alpha_{[k-2]}:\pi(\alpha_{[k-2]})=\pi\setminus B^{*}}\prod_{l=2}^{m}|\mathbf{v}_{l}[\alpha_{l}]|\leq n^{(K_{1}-1)/2}=\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}.

Applying these cases to (
5.4
) for the partitions

\pi\in P_{k}^{e}
where
2K_{0}(\pi)+K_{1}(\pi)=k-m
, this shows
that Assumption
3
holds.

The argument to extend to general
d\asymp n
and
X\neq I
is the same as in
Proposition
2.12
.
∎

Verification of Example
2.16
.

We verify the claims of Example
2.16
.

We first check that the conditions of Proposition
2.13
for
\mathbf{w}

hold under (
2.17
)
and (
2.18
): The conditions
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{*}=I
and
\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{i}]=0
if
\alpha_{1}\notin\{\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\}
are immediate from (
2.17
). Furthermore, (
2.17
) implies that the conditional law
(\mathbf{w}\mid\lambda)
satisfies Assumption
2
uniformly over
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
To check Assumption
2
unconditionally, for any matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we may decompose

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{*}A\mathbf{w}-\operatorname{Tr}A

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{w}^{*}A\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}^{*}A\mathbf{w}\mid\lambda])+(\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}^{*}A\mathbf{w}\mid\lambda]-\operatorname{Tr}A)

(5.5)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{w}^{*}A\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}^{*}A\mathbf{w}\mid\lambda])+(\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}[\alpha]^{2}\mid\lambda]-1)\operatorname{Tr}A.

The first term is
O_{\prec}(\|A\|_{F})
by Assumption
2
conditional on
\lambda
, while the second is
O_{\prec}(\|A\|_{F})
by (
2.18
) and the bound
|\operatorname{Tr}A|\leq\sqrt{n}\|A\|_{F}
.

Next, for the specific example of (
2.19
), we note that
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}[\alpha]^{2}\mid\lambda]-1=c_{n}\lambda
. Then taking
A=I
in (
5.5
) shows that if
c_{n}\gg n^{-1/2}
, then Assumption
2
does not hold. In this case, we also have

\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{Tr}K]=N^{-1}\operatorname{Var}[\|\mathbf{w}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}]=N^{-1}\Big(\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}[\|\mathbf{w}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\mid\lambda]]+\operatorname{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{w}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\mid\lambda]]\Big)\asymp 1+Nc_{n}^{2}\gg 1.

Then Theorem
2.5
cannot hold, as the eigenvalue rigidity in Corollary
2.7
must instead imply
|\operatorname{Tr}K-\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}K|\prec 1
for concentration of the linear spectral statistic
\operatorname{Tr}K
.

Finally, let us check that for this model (
2.19
), Assumption
3
holds as long as
c_{n}\prec n^{-1/4}
: Since
\mathbf{w}
is sign-invariant, we have
\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})=0
for any odd
k
. It is therefore enough to consider the case where
k\geq 4
is even. Let
\gamma=1+c_{n}\lambda
,
denote
P_{k}
the set of all possible partitions of
[k]
, and let
P_{k}^{\prime}
be the set of all possible pairings of
[k]
. For any
T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}
, we have by the law of total cumulance

\displaystyle\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}),T\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])T[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}}\kappa(\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{b}]:b\in B\mid\gamma):B\in\pi)T[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}^{\prime}}\kappa_{k/2}(\gamma)\prod_{\{p,q\}\in\pi}\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{p}],\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{q}])T[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}]

\displaystyle=\kappa_{k/2}(\gamma)\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}T[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}]\prod_{\{p,q\}\in\pi}\mathbf{1}_{\alpha_{p}=\alpha_{q}}

where we used the fact that
(\mathbf{w}\mid\gamma)
has i.i.d.
N(0,\gamma)
entries, so
\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{b}]:b\in B\mid\gamma)=\gamma

if
B=\{p,q\}
where
\alpha_{p}=\alpha_{q}
,
and
\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{b}]:b\in B\mid\gamma)=0
otherwise.

Then, for any even
k\geq 4
,
1\leq m\leq k-1
,
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle

\displaystyle=\kappa_{k/2}(\gamma)\sum_{\pi\in P_{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}\mathbf{s}_{1}[\alpha_{1}]\cdots\mathbf{s}_{m}[\alpha_{m}]T[\alpha_{m+1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}]\prod_{\{p,q\}\in\pi}\mathbf{1}_{\alpha_{p}=\alpha_{q}}.

Note that

|\kappa_{k/2}(\gamma)|=c_{n}^{k/2}|\kappa_{k/2}(\lambda)|\prec c_{n}^{k/2}\prec n^{-1/2}
where the last bound holds for
c_{n}\prec n^{-1/4}
and
k\geq 4
.
Thus we can bound, similar to the proof of Proposition
2.13
,

\displaystyle|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|\prec n^{-1/2}\|T\|_{\infty}\max_{\pi\in P_{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}|\mathbf{s}_{1}[\alpha_{1}]|\cdots|\mathbf{s}_{m}[\alpha_{m}]|\prod_{\{p,q\}\in\pi}\mathbf{1}_{\alpha_{p}=\alpha_{q}}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-1/2}\|T\|_{\infty}n^{(k-m)/2}\|\mathbf{s}_{1}\|_{2}\cdots\|\mathbf{s}_{m}\|_{2}\prec n^{(k-m-1)/2}\|\mathbf{s}_{1}\|_{2}\cdots\|\mathbf{s}_{m}\|_{2}\|T\|_{\infty},

establishing Assumption
3
.
∎

5.3.
Random features model

We now show Proposition
2.17
.
To simplify notation, we will assume that

X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
have equal dimensions
n=d
.
This is without loss of generality, as if
n\neq d
, it
is equivalent to show Proposition
2.17
with
X
,
\mathbf{w}
, and/or

\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{m},T
extended by 0-padding to have all dimensions equal to

\max(n,d)
.

5.3.1.
Tensor networks and bipolar orientations

Definition 5.2
.

(Tensor network)
We say that a tuple
(G,f)
is a tensor network if the following holds:

(1)

G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
is a multigraph with no self-loops.
Each vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}
has an ordered list
\partial v\subseteq\mathcal{E}
of its incident edges, and each edge
e=(u,v)
has an ordering of its 2
incident vertices
u,v\in\mathcal{V}
. We denote by
\text{deg}(v)=|\partial v|
the
vertex degree, counting edge multiplicity.

(2)

f
is a labeling of
\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{E}
such that
f(v)\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(v)}
is a tensor of order
\text{deg}(v)

for each vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}
, and
f(e)=\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a
matrix for each edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
.

The contracted value of this tensor network is then given by

\mathrm{val}(G,f)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{v,e}\in[n]:\,v\in\mathcal{V},\,e\in\partial v\end{subarray}}\,\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}}f(v)[i_{v,e}:e\in\partial v]\prod_{e=(u,v)\in\mathcal{E}}f(e)[i_{u,e},i_{v,e}]

where the summation is over one index
i_{v,e}\in[n]
for each vertex-edge
pair
(v,e)
such that
v
is incident to
e
, and

[i_{v,e}:e\in\partial v]
denotes the ordered tuple of such indices for a
given vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}
.

Note that if one permutes the order of the edges
\partial v
incident to
v\in\mathcal{V}
, and permutes correspondingly the indices of the tensor label
f(v)
,
then
\text{val}(G,f)
remains unchanged. Similarly, if one
exchanges the vertex order of
e=(u,v)
and replaces
f(e)
by the
transpose
f(e)^{*}
, then
\text{val}(G,f)
also remains unchanged. If two tensor
networks
(G,f)
and
(G^{\prime},f^{\prime})
are equivalent under some such permutations of
their vertex/edge orderings and labels, we will consider
(G,f)
and
(G^{\prime},f^{\prime})

to be the same tensor network. Otherwise,
(G,f)
and
(G^{\prime},f^{\prime})
are distinct.
We will omit the specification of the ordering of
\partial v
or

e=(u,v)
if the tensor/matrix label
f(v)
or
f(e)
is symmetric.

Definition 5.3
.

(Bipolar orientation)
Let
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
be a multigraph with no self-loops.
G
is

biconnected
if it consists of a single connected component and
furthermore
G
is not broken into disconnected components by removing any
single vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}
together with its incident edges
\partial v
.

For two vertices
s,t\in\mathcal{V}
,
G
admits a
(s,t)
-bipolar orientation

if there is a choice of direction for each edge such that
G
has no
directed cycles,
s
is the unique source (vertex with no incoming edges),
and
t
is the unique sink (vertex with no outgoing edges).

We note that the existence of a
(s,t)
-bipolar orientation depends only on
which pairs of vertices are connected by an edge in
G
, and not on the edge
multiplicities. It is well-known that the following are equivalent (see e.g.
[
71
, Section 2]
):

(1)

G
admits a
(s,t)
-bipolar orientation.

(2)

G
admits a
(s,t)
-numbering, i.e. a numbering of its vertices

v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}
with
m=|\mathcal{V}|
such that
s=v_{1}
,
t=v_{m}
, and each
other vertex is adjacent to both a lower-numbered and a higher-numbered vertex.

(3)

The multigraph
G^{+}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}\cup(s,t))
adding the additional edge

(s,t)
is biconnected.

For any tensor
T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}
and any partition of
\{1,\ldots,k\}

into two disjoint subsets
I,J
, we denote by
\operatorname{mat}_{I,J}(T)\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{|I|}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n^{|J|}}
its matricization or flattening
with respect to this index partition, and by

\operatorname{vec}(T)\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{k}}
its vectorization. We define the norms

\|T\|_{F}=\|\operatorname{vec}(T)\|_{2},\qquad\|T\|_{I\to J}=\|\operatorname{mat}_{I,J}(T)\|_{\mathrm{op}},\qquad\|T\|_{{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}}=\mathop{\max_{I,J:I\sqcup J=[k]}}_{|I|\geq 1,|J|\geq 1}\|T\|_{I\to J}.

Thus
\|T\|_{{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}}
is the maximum of the matrix operator norm

\|T\|_{I\to J}
over all flattenings of
T
corresponding to all possible
partitions of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
into two non-empty index sets.

The following lemma is similar to the analyses of
[
63
]
,
and allows us to bound the value of any tensor network
with bipolar orientation.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(G,f)
be a tensor network, where
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
admits a

(s,t)
-bipolar orientation. Then

|\mathrm{val}(G,f)|\leq\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{F}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{s,t\}}\|f(v)\|_{{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Proof.

Number the vertices
\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}
according to a
(s,t)
-numbering,
and consider the bipolar orientation such that each edge directs from a
lower-numbered vertex to a higher-numbered vertex. By replacing the label
f(e)

with
f(e)^{*}
, we may assume that the vertices for each edge
e=(u,v)
in
Definition
5.2
are also ordered such that
u
has
lower number than
v
.

For each
k\in\{2,\ldots,m-1\}
, let
I_{k},O_{k}
be the partition of

\{1,\ldots,\text{deg}(v_{k})\}
corresponding to the incoming and outgoing
(ordered) edges of
v_{k}
, respectively. We may then express

\mathrm{val}(G,f)=\mathrm{vec}(f(v_{1}))^{\top}E_{2}M_{2}\cdots E_{m-1}M_{m-1}E_{m}\mathrm{vec}(f(v_{m}))

where

•

Each
M_{k}
for
k=2,\ldots,m-1
is a matrix given by the tensor product
of
\operatorname{mat}_{I_{k},O_{k}}(f(v_{k}))
and a number of identity matrices.

•

Each
E_{k}
for
k=2,\ldots,m
is a matrix given by the tensor product of
the matrices
\{f(e):e\text{ is an incoming edge of }v_{k}\}

and a number of identity matrices.

As an example, for the following network with
V=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{6}\}

f(v_{1})
f(v_{3})
f(v_{2})
f(v_{4})
f(v_{5})
f(v_{6})
f(\{1,3\})
f(\{1,2\})
f(\{2,3\})
f(\{4,5\})
f(\{3,4\})
f(\{2,5\})
f(\{4,6\})
f(\{5,6\})

the matrices
M_{2},\ldots,M_{m-1}
and
E_{2},\ldots,E_{m}
are given by

f(v_{1})
f(v_{2})
f(v_{3})
f(v_{4})
f(v_{5})
f(v_{6})
I
I
I
I
f(\{1,2\})
f(\{1,3\})
f(\{2,3\})
f(\{2,5\})
f(\{1,3\})
f(\{3,4\})
I
f(\{4,5\})
f(\{4,6\})
I
I
f(\{5,6\})
\operatorname{vec}(f(v_{1}))
I\otimes\operatorname{mat}_{1,2}(f(v_{2}))
\operatorname{mat}_{2,1}(f(v_{3}))\otimes I
\operatorname{mat}_{1,2}(f(v_{4}))\otimes I
I\otimes\operatorname{mat}_{2,1}(f(v_{5}))
\operatorname{vec}(f(v_{6}))

More generally, for each directed edge
e=(v_{k^{\prime}},v_{k})
, we introduce a
factor of the identity matrix
I\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
in the tensor product
defining each matrix
M_{k^{\prime}+1},\ldots,M_{k-1}
and
E_{k^{\prime}+1},\ldots,E_{k-1}
,
and a factor of
f(e)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
into that defining
E_{k}
, to form a
path of degree-2 vertices from
v_{k^{\prime}}
to
v_{k}
whose labels have product

f(e)
.

Then, since
\|M_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|f(v_{k})\|_{I_{k}\to O_{k}}

and
\|E_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\prod_{\text{incoming edges $e$ of }v_{k}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
,
where each edge appears in exactly one such term
\|E_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
,
this implies the bound

\displaystyle|\mathrm{val}(G,f)|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathrm{vec}(f(v_{1}))\|_{2}\|\mathrm{vec}(f(v_{m}))\|_{2}\prod_{k=2}^{m-1}\|M_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prod_{k=2}^{m}\|E_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{F}\prod_{v\in V\setminus\{s,t\}}\|f(v)\|_{{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}}\prod_{e\in E}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

∎

Many of the networks that arise in our proof will be of the following form, which by the following lemma admits a bipolar orientation:

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose
(G,f)
is a tensor network where

G=(\{s,t\}\cup\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E})
has its vertices partitioned into three
disjoint sets
\{s,t\},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, such that

•

The subgraph induced by
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is connected.

•

Each vertex of
\{s,t\}
has at least 1 incident edge connecting to
\mathcal{A}
,
and each vertex
v\in\mathcal{A}
has at least 1 incident edge connecting to

\{s,t\}
.

•

Each vertex
u\in\mathcal{B}
has at least 2 distinct neighbors in
\mathcal{A}
.

Then
G
admits a
(s,t)
-bipolar orientation.

Proof.

It suffices to check that the augmented multigraph
G^{+}=(\{s,t\}\cup\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E}\cup(s,t))
is biconnected, i.e. it remains connected upon removing
any vertex
v
and its incident edges. We denote this graph by

G^{+}\setminus\{v\}
.

For
a\in\{s,t\}
,
G^{+}\setminus\{a\}
remains connected since the
subgraph induced by
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
is connected, and the remaining vertex
of
\{s,t\}
different from
a
is a neighbor of
\mathcal{A}
.

For
u\in\mathcal{B}
, note that all vertices of
\mathcal{A}
are neighbors of
\{s,t\}
,
which are neighbors of each other via the added edge
(s,t)
.
Thus
\{s,t\}\cup\mathcal{A}
forms
a single connected component. Upon removing
u
, each remaining vertex of
\mathcal{B}

is a neighbor of
\mathcal{A}
, so
G^{+}\setminus\{u\}
is connected.

For
v\in\mathcal{A}
, by the same reasoning, all
vertices of
\{s,t\}\cup(\mathcal{A}\setminus\{v\})
form a single connected
component of
G^{+}\setminus\{v\}
. Upon removing
v
and its incident edges,
each
u\in\mathcal{B}
remains a neighbor of
\mathcal{A}\setminus\{v\}
, since
u
has at
least 2 distinct neighbors in
\mathcal{A}
. Thus
G^{+}\setminus\{v\}
is connected.
∎

5.3.2.
Tensor network representation

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a random vector having mean 0 and
finite moments of all orders, and let
\mathbf{x}^{\odot l}=(\mathbf{x}[i]^{l})_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

be its entrywise
l^{\text{th}}
power.
Let
k\geq 1
,
l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}\geq 1
, and

U\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}
. Then

\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{x}^{\odot l_{1}},...,\mathbf{x}^{\odot l_{k}}),U\rangle=\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{val}(G,f)

where
\mathcal{G}\equiv\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k},U)
is the set of all distinct tensor
networks
(G,f)
satisfying the properties:

(1)

G=(\{t\}\cup\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
where the vertices are partitioned
into disjoint sets
\{t\},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{V}
. Each edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
has label
f(e)=I\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and connects one vertex of
\{t\}\cup\mathcal{K}
with one
vertex of
\mathcal{V}
(hence
G
is bipartite). Furthermore,
the bipartite subgraph induced by
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
is connected.

(2)

\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
where each
v_{i}
has degree
\text{deg}(v_{i})=l_{i}+1

and label
f(v_{i})=I\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes l_{i}+1}
. Here
I
is the
order-
(l_{i}+1)
identity tensor,
having all diagonal entries 1 and off-diagonal entries 0.

(3)

The vertex
t
has
\text{deg}(t)=k
, label
f(t)=U
, and its ordered edges
connect to
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
.

(4)

Each vertex
u\in\mathcal{K}
has
\text{deg}(u)\geq 2
and label

f(u)=\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{x})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(u)}
.

Proof.

By linearity, it suffices to show the lemma when
U=\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{i_{k}}
is a standard basis element for every choice of indices

i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in[n]
.

We induct on
k
. For
k=1
and
U=\mathbf{e}_{i}
,
we have by the moment-cumulant relation

\langle\kappa_{1}(\mathbf{x}^{\odot l}),U\rangle=\kappa_{1}(\mathbf{x}[i]^{l})=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}[i]^{l}]=\sum_{\pi\in P_{l}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}(\mathbf{x}[i]),

where
P_{l}
is the set of all partitions of
\{1,\ldots,l\}
.
Since
\mathbf{x}
has mean 0, the summand corresponding to
\pi\in P_{l}
is 0 if
\pi

has a singleton block. Each remaining summand corresponding to

\pi=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{j}\}

may be understood as
\text{val}(G,f)
for the tensor network described by the lemma,
where
\mathcal{K}
has
j
vertices of degrees
|B_{1}|,\ldots,|B_{j}|
with all edges
connecting to the single vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}
. The set
\mathcal{G}(l,U)

of such tensor networks is in 1-to-1 correspondence with such summands
\pi\in P_{l}
. This proves the lemma for
k=1
.

Now suppose the lemma holds for all
k\leq t
, and consider
k=t+1
and

U=\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{e}_{i_{t+1}}
. By the
moment-cumulant relation for a mixed moment of order
t+1
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{m=1}^{t+1}\mathbf{x}[i_{m}]^{l_{m}}\right]=\sum_{\pi\in P_{t+1}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}(\mathbf{x}[i_{b}]^{l_{b}}:b\in B).

Let
1_{t+1}\in P_{t+1}
denote the partition having one block containing
all
t+1
elements. Then rearranging gives

\displaystyle\langle\kappa_{t+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\odot l_{t+1}}),U\rangle

\displaystyle=\kappa_{t+1}(\mathbf{x}[i_{1}]^{l_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}[i_{t+1}]^{l_{t+1}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{m=1}^{t+1}\mathbf{x}[i_{m}]^{l_{m}}\right]-\sum_{\pi\in P_{t+1}\setminus 1_{t+1}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}(\mathbf{x}[i_{b}]^{l_{b}}:b\in B).

On the other hand, denote by
\mathcal{A}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k},U)

the set of all distinct tensor networks
(G,f)
satisfying
all properties of the lemma except the property that
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
is
connected. Let
L=l_{1}+\ldots+l_{t+1}
, and let
j_{1}=\ldots=j_{l_{1}}=i_{1}
,

j_{l_{1}+1}=\ldots=j_{l_{1}+l_{2}}=i_{2}
, …,

j_{l_{t}+1}=\ldots=j_{l_{t+1}}=i_{t+1}
. Applying the moment-cumulant relation
for a mixed moment of order
L
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{m=1}^{t+1}\mathbf{x}[i_{m}]^{l_{m}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{m=1}^{L}\mathbf{x}[j_{m}]\right]=\sum_{\pi\in P_{L}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}(\mathbf{x}[j_{b}]:b\in B)=\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{A}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)}\text{val}(G,f).

For each
\pi\in P_{t+1}\setminus 1_{t+1}
and block
B\in\pi
, let

U_{B}=\otimes_{b\in B}\mathbf{e}_{i_{b}}
be the corresponding factors of
U
.
Then by the induction hypothesis, we have

\kappa_{|B|}(\mathbf{x}[i_{b}]^{l_{b}}:b\in B)=\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}((l_{b}:b\in B),\,U_{b})}\mathrm{val}(G,f)

Taking the product over blocks
B\in\pi
, we get

\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}(\mathbf{x}[i_{b}]^{l_{b}}:b\in B)=\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}_{\pi}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)}\mathrm{val}(G,f)

where, if
\pi=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{j}\}
, then

\mathcal{G}_{\pi}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)
is the set of tensor networks in

\mathcal{A}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)
for which
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
splits exactly into

j
connected components, with one component corresponding to each block
B\in\pi
that contains the vertices
\{v_{b}\in\mathcal{V}:b\in B\}
.
Then, since
\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)=\mathcal{G}_{1_{t+1}}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)
is precisely the set of tensor
networks with a single connected component,

\mathcal{A}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)\setminus\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)=\bigcup_{\pi\in P_{t+1}\setminus 1_{t+1}}\mathcal{G}_{\pi}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U).

Thus

\displaystyle\langle\kappa_{t+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\odot l_{t+1}}),U\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{A}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)}\mathrm{val}(G,f)-\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{A}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)\setminus\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)}\mathrm{val}(G,f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{t+1},U)}\mathrm{val}(G,f).

This completes the inductive argument.
∎

5.3.3.
Proof of Assumption
2

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a random vector having mean 0 and finite moments of all
orders. Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any deterministic, symmetric matrix

A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,

\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{x}^{*}A\mathbf{x}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{x}^{*}A\mathbf{x})^{2k}\leq\sum_{{}_{\text{partitions }\pi\text{ of }[4k]}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\|\kappa_{|B|}(\mathbf{x})\|_{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}\|A\|_{F}^{2k}.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{*}
. We can rewrite the expectation as

\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{x}^{*}A\mathbf{x}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{x}^{*}A\mathbf{x})^{2k}=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}((\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\Sigma)A)]^{2k}=\langle\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\Sigma)^{\otimes 2k}],A^{\otimes 2k}\rangle.

Applying Lemma
3.15
,
the above value may be decomposed as

\langle\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\Sigma)^{\otimes 2k}],A^{\otimes 2k}\rangle=\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{N}}\text{val}(G,f)

where
\mathcal{N}
is the set of all distinct tensor networks
(G,f)
satisfying the properties:

(1)

G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
where the vertices
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}

are partitioned into disjoint sets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
. Each edge
e\in\mathcal{E}
has label

f(e)=I\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and connects one vertex of
\mathcal{A}
with one vertex of

\mathcal{B}
(hence
G
is bipartite).

(2)

\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{2k}\}
where each vertex
a_{i}
has degree
2
and label
f(a_{i})=A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.

(3)

\mathcal{B}=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{m}\}
for some
1\leq m\leq 2k
, where each vertex

b_{i}
satisfies
2\leq\text{deg}(b_{i})\leq 4k
and has label
f(b_{i})=\kappa_{\text{deg}(b_{i})}(\mathbf{x})
.

(4)

If any
b_{i}
has degree 2, then the two edges incident to
b_{i}

connect to distinct neighbors in
\mathcal{A}
.

Here
\text{deg}(b_{i})\geq 2
because
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{x}=\kappa_{1}(\mathbf{x})=0
, so only diagrams without

\kappa_{1}(\mathbf{x})
contribute. Additionally, Lemma
3.15

ensures that no copy of
A
is contracted with a single cumulant tensor

\kappa_{2}(\mathbf{x})=\Sigma
, implying property 4. Note that these properties ensure
each connected component of
G
contains at least 2 vertices from
\mathcal{A}
,
and each vertex of
\mathcal{B}
has at least 2 distinct neighbors in
\mathcal{A}
.

Since
\text{val}(G,f)
factorizes across connected components of
G
,
we may bound
\text{val}(G^{\prime},f)
for each connected component
G^{\prime}
of
G
.
Let us modify
G^{\prime}
to yield a
network satisfying the conditions of Lemma
5.5
:
Let
A=U\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{v})U^{*}
be the spectral decomposition of
A
. For each
a\in\mathcal{A}\cap G^{\prime}
,
denote its two incident edges
e_{1},e_{2}
. We introduce a new vertex
v
, connect

v
to
a
via a new edge
e_{3}
so that
\text{deg}(v)=1
and
\text{deg}(a)=3
,
and relabel as

f(v)=\mathbf{v}
,
f(a)=I\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes 3}
(the diagonal order-3 tensor with all
diagonal entries 1),

f(e_{3})=I\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and
f(e_{1})=f(e_{2})=U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. Note that
this does not change
\text{val}(G^{\prime},f)
.
Let
\mathcal{V}
be the set of newly added vertices. Then
|\mathcal{V}|=|\mathcal{A}\cap G^{\prime}|\geq 2
. We
partition
\mathcal{V}
into two non-empty sets
\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2}
of cardinalities
i,j\geq 1
, merge the vertices in

\mathcal{V}_{1}
into a single vertex
s
having label
f(s)=\mathbf{v}^{\otimes i}
, and merge
the vertices in
\mathcal{V}_{2}
into a single vertex
t
having label
f(t)=\mathbf{v}^{\otimes j}
. This also does not change
\text{val}(G^{\prime},f)
.

It is readily checked that
G^{\prime}
now satisfies the condition of Lemma

5.5
. Thus by Lemma
5.4
,

|\text{val}(G^{\prime},f)|\leq\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{i}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{j}\|U\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2|\mathcal{A}\cap G^{\prime}|}\prod_{b\in\mathcal{B}\cap G^{\prime}}\|\kappa_{\text{deg}(b)}(\mathbf{x})\|_{{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}}=\|A\|_{F}^{|\mathcal{A}\cap G^{\prime}|}\prod_{b\in\mathcal{B}\cap G^{\prime}}\|\kappa_{\text{deg}(b)}(\mathbf{x})\|_{{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}},

where we have used
i+j=|\mathcal{V}|=|\mathcal{A}\cap G^{\prime}|
and
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=\|A\|_{F}
.
Multiplying across all connected components
G^{\prime}
of
G
and then
summing over all
(G,f)\in\mathcal{N}
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix any
k\geq 1
and
l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}\geq 1
.
Let
|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|
be the cardinality of the set
\mathcal{G}\equiv\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k},U)
in Lemma
5.6
(where this
cardinality does not depend on
U
). Let
l=l_{1}+\ldots+l_{k}
, and set

C_{l}(\mathbf{w})=\sup_{\text{partitions }\pi\text{ of }[l]}\prod_{B\in\pi}\|\kappa_{|B|}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}
. Then

\|\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}})\|_{{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}}\leq C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|.

Proof.

Consider any partition of
[k]
into two disjoint non-empty sets
I,J
,
and suppose
U\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k}
factorizes as
S\otimes T
with respect
to this partition, for some
S\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes|I|}
and

T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes|J|}
. We have by Lemma
5.6

\langle\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}}),U\rangle=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\text{val}(G,f)

where
\mathcal{G}\equiv\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k},U)
. We proceed to modify each tensor network
(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}
via a sequence of steps that do not change
\text{val}(G,f)
, to yield a network satisfying the requirements of Lemma
5.5
:

(1)

The vertex
t
in Lemma
5.6
has
label
f(t)=U=S\otimes T
. Since
f(t)
factorizes, we may split
t
into two
new vertices
s,t
with labels
f(s)=S
and
f(t)=T
, such that edges
corresponding to
I
connect to
s
and edges corresponding to
J
connect to

t
.

(2)

Each vertex
u\in\mathcal{K}
has label
f(u)=\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(X\mathbf{w})
, with

\text{deg}(u)
incident edges having label
I
. By
multilinearity of mixed cumulants, we may relabel

f(u)=\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})
and relabel each incident edge
e=(v,u)
with
X
.
Note that
f(u)
is then diagonal because
\mathbf{w}
has independent entries.

(3)

For each vertex
u\in\mathcal{K}
having a single unique neighbor

v\in\mathcal{V}
: Let its incident edges be
e_{1},\ldots,e_{\text{deg}(u)}=(v,u)
. Note that

\text{deg}(v)\geq\text{deg}(u)+2
since
v
is connected to at least one of
\{s,t\}

and to
(\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V})\setminus\{u,v\}
. (If
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}=\{u,v\}
, this
continues to hold as then
v
is connected to both
\{s,t\}
.)
We contract
u
by removing
u
and

e_{1},\ldots,e_{\text{deg}(u)}
from
(G,f)
, and replacing the diagonal tensor label
f(v)\equiv\operatorname{diag}(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(v)}
with the new label
\operatorname{diag}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(v)-\text{deg}(u)}
having entries

\hat{\mathbf{v}}[i]=\tilde{\mathbf{v}}[i]\times\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}X[i,j]^{\text{deg}(u)}\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})[j]\bigg).

At the end of Step 2, we have

(5.6)

\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{F}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}\|S\|_{F}\|T\|_{F}.

For each application of Step 3, since
\text{deg}(u)\geq 2
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\infty}\|\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}\|X\|_{\infty}^{\text{deg}(u)-2}\max_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}X[i,j]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\infty}\|\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{\text{deg}(u)},

and thus the inequality (
5.6
) continues to hold after Step
3. It is readily checked that after all three steps of the above procedure,
G

satisfies the condition of Lemma
5.5
with

\mathcal{A}\equiv\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{B}\equiv\mathcal{K}
. So applying Lemma
5.4
gives

\displaystyle|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{x}^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\odot l_{k}}),U\rangle|\leq\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}|\text{val}(G,f)|\leq C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|\cdot\|S\|_{F}\|T\|_{F}.

Since
I,J
and
S,T
defining
U=S\otimes T
are arbitrary,
by definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}
, this completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.17
,
Assumption
2
.

Denote
\mathbf{a}_{l}=(a_{il})_{i=1}^{n}
(where in the setting of Proposition

2.17
(a),
\mathbf{a}_{l}=0
for
l>D
). For any fixed
k\geq 1
and

l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}\geq 1
, we may expand by multilinearity of the mixed cumulant

\displaystyle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{a}_{l_{1}}\odot(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{l_{k}}\odot(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}})=(\mathbf{a}_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{a}_{l_{k}})\odot\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}}).

Thus, noting that
\mathbf{g}=\sigma(X\mathbf{w})=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{a}_{l}\odot(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l}

and any mixed cumulant
\kappa_{k}(\ldots)
is 0 if an argument is constant
(non-random), we have

\displaystyle\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g})\|_{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}=1}^{\infty}(\mathbf{a}_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{a}_{l_{k}})\odot\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}})\right\|_{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}=1}^{\infty}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}\Big)\|\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}})\|_{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}=1}^{\infty}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}\Big)C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|,

the last inequality using Lemma
5.8
.

In the setting of Proposition
2.17
(a),
C_{l}(\mathbf{w})
,
\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}
,

|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|
, and
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}

are all bounded by a constant depend only on
k,D
, so

\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g})\|_{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}\leq C
for a constant
C\equiv C(k,D)>0
.

In the setting of Proposition
2.17
(b) where
\mathbf{w}\sim N(0,I)
, we have

\kappa_{2}(\mathbf{w})=I
and
\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})=0
for all
k\geq 3
. Then

C_{l}(\mathbf{w})=1
for all even
l\geq 2
, and
C_{l}(\mathbf{w})=0
for odd
l
. In Lemma

5.6
, also
\text{val}(G,f)=0
unless
l=l_{1}+\ldots+l_{k}

is even and each vertex
u\in\mathcal{K}
has degree exactly 2, in which
case
|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|\leq l!!\leq C^{l}l^{l/2}

where
l!!
is the number of partitions of
l
into
l/2
blocks of size 2.
Applying the given condition

\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}<C(l_{i}!)^{-\beta}<C(l_{i}/e)^{-\beta l_{i}}
and

\sum_{i=1}^{k}l_{i}\log l_{i}>l\log(l/k)
,
we have that
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}<(Cke^{\beta})^{l}l^{-\beta l}
. Then, for a constant
C^{\prime}>0
not depending on

k,l
and for any
l\geq k
,

(5.7)

\mathop{\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}\geq 1}}_{l_{1}+\ldots+l_{k}=l}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}\Big)C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|\leq\mathop{\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}\geq 1}}_{l_{1}+\ldots+l_{k}=l}(Ck)^{l}l^{(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)l}\leq(Ck^{2})^{l}l^{(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)l}.

Since
\beta>1/2
, this is summable over
l\geq k
, so also

\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g})\|_{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}\leq C
for a constant
C\equiv C(k,\beta)>0
in this
case.

Then by Lemma
5.7
, for any

A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we have
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{x}^{*}A\mathbf{x}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{x}^{*}A\mathbf{x})^{2k}\leq C\|A\|_{F}^{2k}
for each fixed
k\geq 1
and a constant
C\equiv C(k)>0
(where this holds also for asymmetric
A
upon applying the lemma
with
(A+A^{*})/2
). This implies Assumption
2
by
Markov’s inequality.
∎

5.3.4.
Proof of Assumption
3

For each integer
l\geq 0
, let us now define a set of vectors

\mathcal{U}(l)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
for verifying Assumption
3
,
as follows: Define first the normalized matrices and
scalar cumulants, for
k\geq 2
,

\bar{X}=X/\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}},\qquad\bar{\kappa}_{k}(\mathbf{w}[j])=\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}[j])/\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}.

(If
\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}=0
, we set
\bar{\kappa}_{k}(\mathbf{w}[j])=0
.)
Define the diagonal matrices

D_{k}=\operatorname{diag}\bigg(\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bar{X}[i,j]^{k}\bar{\kappa}_{k}(\mathbf{w}[j])\bigg)_{i=1}^{n}\bigg),\qquad K_{k}=\operatorname{diag}\big(\big(\bar{\kappa}_{k}(\mathbf{w}[j])\big)_{j=1}^{n}\big).

Let
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\{I,\bar{X},\bar{X}^{*}\}\cup\{D_{k}\}_{k\geq 2}\cup\{K_{k}\}_{k\geq 2}
. We say that
I
has degrees 0 in both
X
and
\mathbf{w}
, that

\bar{X},\bar{X}^{*}
have degree 1 in
X
and 0 in
\mathbf{w}
,
that
D_{k}
has degrees
k
in both

X
and
\mathbf{w}
, and that
K_{k}
has degree 0 in
X
and
k
in
\mathbf{w}
.
For each
j\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{M}_{j}=\{AB,A\odot B:A,B\in\mathcal{M}_{j-1}\}
(where
AB

is the matrix product and
A\odot B
is the entrywise product). Each
set
\mathcal{M}_{j}
is closed under matrix transpose, since this holds for
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.
If
A,B
have degrees
a_{x},b_{x}
in
X
and
a_{w},b_{w}
in
\mathbf{w}
, then
we say that
AB
and
A\odot B
have degrees
a_{x}+b_{x}
in
X
and
a_{w}+b_{w}

in
\mathbf{w}
. Let
\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{j\geq 0}\mathcal{M}_{j}
, i.e.
\mathcal{M}
consists of all
matrices obtained from
I,\bar{X},D_{k},K_{k}
through combinations of matrix
products, entrywise products, and matrix transposes, and let

\mathcal{M}(l)\subset\mathcal{M}
be
those elements with degree at most
l
in
X
and at most
l
in
\mathbf{w}
. We set

(5.8)

\mathcal{U}(l)=\{M\mathbf{e}_{i}:M\in\mathcal{M}(l),\,M\neq 0,\,i\in[n]\}.

The following lemma is the main step to verify Assumption
3
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Fix any
k\geq 3
and
l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}\geq 1
, and let
l=l_{1}+\ldots+l_{k}
.
Let
|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|
and
C_{l}(\mathbf{w})
be as defined in
Lemma
5.8
.
Then for any
m\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
,
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
,

(5.9)

\Big|\langle\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle\Big|\leq C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|\times\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}(l)}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}.

We proceed to show Lemma
5.9
.
For the later combinatorial arguments and casework, it will be convenient
to first specialize Lemma
5.6
to this setting
where
U=\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T
,
and to simplify the networks by contracting
vertices with 1 or 2 unique neighbors. The structure of the resulting simplified
networks is captured by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.10
.

In the setting of Lemma
5.9
, there exists a set
\mathcal{G}
of
tensor networks
(G,f)
having cardinality
|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|
such that

\langle\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle=\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{val}(G,f),

and each
(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

G=(\{s,t\}\cup\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
where the vertices are
partitioned into disjoint sets
\{s,t\},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{V}
. The label

f(v)
for each
v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
is a diagonal tensor,
the label
f(s)
is a rank-1 tensor (or a vector if
\text{deg}(s)=1
),
and the label
f(e)
for each edge incident to
\{s,t\}
is
I\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.

(2)

The subgraph induced by
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
is connected. Every
edge that is incident to a vertex in
\{s,t\}\cup\mathcal{K}
connects to a
vertex in
\mathcal{V}
. (Vertices of
\mathcal{V}
may also connect to each other, so

G
is not necessarily bipartite.)
The vertices
\{s,t\}
have degrees
\text{deg}(s)=m

and
\text{deg}(t)=k-m
, and each vertex of
\mathcal{V}
is incident to
at least one edge connecting to
\{s,t\}
.

(3)

Each vertex
u\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
has
\text{deg}(u)\geq 3
, and any two
vertices
u,v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
are connected by at most 1 edge (i.e. the
subgraph induced by
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
is simple).

(4)

Let
\mathcal{U}(l)
be as defined in (
5.8
), and let

C_{l}(\mathbf{w})
be as defined in Lemma
5.9
. Then

(5.10)

\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}(l)}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}

Proof.

We begin with the representation of Lemma
5.6

applied to
\mathbf{x}=X\mathbf{w}
,

\langle\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle=\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}}\text{val}(G,f)

where
\mathcal{G}\equiv\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k},\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T)
.
We proceed to modify each tensor network
(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}
via a sequence of
steps that do not change
\text{val}(G,f)
, to yield a network satisfying the stated
properties:

(1)

As in Lemma
5.8
,
we split the vertex
t
with label

f(t)=\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T
into
two vertices
s,t
with labels
f(s)=\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}

and
f(t)=T
, such that the first
m
edges are
incident to
s
and the last
k-m
edges are incident to
t
.

(2)

As in Lemma
5.8
, we relabel each
u\in\mathcal{K}
by
(the diagonal label)
f(u)=\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})
and relabel each incident edge

e=(v,u)
with
X
. This resulting network satisfies
Conditions 1–2 of Lemma
5.10
.

(3)

We then repeatedly apply the following steps. It is readily checked
that each step preserves
\text{val}(G,f)
and Conditions 1–2:

(a)

As in Lemma
5.8
, if
a vertex
u\in\mathcal{K}
has a single unique neighbor

v\in\mathcal{V}
with incident edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{\text{deg}(u)}=(v,u)
,
we contract
u
by removing
u
and

e_{1},\ldots,e_{\text{deg}(u)}
from
(G,f)
, and replacing the label
label
f(v)\equiv\operatorname{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{v}})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(v)}

with the new label
\operatorname{diag}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(v)-\text{deg}(u)}

having entries

\hat{\mathbf{v}}[i]=\tilde{\mathbf{v}}[i]\times\bigg(\underbrace{\sum_{j=1}^{n}X[i,j]^{\text{deg}(u)}\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})[j]}_{=\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{\text{deg}(u)}D_{\text{deg}(u)}[i,i]}\bigg).

(b)

Suppose two vertices
u,v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
each have at least 2
distinct neighbors, and
u,v
are also connected by

a\geq 2
edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{a}=(u,v)
. We replace these edges by a
single edge
e=(u,v)
having label
f(e)=f(e_{1})\odot\ldots\odot f(e_{a})
(the
entrywise product), and reduce the orders of
f(u),f(v)
by
a-1
while keeping
their diagonal entries the same.

(c)

Suppose a vertex
u\in\mathcal{K}
has
\text{deg}(u)=2
and two distinct
neighbors
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
. Let
e=(v,u)
and
e^{\prime}=(u,v^{\prime})
.
We contract
u
by removing
u
and
e,e^{\prime}
, and replacing these with the
single new edge
e^{\prime\prime}=(v,v^{\prime})
labeled by
f(e^{\prime\prime})=f(e)f(u)f(e^{\prime})
.

(d)

Suppose a vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}
has
\text{deg}(v)=2
and is adjacent to one
vertex in
\{s,t\}
— say,
s
via its last incident edge — and
one vertex
u\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
. Let
e=(s,v)
and
e^{\prime}=(v,u)
,
and note that
f(u)
is diagonal and
f(e)=I
. We contract
v
by removing

v
and
e,e^{\prime}
, replacing these by a single new edge
e^{\prime\prime}=(s,u)
with
label
f(e^{\prime\prime})=I
,
reassigning
u
to the vertex set
\mathcal{V}
if it belongs to
\mathcal{K}
(so that each
edge incident to
s
still connects to
\mathcal{V}
), and replacing

f(s)\equiv\widetilde{S}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(s)}
by the new label

\widehat{S}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(s)}
given by the contraction

\widehat{S}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{\text{deg}(s)}]=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{S}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{\text{deg}(s)-1},j]f(v)[j,j]f(e^{\prime})[j,i_{\text{deg}(s)}]

Note that if

\widetilde{S}=\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{\text{deg}(s)}
,
then
\widehat{S}=\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{\text{deg}(s)-1}\otimes(f(e^{\prime})^{*}f(v)\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{\text{deg}(s)})
, so

\widehat{S}
remains rank-1, as required in Condition 1.

We apply the above steps (a–d) iteratively in any order, until none of these
steps are further applicable. (The number of such applications is finite, since
each step removes at least one vertex or edge.)

We now check Conditions 3–4 for this resulting network: Note first that since
Conditions 1–2 hold, it is either the case that every
v\in\mathcal{V}
has at
least 1 neighbor in
\{s,t\}
and at least 1 neighbor in
(\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V})\setminus\{v\}
, or
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}=\{v\}
consists of the single vertex
v

which has
m
edges connecting to
s
and
k-m
edges connecting to
t
.
In both cases,
v
has at least 2 distinct neighbors.
We have
\text{deg}(v)\geq 3
in the former case because Step 3(d) is no longer
applicable, and
\text{deg}(v)\geq 3
in the latter case because
k\geq 3
.
For each
u\in\mathcal{K}
, we also have that
u
has at least 2 distinct neighbors
and
\text{deg}(u)\geq 3
, because Steps 3(a,c) are no
longer applicable. Then any two vertices
u,v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
are connected
by at most 1 edge, as Step 3(b) is no longer applicable. This shows Condition 3.

To check Condition 4, note that the network
(G,f)
after Step 2 has
f(s)=\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}
,
f(t)=T
, and

(5.11)

\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\Big)\|T\|_{\infty}\Big(\prod_{u\in\mathcal{K}}\|\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}\Big)\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}.
