Thus Condition 4 holds for this network. For each application of Step 3(a),
since
\text{deg}(u)\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\infty}\|\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}\|X\|_{\infty}^{\text{deg}(u)-2}\max_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}X[i,j]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\infty}\|\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{\text{deg}(u)}.

For each application of Step 3(b), we have

\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|f(e_{1})\odot\ldots\odot f(e_{a})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|f(e_{1})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ldots\|f(e_{a})\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

For each application of Step 3(c), we have

\|f(e^{\prime\prime})\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|f(e)f(u)f(e^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|f(u)\|_{\infty}\|f(e^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

For each application of Step 3(d) to
s
, we have

\|\widehat{S}\|_{F}=\|\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1}\|_{2}\ldots\|\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{\text{deg}(s)-1}\|_{2}\|f(e^{\prime})^{*}f(u)\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{\text{deg}(s)}\|_{2}\leq\|f(e^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|f(u)\|_{\infty}\|\widetilde{S}\|_{F}.

Thus, each of these operations do not increase the left side of
(
5.11
).

Each time Step 3(d) is applied to
t
, a matrix of
the form
f(v)f(e^{\prime})
is contracted with one axis of the tensor
f(t)\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
.
Here,
f(v)f(e^{\prime})
is obtained from successively applying matrix products,
entrywise products, and transposes to the matrices
\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{k}D_{k}
,

\operatorname{diag}((\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}[j]))_{j=1}^{n})=\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}K_{k}
, and

X=\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\bar{X}
arising in the preceding applications of Steps 3(a–c).
Hence, the final label
f(t)
is given by the contraction of
T
along each
axis
j=1,\ldots,k-m
with a matrix of the form

\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{a_{j}}\prod_{k\in B_{j}}\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}\cdot M_{j}
for some
a_{j}\geq 0
, some list
B_{j}
(possibly empty) of integers
\geq 2
,
and some matrix
M_{j}\in\mathcal{M}
having degree
a_{j}
in
X
and

b_{j}:=\sum_{k\in B_{j}}k
in
\mathbf{w}
, where
\mathcal{M}
and this notion of degree are as
defined preceding (
5.8
). In the network after Step 2, we have
l

edges with label
X
, and also
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{K}}\text{deg}(u)=l
. Thus, for the
label
f(t)
after the conclusion of the above steps, we must have

a_{1}+\ldots+a_{k-m}\leq l
and
b_{1}+\ldots+b_{k-m}\leq l
, and in particular

M_{j}\in\mathcal{M}(l)
for each
j=1,\ldots,k-m
. Thus

\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\leq\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}(l)}\prod_{j=1}^{k-m}\left(\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{a_{j}}\prod_{k\in B_{j}}\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}\right).

Whenever Step 3(d) is applied to contract a matrix of the form

f(v)f(e^{\prime})=\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{a}\prod_{k\in B}\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}M

into
f(t)
, this factor of

\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{a}\prod_{k\in B}\|\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}
is removed
from the bound (
5.11
) for the remaining factors
on the left side of (
5.11
). Thus, for the final network

(G,f)
, we have the bound

\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\Big)\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}(l)}\Big(\prod_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\|\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}\Big)\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}

which shows Condition 4 since

\prod_{u\in\mathcal{K}}\|\kappa_{\text{deg}(u)}(\mathbf{w})\|_{\infty}\leq C_{l}(\mathbf{w})
.
∎

Note that each network
(G,f)
of
Lemma
5.10
satifies
the conditions of Lemma
5.5
with
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{V}
and

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{K}
. Then, by Lemma
5.4
,
we have

|\text{val}(G,f)|\leq\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{F}\prod_{u\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(u)\|_{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Applying also
\|f(t)\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{n}^{k-m}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}
for any
f(t)\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
, and
\|f(u)\|_{\mathrm{mat}\text{-}\mathrm{op}}=\|f(u)\|_{\infty}
because
f(u)
is
diagonal, Lemma
5.10
then implies
the bound

\displaystyle\Big|\langle\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle\Big|\leq\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}}|\text{val}(G,f)|

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt\leq\sum_{(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}}\sqrt{n}^{k-m}\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\prod_{u\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(u)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt\leq C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|\times\sqrt{n}^{k-m}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}(l)}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}.

This shows a weaker form of (
5.9
) with factor

\sqrt{n}^{k-m}
instead of
\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}
.

We conclude the proof of Lemma
5.9
by
improving this factor to
\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}
using a more involved combinatorial argument and some casework.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}
be any tensor network satisfying the properties of
Lemma
5.10
. Suppose that
k-m\geq 2
,
and that the vertex
t
connects to
k-m
distinct neighbors
\mathcal{V}_{T}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
. Let
G^{\prime}
be the subgraph of
G
induced by
\{s\}\cup\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
.

Then
G^{\prime}
contains a tree
H
satisfying following property:
Call a leaf vertex
v
of
H
“good” if
v\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
and

v
is connected via a path of edges in
G^{\prime}\setminus H

to either a different vertex
u\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
or to
s
.
Then
H
has at least 2 good leaf vertices.

Proof.

Since the subgraph induced by
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
is connected, there
exists at least one tree in this subgraph containing
\mathcal{V}_{T}
. Among all such
trees, let
H
be one having the smallest number of vertices.
Then each leaf of
H
belongs to
\mathcal{V}_{T}
, and all edges incident to
s
belong
to
G^{\prime}\setminus H
.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be the vertices neighboring
s
. Note that

\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{S}\cup\mathcal{V}_{T}
, and
\mathcal{V}_{S}\cap\mathcal{V}_{T}
may be non-empty as a vertex
can neighbor both
s
and
t
.
Let us call an edge of
G^{\prime}\setminus H
a “non-tree edge”.
Since
|\mathcal{V}_{T}|=\text{deg}(t)=k-m
, the vertex
t
has only a single
edge in
G
connecting to each vertex of
\mathcal{V}_{T}
. Then, since every

v\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
has degree at least 3 in
G
,
every leaf
v
of
H
must have at least one incident non-tree edge
(v,w)
.
If
w=s
,
w\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
, or
w\in\mathcal{V}_{S}
(and hence connects to
s
via a
non-tree edge), then by definition
v
is
good. If
w\in\mathcal{K}
and
w\notin H
, then letting
u\in\mathcal{V}_{S}\cup\mathcal{V}_{T}

be any neighbor of
w
different from
v
,

(w,u)
must be a non-tree edge, so
v
is also good. Thus we have
established the following claim:

If a leaf vertex
v
of
H
is not good,
then there exists a non-tree edge
(v,w)
where
w\in H\cap\mathcal{K}
.

Note that if
H
consists of two leaf vertices
connected by a single edge, or if
H
is a star consisting of a single vertex
u\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
connecting by an edge to all other vertices of
H

(which are leaves and hence belong to
\mathcal{V}_{T}
),
then no such non-tree edge
(v,w)
can exist. Then all leaf vertices
of
H
are good, so in particular
H
has at least 2 good leaves, and the lemma
holds for
H
.

For any other tree
H
, there is a path of at least 3 distinct edges
in
H
, say
u-r-r^{\prime}-v
. Let us call the two components of
H
connected to
r

and to
r^{\prime}
upon removing the edge
r-r^{\prime}
the
“sub-trees rooted at
r
and
r^{\prime}
” respectively. Let
\mathcal{L}
be the set of
leaves in the sub-tree rooted at
r
that have maximal distance from
r
,
and define similarly
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
for the sub-tree rooted at
r^{\prime}
. If
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}

each contain a good leaf, then again
H
has at least 2 good leaves, so the
lemma holds for
H
.

Otherwise, at least one of
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
— say,
\mathcal{L}
— does not contain
any good leaf. We then consider the following modification of
H
:
Take any
v\in\mathcal{L}
, let
p(v)
be its parent in the sub-tree rooted at
r
,
and let
\mathcal{L}_{v}
be the set of all children of
p(v)
(including
v
).
Each
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}
is a leaf vertex that is not good, so by the above claim,
for each
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}
, there exists a non-tree edge
(v^{\prime},w(v^{\prime}))
where

w(v^{\prime})\in H\cap\mathcal{K}
. We consider the graph

\tilde{H}
that removes the edges
\{(v^{\prime},p(v)):v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}\}
from
H
and adds
the new edges
\{(v^{\prime},w(v^{\prime})):v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}\}
. Note that
\tilde{H}
is a tree
having the same vertices as
H
, that each
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}
and
p(v)
itself are
now leaves of
\tilde{H}
, and that each leaf of
\tilde{H}
other than
p(v)

was a leaf of
H
and hence belongs to
\mathcal{V}_{T}
.
We must have
p(v)\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
, because otherwise
we may remove
p(v)
from
\tilde{H}
to obtain a smaller tree containing

\mathcal{V}_{T}
, contradicting that
H
was such a tree having the smallest number of
vertices. Thus all leaves of
\tilde{H}
belong to
\mathcal{V}_{T}
. Furthermore,
each
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}
connects to
p(v)
by an edge not belonging to

\tilde{H}
, so
p(v)
and all vertices of
\{v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{v}\}
are good leaves of

\tilde{H}
. Thus
\tilde{H}
has at least 2 good leaves, so the lemma holds for

\tilde{H}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.9
.

Consider any tensor network
(G,f)\in\mathcal{G}
given in
Lemma
5.10
.
Recall that the vertex
t
has
\text{deg}(t)=k-m
and label
f(t)\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
. We will establish the bound

(5.12)

|\text{val}(G,f)|\leq\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Case 1:

Suppose that
t
has strictly fewer than
k-m
distinct neighbors,
i.e. that some two edges incident to
t
— say, its last two edges —
connect to
the same vertex of
\mathcal{V}
. Consider the modified network
(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})

that replaces the last two edges incident to
t
by a single edge with
label
\tilde{f}(e)=I
, and replaces
f(t)
by
\tilde{f}(t)\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m-1}
having entries

\tilde{f}(t)[i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-m-1}]=f(t)[i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-m-2},i_{k-m-1},i_{k-m-1}].

Then
\text{val}(G,f)=\text{val}(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})
. Lemma
5.5

(with
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{K}
)
implies that
\tilde{G}
still admits a
(s,t)
-bipolar orientation. Then,
denoting by
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
the edges of
\tilde{G}
, we have
by Lemma
5.4
that

\displaystyle|\text{val}(G,f)|=|\text{val}(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{f}(s)\|_{F}\|\tilde{f}(t)\|_{F}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|\tilde{f}(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}\|\tilde{f}(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}},

the second inequality applying

\|\tilde{f}(t)\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|\tilde{f}(t)\|_{\infty}=\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}
.

Case 2:

Suppose now that
t
has exactly
k-m
distinct neighbors, and
k-m=1
.
Let
v_{t}\in\mathcal{V}
be the vertex adjacent to
t
. We modify the network
(G,f)

into a new network
(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})
via two steps:

•

We contract
f(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

by removing
f(t)
and its incident edge, and replacing
the diagonal label
f(v_{t})\equiv\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{v})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(v)}
by

\tilde{f}(v_{t})=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{v}\odot f(t))\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\text{deg}(v)-1}
.

•

Note that
m\geq 2
, since
k\geq 3
. Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
be the
ordered edges incident to
s
in
G
, and suppose its (rank-1) label factorizes
as
f(s)=\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{m}
. We
split
s
into two vertices
s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}
of
\tilde{G}
,
where
s^{\prime}
is incident to
e_{1}
with label
\tilde{f}(s^{\prime})=\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{1}
, and

s^{\prime\prime}
is incident to
e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}
with label
\tilde{f}(s^{\prime\prime})=\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{m}
.

Then
\text{val}(G,f)=\text{val}(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})
. We claim that
(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})
admits a
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})
-bipolar orientation. Indeed, if
v_{t}
has an edge
connecting to either
s^{\prime}
or
s^{\prime\prime}
in
\tilde{G}
, then Lemma

5.5
(with
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{K}
) applies to show

(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})
has a
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})
-bipolar orientation.
Otherwise, each edge of
\tilde{G}

incident to
v_{t}
must connect to a distinct neighbor in
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
,
and there are at least 2 such edges, with each such neighbor
having degree
\geq 3
. Then
the graph
\tilde{G}\setminus\{v_{t}\}
removing
v_{t}
and its incident edges
admits a
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})
-bipolar orientation by Lemma
5.5

applied with
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{V}\setminus\{v_{t}\}
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{K}
.
Since
v_{t}
has at least 2 distinct neighbors in

\tilde{G}\setminus\{v_{t}\}
, this implies that
\tilde{G}
also admits
a
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})
-bipolar orientation, verifying our claim.
Then, denoting by
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
the edges of
(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})
, we have
by Lemma
5.4
that

\displaystyle|\text{val}(G,f)|=|\text{val}(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{f}(s^{\prime})\|_{F}\|\tilde{f}(s^{\prime\prime})\|_{F}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|\tilde{f}(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}\|\tilde{f}(e)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|f(e)\|_{\infty},

the second inequality applying

\|\tilde{f}(s^{\prime})\|_{F}\|\tilde{f}(s^{\prime\prime})\|_{F}=\|f(s)\|_{F}
and

\|\tilde{f}(v_{t})\|_{\infty}\leq\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\|f(v_{t})\|_{\infty}
.

Case 3:
Suppose
t
has exactly
k-m
distinct neighbors,
and
k-m\geq 2
. Let
\partial s
and
\partial t
denote the edges incident
to
s
and
t
, respectively, where
|\partial s|=m
and
|\partial t|=k-m
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}
denote all remaining edges, which belong to the
connected subgraph induced by
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
.
For each edge
e\in\partial s\cup\partial t
, denote by
v_{e}\in\mathcal{V}

the vertex other than
\{s,t\}
that is incident to
e
. As
f(s)
is rank-1,
it admits a factorization
f(s)=\otimes_{e\in\partial s}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}

for some
m
vectors
(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}:e\in\partial s)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, since

f(e)=I
for each
e\in\partial s\cup\partial t
and
f(v)
is diagonal
for each vertex
v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
, we may express
\text{val}(G,f)
as

\displaystyle\text{val}(G,f)=\sum_{i_{v}\in[n]:v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}f(t)[i_{v_{e}}:e\in\partial t]\prod_{e\in\partial s}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}[i_{v_{e}}]\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}f(v)[i_{v},\ldots,i_{v}]\prod_{e=(u,v)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}}f(e)[i_{u},i_{v}]

where the summation is over one distinct index
i_{v}\in[n]
for each vertex of

\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{T}=\{v_{e}:e\in\partial t\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}

and
\mathcal{V}_{S}=\{v_{e}:e\in\partial s\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
denote the sets of vertices
adjacent to
t
and
s
, where
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{T}\cup\mathcal{V}_{S}
and
|\mathcal{V}_{T}|=k-m\geq 2
.
(We recall that
\mathcal{V}_{S}\cap\mathcal{V}_{T}
may be non-empty.)
We will next partition the edges of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\cup\partial s
—
including those incident to
s
but not those incident to
t
— into two
non-empty
disjoint sets
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
, so as to bound the above expression using
Cauchy-Schwarz. Given any such partition
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
, let us
split the vertices of
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
that are
incident to
\mathcal{E}_{1}
into two types: Let
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
be
those vertices incident to some edge of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, but not belonging to
\mathcal{V}_{T}

or incident to any edge of
\mathcal{E}_{2}
.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
be those remaining vertices which are
incident to an edge of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and either belong to
\mathcal{V}_{T}
or are incident
to an edge of
\mathcal{E}_{2}
. We define a tensor
U_{1}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes|\mathcal{U}_{1}|}
of
order
|\mathcal{U}_{1}|
, which sums over vertices of
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{*}
and is
indexed by
(i_{v}:v\in\mathcal{U}_{1})
corresponding to vertices of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
. This tensor

U_{1}
has entries

U_{1}[i_{v}:v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}]=\sum_{i_{v}\in[n]:v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}^{*}}\prod_{e\in\partial s\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}[i_{v_{e}}]\prod_{v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}^{*}}f(v)[i_{v},\ldots,i_{v}]\prod_{e\in(u,v)\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}}f(e)[i_{u},i_{v}].

We define analogously the sets
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{*},\mathcal{U}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
and tensor

U_{2}
for
\mathcal{E}_{2}
. Note that
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
is then the disjoint union of the
three sets
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{*}
,
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{*}
, and
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}
, where
\mathcal{V}_{T}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}
because each vertex of
\mathcal{V}_{T}
must be incident to at least
one edge of the connected subgraph
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
. Then

\text{val}(G,f)=\sum_{i_{v}\in[n]:v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}}f(t)[i_{v_{e}:e\in\partial t}]\,\bigg(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}}f(v)[i_{v},\ldots,i_{v}]\bigg)U_{1}[i_{v}:v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}]\,U_{2}[i_{v}:v\in\mathcal{U}_{2}].

Applying Cauchy-Schwartz over the indices of
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}
,

\displaystyle|\text{val}(G,f)|

\displaystyle\leq\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\bigg(\sum_{i_{v}\in[n]:v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}}U_{1}[i_{v}:v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}]^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\sum_{i_{v}\in[n]:v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}}U_{2}[i_{v}:v\in\mathcal{U}_{2}]^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Note that
(\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2})\setminus\mathcal{U}_{1}
consists of the vertices of

\mathcal{V}_{T}
that are not incident to any edge of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
. Let
k_{1}
be the
number of such vertices, and define similarly
k_{2}
as the number of vertices of

\mathcal{V}_{T}
that are not incident to any edge of
\mathcal{E}_{2}
. Then the above yields the
bound

(5.13)

|\text{val}(G,f)|\leq\sqrt{n}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\bigg)\|U_{1}\|_{F}\|U_{2}\|_{F}.

To further bound
\|U_{1}\|_{F}
,
consider the connected components of the subgraph induced by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
(i.e. the
edges
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, their incident vertices
\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{1}^{*}
, and the vertex
s
if

\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\partial s
is non-empty).
If
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\partial s
is non-empty, and the component of
s

breaks into
j
pieces upon removing
s
, let us further split this
component of
s
into
j
separate components, each containing a copy of
s
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be the set of all components. For each component
C\in\mathcal{C}
, let

\mathcal{U}_{C}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{1}^{*}
,
\mathcal{U}_{C}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{1}
,

\mathcal{E}_{C}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}
,
and
\partial s_{C}\subseteq\partial s
be the vertices and edges belonging to

C
, where we set
\partial s_{C}=\emptyset
if
C
does not contain (a copy of)

s
. Note that
\mathcal{U}_{C}
must be non-empty, because
Lemma
5.10
ensures that
the subgraph
(\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}})
is connected.
Then
U_{1}
factorizes as

U_{1}=\bigotimes_{C\in\mathcal{C}}U_{C}

where each
U_{C}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes|\mathcal{U}_{C}|}
is the tensor indexed by vertices
of
\mathcal{U}_{C}
, with entries

U_{C}[i_{v}:v\in\mathcal{U}_{C}]=\sum_{i_{v}\in[n]:v\in\mathcal{U}_{C}^{*}}\prod_{e\in\partial s_{C}}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}[i_{v_{e}}]\prod_{v\in\mathcal{U}_{C}^{*}}f(v)[i_{v},\ldots,i_{v}]\prod_{e=(u,v)\in\mathcal{E}_{C}}f(e)[i_{u},i_{v}].

Thus,

(5.14)

\|U_{1}\|_{F}=\prod_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\|U_{C}\|_{F}=\prod_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\bigg(\sup_{T^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes|\mathcal{U}_{C}|}:\|T^{\prime}\|_{F}=1}\langle U_{C},T^{\prime}\rangle\bigg).

For each component
C\in\mathcal{C}
where
C
contains (a copy of)
s
,
let
\tilde{G}
be the graph that adds a sink vertex
t^{\prime}
to
C
,
connected by an edge to each vertex of
\mathcal{U}_{C}
.
We claim that
\tilde{G}
admits a
(s,t^{\prime})
-bipolar
orientation: Let
\mathcal{A}
be the vertices of
\tilde{G}\setminus\{s,t^{\prime}\}

that are connected to
\{s,t^{\prime}\}
by an edge, and let
\mathcal{B}
be all remaining
vertices of
\tilde{G}\setminus\{s,t^{\prime}\}
. The subgraph induced by

\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
in
\tilde{G}
is connected,
by the construction of
C
as a single component in
\mathcal{C}
.
By definition, any vertex
v\in\tilde{G}\cap\mathcal{U}_{C}
is adjacent to
t^{\prime}
, hence

v\in\mathcal{A}
. Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
, note that any vertex

v\in\tilde{G}\cap\mathcal{V}_{T}
must be in
\mathcal{U}_{C}
, hence also
v\in\mathcal{A}
.
Any vertex
v\in\tilde{G}\cap\mathcal{V}_{S}
must either neighbor
s

in
\tilde{G}
(if the edge
(s,v)
belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and hence to
\tilde{G}
)
or neighbor
t^{\prime}
in
\tilde{G}
(if
(s,v)
belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{2}
and hence

v\in\mathcal{U}_{C}
); thus also
v\in\mathcal{A}
. Then
\tilde{G}\cap(\mathcal{U}_{C}\cup\mathcal{V})\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, implying that
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{C}^{*}\cap\mathcal{K}
. Any such vertex
u\in\mathcal{B}
has at least 3
incident edges, all of these edges belong to
\tilde{G}
(since
u\in\mathcal{U}_{C}^{*}
), and each such edge
must connect to a distinct neighbor in
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{S}\cup\mathcal{V}_{T}
(since
u\in\mathcal{K}
) which
must belong to
\mathcal{A}
. Thus,
\tilde{G}
satisfies the conditions of Lemma

5.5
, implying that
\tilde{G}
has a
(s,t^{\prime})
-bipolar
orientation as claimed. We observe that

\langle U_{C},T^{\prime}\rangle=\text{val}(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})
for a labeling
\tilde{f}
where

\tilde{f}(s)=\otimes_{e\in\partial s_{C}}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}
and

\tilde{f}(t^{\prime})=T^{\prime}
. Then, applying Lemma
5.4

and
\|\tilde{f}(s)\|_{F}=\prod_{e\in\partial s_{C}}\|\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}\|_{2}
, we get

(5.15)

|\langle U_{C},T^{\prime}\rangle|=|\text{val}(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})|\leq\|T^{\prime}\|_{F}\prod_{e\in\partial s_{C}}\|\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}\|_{2}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{U}_{C}^{*}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{C}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

For each
C\in\mathcal{C}
where
C
does not contain
s
,
let
\tilde{G}
be the graph that adds two vertices
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}
to
C
,
where
s^{\prime}
connects by an edge to any single vertex of
\mathcal{U}_{C}
,
and
t^{\prime}
connects by an edge to every vertex of
\mathcal{U}_{C}
. The same arguments as
above show that
\tilde{G}
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma
5.5
(with
\mathcal{A}
being the vertices adjacent to

\{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\}
and
\mathcal{B}
being the rest), and hence admits a
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
-bipolar
orientation. We observe that

\langle U_{C},T^{\prime}\rangle=\text{val}(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})
for a labeling
\tilde{f}
where

\tilde{f}(s)=(1,1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

\tilde{f}(t^{\prime})=T^{\prime}
. Applying
Lemma
5.4
and
\|\tilde{f}(s)\|_{F}=\sqrt{n}
,
we get

(5.16)

|\langle U_{C},T^{\prime}\rangle|=|\text{val}(\tilde{G},\tilde{f})|\leq\sqrt{n}\|T^{\prime}\|_{F}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{U}_{C}^{*}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{C}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Let
m_{1},m_{2}
be the numbers of connected components in the subgraphs induced by

\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
that do not contain
s
.
Then, applying the bound (
5.15
)
or (
5.16
) to each component
C\in\mathcal{C}
of (
5.14
), we
obtain

\|U_{1}\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{n}^{m_{1}}\prod_{e\in\partial s\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}}\|\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}\|_{2}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{U}_{1}^{*}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}},

and similarly for
\|U_{2}\|_{F}
and
m_{2}
. Applying these bounds back to
(
5.13
) and recalling that
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{*}
,
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{*}
, and
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}
form a partition of
\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
,

|\text{val}(G,f)|\leq\sqrt{n}^{k_{1}+k_{2}+m_{1}+m_{2}}\|f(s)\|_{F}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\|f(v)\|_{\infty}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}}\|f(e)\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Finally, to show (
5.12
), let us argue that we may choose
the edge partition
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\cup\partial s

to ensure that
k_{1}+k_{2}+m_{1}+m_{2}\leq k-m-1
. Let
G^{\prime}
be the subgraph induced
by
\{s\}\cup\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
, i.e. containing the vertices

\{s\}\cup\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}
and edges
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{V}}\cup\partial s
.
Since
t
has exactly
k-m

distinct neighbors in
\mathcal{V}_{T}
and
k-m\geq 2
, Lemma
5.11

implies that there exists a tree
H
in
G^{\prime}

with at least two good leaves in
\mathcal{V}_{T}
. Let
G^{\prime\prime}
be the graph
G^{\prime}
removing
all edges of
H
; here
G^{\prime\prime}
may be disconnected and/or have isolated vertices.
By definition, each good leaf
v\in\mathcal{V}_{T}

is connected by a path in
G^{\prime\prime}
either to another vertex
u\in\mathcal{V}_{T}

or to
s
. We consider two cases:

(a) Suppose there is a good leaf
v\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
whose connected component in

G^{\prime\prime}
does not contain
s
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be all edges of this connected
component, and let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be all remaining edges of
G^{\prime}
.
Since
v
is connected by a path in
G^{\prime\prime}
to some other
u\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
,
the (connected) subgraph induced by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
has at least two vertices of

\mathcal{V}_{T}
, so
k_{1}\leq k-m-2
and
m_{1}=1
. The subgraph induced by
\mathcal{E}_{2}
is
also connected, because all connected components of
G^{\prime\prime}
must be
connected via the removed tree
H
. Furthermore, this subgraph contains both
s

and all vertices of
\mathcal{V}_{T}
, since
\mathcal{E}_{2}
contains all edges of
H
.
Thus
k_{2}=0
and
m_{2}=0
, so
k_{1}+k_{2}+m_{1}+m_{2}\leq k-m-1
.

(b) Suppose all good leaves
v\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
belong to the connected component of

s
in
G^{\prime\prime}
. Again let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be all edges of this connected component,
and let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be all remaining edges of
G^{\prime}
.
The subgraph induced by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
has at least two good leaves in
\mathcal{V}_{T}
and also
contains
s
, so
k_{1}\leq k-m-2
and
m_{1}=0
. The subgraph induced by
\mathcal{E}_{2}

again consists of a single connected component which contains all vertices of

\mathcal{V}_{T}
, so
k_{2}=0
and
m_{2}=1
. Thus, we also have

k_{1}+k_{2}+m_{1}+m_{2}\leq k-m-1
.

This shows that (
5.12
) holds in all cases.
The desired bound (
5.9
) then follows from
(
5.12
), (
5.10
), and

|\mathcal{G}|=|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|
in Lemma

5.10
, completing the proof of
Lemma
5.9
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.17
, Assumption
3
.

Denote
\mathbf{a}_{l}=(a_{il})_{i=1}^{n}
.
We may expand by multilinearity of the mixed cumulant

\displaystyle\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\,\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}=1}^{\infty}\Big\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{a}_{l_{1}}\odot(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{l_{k}}\odot(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}}),\,\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}=1}^{\infty}\Big\langle(\mathbf{a}_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{a}_{l_{k}})\odot\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}=1}^{\infty}\Big\langle\kappa_{k}((X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{1}},\ldots,(X\mathbf{w})^{\odot l_{k}}),(\mathbf{a}_{l_{1}}\odot\mathbf{s}_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes(\mathbf{a}_{l_{m}}\odot\mathbf{s}_{m})\otimes((\mathbf{a}_{l_{m+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{a}_{l_{k}})\odot T)\Big\rangle.

Then Lemma
5.9
implies

\displaystyle\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\,\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}=1}^{\infty}C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|(\mathbf{a}_{l_{m+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{a}_{l_{k}})\odot T\|_{\mathcal{U}(l)}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\odot\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}.

In the definitions preceding (
5.8
), we have

\|\bar{X}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
,
\|K_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
, and

\|D_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|K_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\max_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\bar{X}[i,j]|^{k}\leq 1
for all
k\geq 2
. Then each
M\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
has
\|M\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
,
implying that also each
M\in\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{j\geq 0}\mathcal{M}_{j}
has
\|M\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
. Thus,
\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{U}(l)}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq 1
. Furthermore
I\in\mathcal{M}(l)
,
so
\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\in\mathcal{U}(l)
. Let us define

\mathbf{e}=(1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and set

\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)=\{(\mathbf{a}/\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty})\odot\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{l}\},\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{U}(l)\}.

Then also
\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)
,

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq 1
, and we have

\|(\mathbf{a}_{l_{m+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{a}_{l_{k}})\odot T\|_{\mathcal{U}(l)}\leq\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)}\prod_{i=k-m}^{k}\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}
.
Applying this and

\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\odot\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}
,

\displaystyle\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\,\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}=1}^{\infty}C_{l}(\mathbf{w})\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}\bigg)\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}.

Under Proposition
2.17
(a),
noting that
C_{l}(\mathbf{w})
,
\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{l}
,
|\mathcal{G}(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k})|
, and

\prod_{i=1}^{k}\|\mathbf{a}_{l_{i}}\|_{\infty}
are all bounded by a constant, this shows
for a constant
C\equiv C(k,D)>0
that

\displaystyle|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(kD)}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}.

Since also
|\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(kD)|\leq C(k,D)n
for a constant
C(k,D)>0
,
this shows Assumption
3
.

Under Proposition
2.17
(b), recalling the bound
(
5.7
), we have

\displaystyle|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\,\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|\leq\sum_{l=k}^{\infty}\underbrace{(Ck^{2})^{l}l^{(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)l}\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}}_{:=F(l)}.

Note that in the definitions preceding (
5.8
),
the number of distinct non-zero matrices in
\mathcal{M}(l)
is at most the number of
strings in the symbols
I,\bar{X},\bar{X}^{*},D_{2},K_{2},\odot,\times,(,)
of
length
Cl
, for some absolute constant
C>0
. Then for some constants

C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}>0
, we have
|\mathcal{M}(l)|\leq{C^{\prime}}^{l}
and
|\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)|\leq{C^{\prime\prime}}^{l}
.
For a sufficiently large constant
C_{0}>0
, let us set

L=\lfloor C_{0}k\log n+k^{C_{0}}\rfloor,\qquad\mathcal{U}\equiv\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(L).

Then
|\mathcal{U}|\leq n^{C}
for a constant
C\equiv C(C_{0},k)>0
.
For all
l>L
, recalling that
\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq 1
,
we may bound
\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)}\leq\|T\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{n}^{k-m}\|T\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{n}^{k}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}
. This gives

\displaystyle\sum_{l>L}F(l)\leq\sqrt{n}^{k}\cdot\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\times\sum_{l>L}(Ck^{2})^{l}l^{(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)l}.

Under the given condition
\beta>1/2
, choosing a large enough constant

C_{0}>0
that defines
L
ensures

\sum_{l>L}(Ck^{2})^{l}l^{(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)l}<\sqrt{n}^{-k}
, and thus

\sum_{l>L}F(l)<\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}.

For
l\leq L
, since
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, we may bound

\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(l)}\leq\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}
. Then for a constant
C(k,\beta)>0
,

\sum_{l=k}^{L}F(l)\leq\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}(Ck^{2})^{l}l^{(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)l}\leq C(k,\beta)\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}.

This implies

|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|\leq(C(k,\beta)+1)\sqrt{n}^{k-m-1}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}
,
which again shows Assumption
3
.
∎

5.4.
Random features tilt

To prove Proposition
2.18
, we use techniques developed in constructive field
theory. In particular, we follow a similar approach as outlined in
[
48
, Section
A]
. The main technical tool we rely on is the universal
Brydges-Kennedy-Abdesselam-Rivasseau forest formula
[
2
, Theorem
III.1]
, which we state here for the reader’s
convenience:

Lemma 5.12

(BKAR forest formula)
.

For some
l\geq 1
, let
f:[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

be a smooth function. Then

\displaystyle f(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{l(l-1)/2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{F}_{l}}\int_{[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}}\left.\frac{\partial^{|\mathcal{E}(G)|}}{\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(G)}\partial y_{ij}}f(y)\right|_{y=y(G,u)}\prod_{i<j}du_{ij}

where
\mathcal{F}_{l}
is the set of all forests (i.e. simple graphs
G
with no loops
and cycles) on
l
labeled vertices,
\mathcal{E}(G)
is the edge set of
G
,

u=(u_{ij})_{i<j}
, and
y(G,u)=(y_{pq}(G,u))_{p<q}\in\mathbb{R}^{l(l-1)/2}

has the coordinates

y_{pq}(G,u)=\min_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(G)\cap\{\text{unique path from }p\text{ to }q\}}u_{ij}

if
p,q
belong to the same connected component of
\mathcal{F}_{l}
,
or
y_{pq}(G,u)=0
otherwise.

Moreover, the symmetric matrix
Y(G,u)
with entries

Y(G,u)[p,q]=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $p=q$}\\
y_{pq}(G,u)&\text{if $p<q$}\\
y_{qp}(G,u)&\text{if $p>q$}\\
\end{cases}

is positive-semidefinite for all
0\leq u_{ij}\leq 1
.

We also need the following simple identity.

Lemma 5.13
.

Fix any
l\geq 1
, and let
Y=(y_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq l}\in\mathbb{R}^{l\times l}

be any positive-semidefinite matrix. For a smooth function
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{nl}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by
(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})\in\mathbb{R}^{nl}\mapsto\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})
, denote

\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\Phi=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}[\alpha]\partial\mathbf{w}_{j}[\alpha]}\Phi.

If
(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})\sim N(0,Y\otimes I_{n})
, then for any
i<j
,

\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{ij}}\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})]=\mathbb{E}[\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})].

Proof.

Since the covariance
Y\otimes I_{n}
can be singular, we shall prove this via
the Fourier transform. Let
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})\sim N(0,Y\otimes I_{n})
.
Its characteristic function, for any
\mathbf{t}=(\mathbf{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{t}_{l})\in\mathbb{R}^{nl}
,
is given by

\mu_{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{t})=\mathbb{E}\exp(i\langle\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}\rangle)=\exp\left({-}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{t}^{*}(Y\otimes I_{n})\mathbf{t}\right)=\exp\left({-}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{l}y_{ii}\|\mathbf{t}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{i<j}y_{ij}\mathbf{t}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{t}_{j}\right).

Thus,

\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{ij}}\mu_{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{t})=-(\mathbf{t}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{t}_{j})\mu_{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{t}).

Denote by

\mathcal{F}[\Phi](\mathbf{t})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Phi(\mathbf{w})e^{{-}i\langle\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}\rangle}d\mathbf{w},\quad\mathcal{F}[\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\Phi](t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\Phi(\mathbf{w})e^{{-}i\langle\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}\rangle}d\mathbf{w}

the Fourier transforms of
\Phi
and
\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\Phi
.
Then by standard properties of the Fourier transform, for any
i<j
,

\mathcal{F}[\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\Phi](\mathbf{t})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{F}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}[\alpha]\partial\mathbf{w}_{j}[\alpha]}\Phi\right](\mathbf{t})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}(-it_{i}[\alpha])(-it_{j}[\alpha])\mathcal{F}[\Phi](\mathbf{t})=-\mathbf{t}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{t}_{j}\mathcal{F}[\Phi](\mathbf{t}).

So by the Fourier inversion formula,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{ij}}\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mathbf{w})]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{ij}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{nl}}\int\mathcal{F}[\Phi](\mathbf{t})e^{i\langle\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}\rangle}d\mathbf{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{nl}}\int\mathcal{F}[\Phi](\mathbf{t})\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{ij}}\mu_{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{t})\;d\mathbf{t}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{nl}}\int(-\mathbf{t}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{t}_{j})\mathcal{F}[\Phi](\mathbf{t})\mu_{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{t})\;d\mathbf{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{nl}}\int\mathcal{F}[\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\Phi](\mathbf{t})\mu_{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{t})\;d\mathbf{t}=\mathbb{E}[\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\Phi(\mathbf{w})].

∎

For any
l\geq 1
, any tree
G
on
l
vertices, and any
u\in[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}
, let
Y(G,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{l\times l}
be defined as in Lemma

5.12
. We write
\mathbb{E}_{Y(G,u)}
for the expectation with
respect to
(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})\sim N(0,Y(G,u)\otimes I_{n})
. We will assume without loss of generality in Proposition
2.18
that
\sigma_{i}
is centered such that

(5.17)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim N(0,I_{n})}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{w})=0\text{ for all }i=1,\ldots,d

Using the above two lemmas, we now derive the following series expansion of the cumulant generating function.

Lemma 5.14
.

In the setting of Proposition
2.18
, for any
\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, denote

\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)=\langle\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}\rangle-\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{w}).

Let
\mathcal{T}_{l}
be the set of all trees on
l
labeled vertices. For each
G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
and
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let

F(G,\lambda,\mathbf{t})=\int_{[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}}\mathbb{E}_{Y(G,u)}\left[\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(G)}\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{t},\lambda)\right]\prod_{i<j}du_{ij}.

Then there exists a constant
c_{*}>0
such that if
\max(|\lambda|,\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2})<c_{*}
and

\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\frac{1}{l!}|F(G,\lambda,\mathbf{t})|<\infty
,
then

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim N(0,I_{n})}\exp\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\frac{1}{l!}\,F(G,\lambda,\mathbf{t}).

Proof.

To begin, we expand
\mathbb{E}\exp\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)
as

(5.18)

\mathbb{E}\exp\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\mathbb{E}\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)^{l}.

This exchange of summation and expectation
is justified by the Fubini-Tonelli theorem: Since
\mathbf{w}\sim N(0,I_{n})
, we have by
[
3
, eq. (2.4)]
that
under the conditions of Proposition
2.18
and the assumption (
5.17
), for
some constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}|\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)|^{l})^{1/l}

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{E}|\mathbf{t}^{*}\mathbf{w}-\lambda\mathbf{1}_{d}^{*}\sigma(X\mathbf{w})|^{l})^{1/l}\leq(\mathbb{E}|\mathbf{t}^{*}\mathbf{w}|^{l})^{1/l}+|\lambda|(\mathbb{E}|\mathbf{1}_{d}^{*}\sigma(X\mathbf{w})|^{l})^{1/l}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{l}(\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2}+|\lambda|(\mathbb{E}\|X^{*}\sigma^{\prime}(X\mathbf{w})\|_{2}^{l})^{1/l})

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\sqrt{l}(\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2}+|\lambda|)

For
\max(\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2},|\lambda|)<c_{*}
sufficiently small,
this implies

\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\mathbb{E}|\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)|^{l}<\infty
,
which justifies (
5.18
).

We may then write

\mathbb{E}\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)^{l}=\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{t},\lambda)

where
\mathbf{w}_{1}=\ldots=\mathbf{w}_{l}=\mathbf{w}
, i.e.
(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})\sim N(0,1_{l}1_{l}^{*}\otimes I_{n})
. Let
S_{l}\subset[0,1]^{l\times l}
be the space of
positive-semidefinite matrices such that
S_{l}[i,i]=1
for all
i\in[l]

and
S_{l}[i,j]\in[0,1]
for all
i\neq j
, and define the function

f:S_{l}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

f(Y)=\mathbb{E}_{Y}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{t},\lambda).

Identifying
S_{l}
with a subset of
[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}
given by its
upper-triangular entries, we have by Lemmas
5.12

and
5.13

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)^{l}=f(1_{l}1_{l}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{F}_{l}}\int_{[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}}\left.\frac{\partial^{|\mathcal{E}(G)|}}{\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(G)}\partial y_{ij}}f(Y)\right|_{Y=Y(G,u)}\prod_{i<j}du_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{F}_{l}}\int_{[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}}\mathbb{E}_{Y(G,u)}\left[\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(G)}\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{t},\lambda)\right]\prod_{i<j}du_{ij}.

Note that this is well-defined since the matrix
Y(G,u)
is
positive-semidefinite. If
i,j\in[l]
belong to two disconnected components of

G
, then by construction we have
Y(G,u)[i,j]=0
, so
\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{w}_{j}
are
independent. Thus,
\mathbb{E}_{Y(G,u)}
factorizes over the the connected components of

G
. By a standard combinatorial identity for exponential
generating functions (see e.g.
[
48
, eq.(109)]
and

[
60
, pg.3]
), if a function is a sum over forests of
contributions which factorize over its connected trees, then its logarithm is
the sum over trees of these individual factors. That is, as an identity of
formal series,

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}\exp\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)=\log\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\mathbb{E}\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)^{l}

(5.19)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\underbrace{\int_{[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}}\mathbb{E}_{Y(G,u)}\left[\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(G)}\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{t},\lambda)\right]\prod_{i<j}du_{ij}}_{=F(G,\lambda,\mathbf{t})}

where
\mathcal{T}_{l}
is the set of all trees on
l
labeled vertices. This holds as an
identity of analytic functions as long as the right side is absolutely
convergent, i.e.
\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\frac{1}{l!}\,|F(G,\lambda,\mathbf{t})|<\infty
.
∎

We now show that the series expansion established in Lemma
5.14
is indeed absolutely convergent, and represent its terms using
the tensor network definitions/notions developed in Section
5.3
.
Here, all dimensions of tensors and matrices in the network are
d
rather than

n
.

Lemma 5.15
.

In the setting of Proposition
2.18
,
let
\sigma^{(k)}(X\mathbf{w})=(\sigma_{i}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{w}))_{i=1}^{d}
for all
k\geq 1
.
Then there exists a constant
c_{*}>0
such that if
\max(|\lambda|,\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2})<c_{*}
, then

(5.20)

\log\mathbb{E}\exp\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}(G)}\int_{[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}}\mathbb{E}_{Y(G,u)}[\text{val}(G,f)]\prod_{i<j}du_{ij}.

Here
\mathcal{F}(G)
is a set of labelings of cardinality
|\mathcal{F}(G)|\leq 2^{l}
.
Denoting the vertices of
G
by
v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}
, for each
f\in\mathcal{F}(G)
,

(G,f)
is a tensor network satisfying:

(1)

If
v_{i}\in G
is such that
\text{deg}(v_{i})=1
(it is a leaf), then
f(v_{i})\in\{X\mathbf{t},-\lambda\sigma^{(1)}(X\mathbf{w}_{i})\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(2)

If
v_{i}\in G
is such that
\text{deg}(v_{i})\geq 2
, then

f(v_{i})=\operatorname{diag}({-}\lambda\sigma^{(\text{deg}(v_{i}))}(X\mathbf{w}_{i}))\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\text{deg}(v_{i})}
.

(3)

For all edges
e=(v_{i},v_{j})\in\mathcal{E}(G)
, if
f(v_{i})=X\mathbf{t}
or

f(v_{j})=X\mathbf{t}
, then
f(e)=I_{d}
, and otherwise
f(e)=XX^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

Moreover, for any
k\geq 1
and
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in[n]
,
the derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{1}]}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{k}]}\log\mathbb{E}\exp\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)
is given by differentiating the
series (
5.20
) term-by-term.

Proof.

In the series expansion developed in Lemma
5.14
,
we first show that for any
l\geq 1
and
G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
,

(5.21)

\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(G)}\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{t},\lambda)=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}(G)}\text{val}(G,f)

for a family of tensor networks
\{(G,f)\}_{f\in\mathcal{F}(G)}
satisfying the given
conditions.
We will then use this tensor network representation to check the condition of
absolute convergence in Lemma
5.14
.

Expanding
\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{t},\lambda)=\mathbf{t}^{*}\mathbf{w}_{i}-\lambda\mathbf{1}_{d}^{*}\sigma(X\mathbf{w})
, we have

(5.22)

\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(G)}\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{t},\lambda)=\sum_{S\subseteq[l]}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}(G)}\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\prod_{i\in S}(\mathbf{t}^{*}\mathbf{w}_{i})\prod_{j\in[l]\setminus S}(-\lambda\mathbf{1}_{d}^{*}\sigma(X\mathbf{w}_{j}))

If
S\subseteq[l]
contains a vertex
i
that is not a leaf of the tree
G
,
then the term corresponding to
S
in (
5.22
) is 0
because the differential
\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}}
is applied at least twice. For all
remaining subsets
S
, we may repeatedly apply the identities,
for any
k,m\geq 0
and
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\left(\mathbf{t}^{*}\mathbf{w}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{u}^{*}[{-}\lambda\sigma^{(k)}(X\mathbf{w}_{j})]\right)=(X\mathbf{t})^{*}\operatorname{diag}({-}\lambda\sigma^{(k+1)}(X\mathbf{w}_{j}))\mathbf{u},

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle\left(\mathbf{u}^{*}[{-}\lambda\sigma^{(k)}(X\mathbf{w}_{i})]\cdot\mathbf{v}^{*}[{-}\lambda\sigma^{(m)}(X\mathbf{w}_{j})]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt=\mathbf{u}^{*}\operatorname{diag}({-}\lambda\sigma^{(k+1)}(X\mathbf{w}_{i}))XX^{*}\operatorname{diag}({-}\lambda\sigma^{(m+1)}(X\mathbf{w}_{j}))\mathbf{v},

to evaluate
\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{i}},\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{j}}\rangle
for each edge of
G
.
Then writing
\operatorname{diag}({-}\lambda\sigma^{(1)}(X\mathbf{w}_{j}))\mathbf{1}_{d}={-}\lambda\sigma^{(1)}(X\mathbf{w}_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for the leaf vertices of
G
belonging
to
[l]\setminus S
, we see that the summand
for
S
in (
5.22
) takes the form
\text{val}(G,f)

for some labeling
f
satisfying the given properties. Letting
\mathcal{F}(G)
denote the set of
labelings corresponding to all such non-zero summands, we then have

|\mathcal{F}(G)|\leq 2^{l}
. This shows (
5.21
).

We now check absolute convergence by bounding
\text{val}(G,f)
:
We may identify one leaf vertex of
G
as a source
s
and merge
all remaining leaf vertices
u_{1},\ldots,u_{p}\in G
into a sink
t
with label

f(t)=f(u_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes f(u_{p})
. This does not change
\text{val}(G,f)
,
and the resulting network clearly admits a
(s,t)
-bipolar orientation since
G

is a tree. Then, further bounding the operator norm of a diagonal matrix by the
Euclidean norm of its vector of diagonal entries,

|\text{val}(G,f)|\leq\prod_{i=1}^{l}\|f(v_{i})\|_{2}\times\max\{\|X^{*}X\|_{\mathrm{op}},1\}^{|\mathcal{E}(G)|}

Here, by the conditions of Proposition
2.18
,

\|f(v_{i})\|_{2}\leq C\max(\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2},|\lambda|C^{\text{deg}(v_{i})})
for a constant
C>0
.
Since
G
is a tree on
l
vertices, it has
l-1
edges, and
\sum_{i=1}^{l}\text{deg}(v_{i})=2|\mathcal{E}(G)|=2(l-1)
. Then this implies

|\text{val}(G,f)|\leq{C^{\prime}}^{l}\max(\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2},\lambda)^{l}
(with probability 1 over

(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})
), so also

|F(G,\lambda,\mathbf{t})|\leq\int_{[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}}\mathbb{E}_{Y(G,u)}\left[\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}(G)}|\text{val}(G,f)|\right]\prod_{i<j}du_{ij}\leq(C^{\prime\prime})^{l}\max(\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2},\lambda)^{l}

for a constant
C^{\prime\prime}>0
. Using Cayley’s formula
|\mathcal{T}_{l}|=l^{l-2}

and
l!\geq(l/e)^{l}
, we obtain for a constant
C>0
that

\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\frac{1}{l!}|F(G,\lambda,\mathbf{t})|\leq\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l^{2}}\,C^{l}\max(\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2},\lambda)^{l}.

This is finite for
\max(\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2},|\lambda|)<c_{*}
sufficiently small,
so Lemma
5.14
applies to show (
5.20
).

All preceding arguments apply equally with
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
in place of

\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, so for each fixed
\lambda
with
|\lambda|<c_{*}
, the Weierstrass M-test implies
\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\frac{1}{l!}F(G,\lambda,\mathbf{z})
is
uniformly convergent to an analytic limit
F(\mathbf{z})
over the disk

\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:\|\mathbf{z}\|_{2}<c_{*}\}
. This implies the uniform convergence of the
partial derivatives in
\mathbf{z}
, hence also

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{1}]}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{k}]}\log\mathbb{E}\exp\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\frac{1}{l!}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{1}]}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{k}]}F(G,\lambda,\mathbf{t}).

∎

We now show that the quantity to be bounded in Assumption
3

admits a series expansion in terms of expected tensor network values.

Lemma 5.16
.

In the setting of Proposition
2.18
,
there exist a constant
\lambda_{*}>0
such that for all
|\lambda|<\lambda_{*}
the
following holds: For any
k\geq 3
,
1\leq m\leq k-1
, any

\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and any
T\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes k-m}
, we have the convergent series expansion

\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle=\sum_{l=k+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\sum_{(H,f)\in\mathcal{G}(G)}\int_{[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}}\mathbb{E}_{Y(G,u)}[\text{val}(H,f)]\prod_{i<j}du_{ij}

where
\mathcal{G}(G)
is a family of tensor networks satisfying
|\mathcal{G}(G)|\leq 2^{l}k!
,
and for all
(H,f)\in\mathcal{G}(G)
, the following holds:

(1)

H=(\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{m},t\}\cup\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})
is a connected, simple graph,
and the subgraph induced by the vertices
\mathcal{A}
is a tree on
l-k
vertices.

(2)

For all
1\leq i\leq m
, we have
\text{deg}(s_{i})=1
and
f(s_{i})=X\mathbf{s}_{i}
.
Similarly,
\text{deg}(t)=k-m
and
f(t)=X^{\otimes k-m}\cdot T
(the contraction of

T
with
X
along each axis). Moreover, the neighbors of
s_{1},\ldots,s_{m},t
are
all in
\mathcal{A}
, and the edges connecting them to
\mathcal{A}
all have label
f(e)=I_{d}
.

(3)

Each vertex
v\in\mathcal{A}
has label

f(v)=\operatorname{diag}({-}\lambda\sigma^{(\text{deg}(v))}(X\mathbf{w}_{i_{v}}))\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\text{deg}(v)}

for some
i_{v}\in[l]
, and each edge
e
between two vertices of
\mathcal{A}
has
label
f(e)=XX^{*}
.

Proof.

Note that
\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{g})=\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})
since
\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w}
and
k\geq 3
.
We begin with an entrywise understanding of
\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w})
. For any

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in[n]
, Lemma
5.15

implies we can differentiate term-by-term to get

\displaystyle\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],...,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{1}]}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{k}]}\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim N(0,I_{n})}\exp\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{t},\lambda)\right|_{\mathbf{t}=0}

(5.23)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}(G)}\int_{[0,1]^{l(l-1)/2}}\mathbb{E}_{Y(G,\mathbf{u})}\left[\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{1}]}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{k}]}\text{val}(G,f)\right|_{\mathbf{t}=0}\right]\prod_{i<j}du_{ij}

The above summand corresponding to
G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}(G)
is
0 if the number of
leaves of
G
having label
X\mathbf{t}
is less than
k
. Since the minimum number of
vertices to form a tree with
k
leaves is
k+1
, the above sum must start with

l=k+1
. Moreover, since we evaluate the sum at
\mathbf{t}=0
, the non-zero summands
must correspond to
(G,f)
where exactly
k
leaves of
G
are labeled as
X\mathbf{t}
.
For any such
(G,f)
, observe that

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{1}]}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{k}]}\text{val}(G,f)
is the sum over
k!
tensor network values that replace the
k

labels of
X\mathbf{t}
in
(G,f)
by the labels

X\mathbf{e}_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,X\mathbf{e}_{\alpha_{k}}
in some order. Then

\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}(G)}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{1}]}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{t}[\alpha_{k}]}\text{val}(G,f)\mathbf{s}_{1}[\alpha_{1}]\cdots\mathbf{s}_{m}[\alpha_{m}]T[\alpha_{m+1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}]=\sum_{(H,f)\in\mathcal{G}(G)}\text{val}(H,f)

where
\mathcal{G}(G)
is the set of at most
2^{l}k!

tensor networks that take some labeling
f\in\mathcal{F}(G)
, replace the labels of
some choice of
m
vertices having label
X\mathbf{t}
by

X\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,X\mathbf{s}_{m}
, and merge the remaining
k-m
vertices
having label
X\mathbf{t}
into a single vertex with (incident edges in some
order and) label
X^{\otimes k-m}\cdot T
. This network
(H,f)
satisfies all
properties of the lemma, by the properties established
for
G
in Lemma
5.15
. Since

\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle=\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{1}],\ldots,\mathbf{w}[\alpha_{k}])\mathbf{s}_{1}[\alpha_{1}]\cdots\mathbf{s}_{m}[\alpha_{k}]T[\alpha_{m+1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}],

the lemma follows from applying this representation to
(
5.23
).
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
2.18
.

Proof of Proposition
2.18
.

To check Assumption
1
for
\Sigma
and Assumption

2
for
\mathbf{g}
,
note that the Hessian of the potential satisfies

\nabla^{2}U(\mathbf{w})=\frac{1}{2}I_{n}+\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sigma_{i}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{w})\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}^{*}=\frac{1}{2}I_{n}+\lambda X^{*}\operatorname{diag}(\sigma^{\prime\prime}(X\mathbf{w}))X.

Then for
|\lambda|<\lambda_{*}
sufficiently small,

(1/4)I_{n}\preceq\nabla^{2}U(\mathbf{w})\preceq(3/4)I_{n}
. Therefore the distribution of

\mathbf{g}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}
is strongly log-concave, so Assumption
2

follows from the Bakry-Emery criterion and a standard Lipschitz extension
argument. Furthermore by the Brascamp-Lieb inequality,

\Sigma=\mathbb{E}\mathbf{g}\mathbf{g}^{*}\preceq 4I_{n}.

To prove a lower bound on
\Sigma
, consider the random vector

\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w},\nabla U(\mathbf{w}))\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. We have

\mathbb{E}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{z}^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma&\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w})\nabla U(\mathbf{w})^{*}\\
\mathbb{E}\nabla U(\mathbf{w})(\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w})^{*}&\mathbb{E}\nabla U(\mathbf{w})\nabla U(\mathbf{w})^{*}\end{bmatrix}\succeq 0.

We deduce, from integration by parts

\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w})\nabla U(\mathbf{w})^{*}=\mathbb{E}\nabla U(\mathbf{w})(\mathbf{w}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{w})^{*}=I_{n},\quad\mathbb{E}\nabla U(\mathbf{w})\nabla U(\mathbf{w})^{*}=\mathbb{E}\nabla^{2}U(\mathbf{w}).

Since both
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{z}^{*}
and
\mathbb{E}\nabla U(\mathbf{w})\nabla U(\mathbf{w})^{*}=\mathbb{E}\nabla^{2}U(\mathbf{w})
are
positive-semidefinite, Schur’s complement implies

\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}-I_{n}[\mathbb{E}\nabla^{2}U(\mathbf{w})]^{-1}I_{n}\succeq 0
, hence

\Sigma\succeq(4/3)I_{n}.

This implies the conditions for
\Sigma
in Assumption
1
.

Finally we check Assumption
3
. It suffices to bound

\text{val}(H,f)
in Lemma
5.16
, and we will do so deterministically over
(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{l})
. For any
l\geq k+1
, any
G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
, and any
(H,f)\in\mathcal{G}(G)
, let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k-m}\in\mathcal{A}

be the vertices adjacent to
t
in
H
.
We modify
(H,f)
as follows: For each such
vertex
v_{i}
adjacent to
t
, add a degree-1 vertex
v_{i}^{\prime}
that connects to

v_{i}
by an edge with label
I_{d}
, label
v_{i}^{\prime}
by the label

f(v_{i})=\operatorname{diag}({-}\lambda\sigma^{(\text{deg}(v_{i}))}(X\mathbf{w}_{j_{i}}))
of
v_{i}
, and
re-label
f(v_{i})
by the identity tensor. This does not change
\text{val}(H,f)
.
Now, letting
K\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes p}
be the tensor representing the contraction
of internal edges of

H\setminus\{t,v_{1}^{\prime},...,v_{k-m}^{\prime}\}
, we apply Cauchy-Schwartz to bound

\displaystyle|\text{val}(H,f)|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-m}=1}^{d}K[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-m}]f(v_{1}^{\prime})[\alpha_{1}]\cdots f(v_{k-m}^{\prime})[\alpha_{k-m}]f(t)[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-m}]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|K\|_{F}\|f(v_{1}^{\prime})\|_{2}\cdots\|f(v_{k-m}^{\prime})\|_{2}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}

Here, since
f(t)=X^{\otimes k-m}\cdot T
, we have

\|f(t)\|_{\infty}\leq\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}

by definition of
\mathcal{U}=\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\}\cup\{\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}\}
.
To bound
\|K\|_{F}
, we have by duality

\|K\|_{F}=\sup_{\|U\|_{F}=1}\langle U,K\rangle=\text{val}(H^{\prime},f)

where
(H^{\prime},f)
is formed by removing
t,v_{1}^{\prime},...,v_{k-m}^{\prime}
from
H
, adding a
new vertex
t^{\prime}
with label
f(t^{\prime})=U
, and adding
k-m
edges between
t^{\prime}
and

v_{1},\ldots,v_{k-m}
with labels
I_{d}
. Note that these vertices

v_{1},\ldots,v_{k-m}
are leaves of the tree
H^{\prime}\setminus\{t^{\prime}\}
.
Let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{p}
be the remaining
leaves of
H^{\prime}\setminus\{t^{\prime}\}
, which include the leaves
s_{1},\ldots,s_{m}
of
H
together with any leaves in
\mathcal{A}
. We set
s=u_{1}
as a source, and
further merge the leaves
u_{2},\ldots,u_{p}
with
t^{\prime}
into a sink
t^{\prime\prime}

having label

f(t^{\prime\prime})=U\otimes f(u_{2})\otimes\cdots\otimes f(u_{p})
. This does not change

\text{val}(H^{\prime},f)
, and the resulting network admits a
(s,t^{\prime\prime})
-bipolar orientation
because
H^{\prime}\setminus\{t^{\prime}\}
is a tree. Then applying
Lemma
5.4
and combining with the above,

\displaystyle|\text{val}(H,f)|

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|X\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{A}}\|{-}\lambda\sigma^{(\text{deg}(v))}(X\mathbf{w}_{i_{v}})\|_{2}\times\|XX^{*}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{|\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{A})|}\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{m+2(l-k-1)}|\lambda|^{l-k}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\right)\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}

where we applied that
\mathcal{A}
is a tree on
l-k
vertices so

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{A}}\text{deg}(v)=2|\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{A})|=2(l-k-1)
. Substituting this back into the
series expansion of Lemma
5.16
, and applying

|\mathcal{G}(G)|\leq 2^{l}k!
, a
similar counting argument as in the proof of Lemma
5.15
gives

\displaystyle|\langle\kappa_{k}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{s}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{s}_{m}\otimes T\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=k+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\sum_{(H,f)\in\mathcal{G}(G)}C^{m+2(l-k-1)}|\lambda|^{l-k}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\right)\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle\leq k!C^{m}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2}\right)\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}\sum_{l=k+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l^{2}}{C^{\prime}}^{l}|\lambda|^{l-k}.

For
|\lambda|<\lambda_{*}
sufficiently small, this is at most

C_{k}(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|\mathbf{s}_{i}\|_{2})\|T\|_{\mathcal{U}}
for a constant
C_{k}>0
,
completing the proof.
∎
